How to Use This Guide
Throughout this guide, every teaching activity is marked with a coloured box and an icon, so you can
see at a glance what you are expected to do. Plain text is content to be taught; the boxes are where
you act.
FACILITATOR HINT What you do, say and prepare as facilitator — instructions,
scripts, prompts and notes.
EXERCISE / PRACTICE Hands-on work: role play, demonstration, individual and
supervised practice, video sessions.
GROUP DISCUSSION Questions you put to the whole group or to small groups, and
the Q&A guides.
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION Clinical cases and scenarios to be read out and
worked through by participants.
ANSWER KEY The expected answers — for the facilitator, after the group has tried.
KEY MESSAGE The one sentence participants must leave the session with.
Timing shown in a box heading is the time allocated to that activity. Answer keys are for you only
— do not read them out before the group has worked the case.

Introduction & Training Framework
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
5 | Page
1. Introduction
This Facilitator Guide has been developed to support the effective implementation of the Sudan
Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns (MSSN) – 2026.
It provides practical, step-by-step guidance to facilitators on:
● Planning and organizing the training
● Delivering high-quality, competency-based sessions
● Facilitating skills practice and clinical learning
● Assessing participant competence
● Adapting training to real hospital constraints in Sudan
This guide is intended to be used together with:
● The Sudan Training Manual for Management of Sick and Small Newborn – 2026
● Participant modules and job aids
● Skills checklists and assessment tools
1.1 National Context and Alignment
Care of sick and small newborns is a strategic priority under the Sudan Newborn Action Plan (2022–
2030)
Neonatal mortality remains a major public health challenge in Sudan. Most preventable neonatal
deaths are due to:
● Birth asphyxia
● Prematurity and its complications
● Neonatal sepsis
● Delayed recognition and referral of sick newborns
This training course and facilitator guide are designed to:
● Strengthen hospital-based newborn care
● Improve early recognition and timely management
● Standardize care across facilities
● Build practical clinical competence among doctors, nurses, and midwives
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
6 | Page
1.2 Scope of the Training
This course covers newborn care from:
● Birth and the Golden Minute
● Emergency and essential newborn care
● Special care (SCBU / NICU level)
● Ventilation and advanced care
● Stabilization, referral, and discharge
This is a hospital-based course only.
Training assumes access to:
● Delivery rooms
● Newborn units (SCBU / NICU)
● Skills labs or simulation space
1.3 TARGET PARTICIPANTS
Primary Participants
● Medical Officers (General Practitioners)
● Pediatric residents and pediatricians
● NICU / SCBU Nurses
Prerequisites
Participants should have:
● Basic training in maternal and newborn care
● Current clinical role in hospital newborn services
● Commitment to full course attendance.
1.4 COURSE CERTIFICATION
● Certification authority: Federal Ministry of Health – Sudan
● Certification is awarded only after:
o Completion of required sessions
o Passing knowledge assessments
o Demonstrated clinical and skills competence
● No state certification is permitted
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

1.5 Training approach
Educational Principles
This course is based on:
● Adult learning principles
● Competency-based training
● Learning by doing
● Case-based and skills-focused instruction
Emphasis
● Skills over theory
● Decision-making over memorization
● Patient safety over speed
● Team communication
1.6 Participant & facilitator numbers
Participants
● Ideal: 16–24
● Maximum: 24
Facilitators
● Ratio: 1 facilitator: 4–6 participants
● Minimum facilitators for 24 participants: 4 facilitators + 1 course director

● Identify facilitators

item done

1.9 Who is the Facilitator
A facilitator is not a lecturer.
A facilitator:
● Guides learning
● Coaches skills
● Observes practice
● Provides immediate feedback
● Protects patient safety during training
Facilitators spend most of their time:
● In small groups
● At skills stations
● In clinical areas
● Giving individual feedback
Core Responsibilities of Facilitators
Instruct
● Explain how to use modules and job aids
● Demonstrate clinical skills
● Clarify difficult concepts
● Guide case discussions and scenarios
● Supervise bedside practice
Motivate
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
9 | Page
● Encourage participation
● Reinforce correct practice
● Create a safe learning environment
Manage
● Monitor participant progress
● Ensure smooth movement between sessions
● Maintain time discipline
● Ensure required materials are available
What Facilitators Should NOT Do
● Deliver long lectures
● Read slides or manuals word-for-word
● Humiliate or embarrass participants
● Allow unsafe clinical practice
● Rush skills training without supervision
1.10 Course Competencies
Module (1) Essential and Emergency Newborn Care competency
This module addresses the leading immediate causes of neonatal mortality in Sudan, particularly:
● Birth asphyxia
● Hypothermia
● Hypoglycemia
● Delayed or inadequate resuscitation
● Poor early assessment and stabilization and referaal
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
By the end of this module, participants should be able to:
Knowledge
● Explain the concept and importance of the Golden Minute
● Identify newborns requiring emergency intervention
● Describe immediate newborn care and resuscitation steps
Skills
● Perform rapid assessment at birth

● Initiate basic neonatal resuscitation
● Prevent and manage hypothermia and hypoglycemia
● Stabilize newborns before referral
Attitudes
● Demonstrate urgency and teamwork at birth
● Prioritize patient safety
● Communicate effectively with mothers and team members

MODULE 2 SPECIAL NEWBORN CARE competencies
This module addresses:
● Ongoing care after stabilization
● Prevention of deterioration
● Care of preterm and low birth weight babies
● Safe admission, monitoring, and discharge
Most newborn deaths in hospitals occur after admission, due to:
● Poor monitoring
● Delayed response
● Infection
● Feeding and temperature problems

1.11 Sudan Neonatal Health System
Teaching Method
● Interactive lecture
● Data interpretation discussion
● National policy reflection
Facilitator Instruction
Begin by asking participants:
● “When is the highest risk period for newborn mortality?”
● “What are the major causes of neonatal deaths in Sudan?”
Allow responses before presenting the manual content.
Present the Introduction Exactly as Written
Facilitator Discussion
After presenting the data, ask:
● What does 24.8 per 1,000 live births mean in your facility context?
● Which causes listed are most common in your hospital?
● Which of these are preventable through improved quality of care?
Ensure discussion remains linked to manual-stated causes.
Key Facilitator Emphasis
● First week of life = highest risk period
● First 24 hours = critical window
● Majority of deaths are preventable
● National commitment through SuNAP
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
15 | Page
Facilitator Activity (15 minutes)
Ask participants to:
● Identify which of these services are currently available in their SCNU.
● Identify gaps in equipment or training.
● Keep discussion aligned to listed services only
Facilitator concludes by reinforcing:
Correct classification of level of care ensures:
● Appropriate management
● Timely referral
● Reduced mortality
Closing Summary (10 minutes)
Facilitator summarizes:
● Neonatal mortality burden nationally and globally
● National targets under SuNAP
● Importance of organized health system structure
● Clear distinction between Level 1, 2, and 3 services
Practical Exercise: Level Mapping (15 minutes)
Divide participants into groups. Each group:
1. Draws a simple diagram of referral pathway from Level 1 → Level 2 → Level 3.
2. Identifies where delays commonly occur.

2. Early Essential Newborn Care
2.1 Early essential newborn Care (Birth Up to 90 minutes of age)
Interactive session: the golden minute of life
By the end of this section, participants should be able to:
● Define the Golden Minute
● Explain why it is critical for neonatal survival
● Demonstrate readiness for immediate newborn care at birth
Teaching Method: short presentation +Interactive discussion
Facilitator Instructions
Opening Discussion
Ask participants:
● “What happens in the first minute after birth?”
● “Why do some babies fail to breathe at birth?”
Allow brief responses. Do not correct immediately.
Key Teaching Points
Emphasize:
● The Golden Minute starts when the baby is fully delivered
● It is the critical window to establish breathing
● Do NOT wait for APGAR scoring to act
● Delayed action leads to:
o Brain injury
o Long-term disability
o Death
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
18 | Page
Antenatal Counseling (Discussion) Ask:
● “Why should parents be counseled before delivery?”
● “Who should do counseling?”
Highlight:
● Counseling reduces anxiety
● Supports informed decisions
● Is essential for preterm or high-risk births
● Should involve obstetric + neonatal teams when possible
Communication with the Labour Team and Introduce SBAR communication.
Demonstrate:
● Situation
● Background
● Assessment
● Recommendation
Equipment & Environment Preparation
● Resuscitation area
● Essential equipment
Demonstrate:
● Quick equipment check
● Assigning a team member to check equipment
● Importance of warmth and timing
Ask participants:
● “What equipment is essential even in low-resource settings?”
Common Errors
● Equipment not checked
● No clear team leader
● Poor role clarity
Common Mistakes to Address
● Waiting for senior staff before starting care
● Focusing on APGAR instead of breathing
● Poor preparation before delivery
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
19 | Page
Role-play (1): delivery of a breathing newborn without coaching
This role play includes scenario of a health-care provider doing their usual practice (to be done in
a large group); the facilitators use the Skills checklist to assess every step of the action by the
demonstrator including the exact time (to the second) where relevant. This will be used as a
baseline performance score of the demonstration.
● Ask for three participants, one participant each to play: (1) the woman delivering the
baby; (2) the attending health worker; and (3) the recorder.
● Ask the “mother” to wear the Mamanatalie™ and sit on the delivery bed. A facilitator
will do the set-up of the “baby” to appear to be in the mother’s womb.
● Explain to the attending health worker: “You will deliver a baby and provide new-born
care. The woman is fully dilated. You have already weighed her, taken her blood
pressure, and listened for fetal heart tones. There is no more time, she will deliver in
about a minute. Please do exactly as you would during and immediately following the
delivery from the first step immediately before delivery until the last step in the
immediate postpartum care period. Please do not explain what you are doing. However,
please do request the mother or other health workers to perform actions as needed.” Ask if
there is anything that needs further clarification
● Explain to the note taker: “You will write down the exact actions/interventions you
observe before, during and immediately after delivery. Please record the time of each
activity: to the second for events occurring within three minutes of delivery, and to the
minute for events occurring thereafter.”
● Ask the other participants to pay close attention to what the attendant is doing, and take
key notes, as they will be asked to comment later. During the demonstration, they should
not tell the demonstrator what to do or how to do it.
● Ask the attendant to start the role-play. When the baby is “born”, a facilitator will make
the baby’s crying sound and make the cord pulsate. The facilitator should record – and
time – exactly what is happening: to the second for events occurring within the first three
minutes after delivery, and to the minute, at other times.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
20 | Page
Notes
● Most participants doing the role-play will describe what they do in their practice instead
of actually doing it. In this case, ask them to do what they are describing.
● Many participants will not do exactly what they do in their practice during deli-very (e.g.
wash hands, put on gloves and gowns, and organize delivery space). You can say, “Most
people… [Wear gloves, for instance]. Would you like to start over and do it exactly as
you would normally do where you work?”
Discussion and simulation based on current practices and sequence of events
FACILITATOR HINT
Facilitators generate participatory agreement or disagreement with every stage of practice and
encourage discussion on the evidence base for the correct practice (done in a large group).
A. Give the attendant a chance to discuss her/his thoughts on the delivery, then ask the
participants to give their thoughts. If there are interesting points raised that will be covered
during re-enactment, take note of them and state: “We will come back to this point shortly.”
B. Ask the note taker to read aloud his record of only the first action that the attendant(s) had
performed. Ask the attendant to do the action again; ask the participants if this is correct or
needs improvement. Facilitate a discussion around the: (1) order of actions; (2) quality of the
action; and (3) the evidence base for the recommendation (see Facilitator’s Notes on page
15).
C. At the appropriate time during step b, have each of the participants demonstrate their
proficiency in the following areas:
■ setting up the newborn resuscitation area,
■ checking bag and mask for functionality,
■ immediate, thorough and sequential drying,
■ checking cord pulsations, and
■ Measuring distance for cord clamping.
D. Repeat steps b and c until all actions have been practiced.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
21 | Page
Role-play: delivery of a breathing newborn with coaching and incremental correction of gaps
and inappropriate practices
General Guide
■ This role play includes scenario of a health-care provider doing their Standard practice of
delivering breathing baby
■ Repeat role-play several times until at least a few get it perfect.
Individual practice: Supervised practice of delivery of a normal breathing baby
■ Done in a small group.
■ Break into groups according to the number of delivery beds/practice stations.
■ Each group should have one facilitator.
■ Each participant should repeat practice until all demonstrate it perfectly.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
22 | Page
Group discussion (early essential newborn care)
let us think together about why newborns die, and what we can actually do about it in the first
minutes of life.
I will ask you a series of questions. There is no test here. Most of what we need is already in this
room, in what you do every day — so please answer from your own practice, not from a
textbook.
Let each other finish. I will not correct answers immediately; we will build the picture together
and reach the right answer as a group.
By the end you should see how a small number of simple actions — keeping the baby warm,
drying well, helping the baby breathe, touching less — prevent most newborn deaths."
● What are the major causes of newborn death?
o Asphyxia, sepsis and prematurity.
● What are underlying causes of death?
o Not breathing, hypothermia, not breastfeeding.
● Why is hypothermia dangerous to newborns?
o Hypothermia can cause newborns to have infection, coagulation defects, acidosis,
delayed fetal-to-newborn circulatory adjustment, hyaline membrane disease
(respiratory distress syndrome) and brain haemorrhage. It is dangerous.
● What are major causes of hypothermia?
o Cold room with draft (convection), being wet (evaporation), touching cold surfaces
(conduction), being exposed (radiation).
● What actions can health workers perform to prevent hypothermia?
o Ensure room temperature of 25–28 °C. Close all windows, turn off fan and air-
conditioning to ensure there is no draught (i.e. airflow) in the room. Dry baby
thoroughly and then cover; put in skin-to-skin contact with the mother. For the rare
resuscitation that would be needed, turn on the radiant warmer (if available) before
delivery or cover the resuscitation area surface with a clot
● Can we predict which babies will not be breathing?
o Even babies born of mothers with no risk factors may not breathe.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
23 | Page
● Is it important to prepare the newborn resuscitation area?
o Even though rare, we cannot predict which babies will not breathe at birth. Thus, we
need the newborn resuscitation area set up for all deliveries.
● How do we prepare the delivery space?
o Ensure that all delivery equipment and supplies are available, clean, and ready for
use. Lay out in sequence: gloves*, gauze, povidone-iodine, 2 cloths, oxytocin, needle
and syringe, 2 cord clamps** and sterile scissors.
o Aside from cloth, ventilation bag and mask, ensure that suction device, hat and
clock/timer are in easy reach/view of the newborn resuscitation area. All equipment
should be tested before the delivery.
o If only one provider handles the mother’s and the newborn’s cord sections, 2 pairs of
gloves should be worn. The outer pair should be removed prior to handling the cord.
● Why is drying important?
o It stimulates breathing. Most babies who are not breathing at birth will start to breathe
after thorough drying.
o It prevents hypothermia.
● How do we dry well?
o Before delivery, place a clean cloth over the mother’s bare abdomen. Deliver the baby
and place on the clean cloth. Drying should be immediate (within a few seconds of
delivery), thorough and systematic. Wipe the eyes, mouth/nose, face, head, front,
back, arms and legs. Thorough drying takes around 30 seconds. It should be by
rubbing, not patting, but should not remove the vernix. Assess the baby for breathing
during drying.
● Why is skin-to-skin contact good?
o It keeps a baby warm, promotes bonding, contributes to overall success of
breastfeeding/- colostrum feeding, stimulates the immune system (mucosa-associated
lymphoid tissue), protects from hypoglycaemia, and helps colonization with maternal
skin flora (friendly family bacteria).
● How do we maintain direct skin-to-skin contact?
o You have just finished drying the baby. Remove the wet cloth so that the baby is in
direct skin-to-skin contact with the mother’s bare abdomen. Cover, but do not wrap,
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
24 | Page
the baby with another clean dry cloth. This keeps the skin of the baby’s abdomen
directly in contact with the skin of the mother’s abdomen. Cover the newborn’s head
with a hat.
● Why should we delay cord clamping until after the cord stops pulsating?
o Delaying cord-clamping protects against anaemia, intraventricular haemorrhage and
necrotizing enterocolitis.
● How do we know the cord is pulsating?
o Demonstrate with the manikin. Ask every participant to look and to feel the cord.
● Why should we stop our usual practice of clamping the cord far from the umbilical base for later
trimming?
o Unnecessary handling of the cord gives opportunity for bacteria to get into the cord.
o Cutting at 2 cm (between the clamps/ties) eliminates the need to “trim” the cord later.
● Should we apply disinfectant to the cord?
o No. If allowed to air dry, the cord will dry more quickly. Remind participants that
they need to tell caretakers: “Do not use a binder or apply any substance to the cord.”
● Why should we call out the time of delivery precise to the second?
o Actions in the first minute especially need timing to the second. Thorough drying
takes around 30 seconds. A baby not breathing after 30 seconds needs to be helped to
breathe/ or have lungs aerated within 60 seconds. The cord should be clamped after
cord stops pulsating at 1–3 minutes.
● Why is unnecessary handling bad?
● Each time a health worker handles a baby, the baby is at risk for infection and cold.
● When should a health worker handle the baby?
o Only when there is a medical need.
● When should a health worker perform hand hygiene (handwashing or alcohol hand rub)?
o Immediately before and immediately after handling a baby/mother or their
surroundings, after exposure to body fluids, and before and after an aseptic procedure.
● When should a baby be suctioned?
o Only when the airway is blocked and baby is completely not breathing, for example, a
non-vigorous, meconium-stained baby.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
25 | Page
● Does a baby with thick meconium staining but who is breathing well need suctioning?
o No. The baby is breathing well and suctioning does not prevent meconium aspiration.
● Why is suctioning a baby dangerous? (Ask for as many answers as possible.)
o Hold the bulb up. Pretend to suction a participant, then move to the next and ask if it
is OK to suction them with this. Ask why. Do the same for five participants. Bulbs are
dirty even when cleaning has been attempted. You cannot get rid of all the bacteria.
Suction bulbs spread infection. Ask if they would feel comfortable if you suctioned
them. Ask why. Gagging a baby is dangerous. It can cause the baby to stop breathing
and get sick. It can cause trauma to the mouth and nose.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
26 | Page
2.2 Support breast feeding in the first hour of life
Facilitator has following objectives to achieve by end of this session:
The participant after completing this module should be able to:
1. Enumerate advantages of breastfeeding.
2. Understand and help mother(s) with the correct technique of breastfeeding.
3. Understand the physiology of lactation.
4. Identify factors enhancing lactation.
5. Describe difficulties associated with breast feeding and their solutions.
6. Learn skills related to expression of breast milk.
Demonstration Using the MamaBreast Simulator
MamaBreast is a wearable simulator designed to provide a highly realistic simulation of
breastfeeding. It specifically supports training in proper breastfeeding positioning and
attachment, making it ideal for this demonstration. To prepare and adjust the MamaBreast
simulator for the volunteer, follow these steps:
● Open the cap on the MamaBreast and pour in water until the bag is partially full.
● Massage the bag from the outside with both hands to separate the foils, which makes
filling the bag easier.
● Pour a total of 500 ml of water into each bag.
● Avoid using actual milk in the simulator, as this will damage the product.
● Place the MamaBreast simulator on the volunteer's body.
● Adjust the waist strap to ensure a comfortable fit around the side of the body.
● Adjust the overall height of the simulator by pulling the strap located on the neck.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
27 | Page
Step 1: Simulator Preparation
● Fill both bags of the MamaBreast simulator with exactly 500 ml of water each, strictly
avoiding the use of real milk to prevent damage to the equipment.
● Assist the volunteer in putting on the MamaBreast simulator.
● Adjust the waist strap to ensure a secure, comfortable fit around the side, and adjust the neck
strap so the breasts sit at a natural height.
Step 2: Demonstrating Good Positioning
● Hand the newborn doll to the volunteer and instruct them to hold the baby close to their body.
● Demonstrate that the neonate's head and body must be aligned in a straight line, ensuring the
neck is neither twisted nor bent.
● Show participants how to support the baby's entire body rather than just the head and neck,
which is particularly vital when handling neonates under 28 days old.
● Position the baby so they are facing the breast, with their nose directly opposite the nipple, to
encourage the rooting reflex.
Step 3: Demonstrating Good Attachment
● Verbally outline the four key clinical signs of a proper latch while the volunteer acts out the
motion with the doll.
● Chin: The baby's chin should firmly touch the breast.
● Mouth: The baby's mouth must be open wide.
● Lip: The baby's lower lip should be curled or turned outward.
● Areola: More of the mother's areola should be visible above the baby's upper lip than below
the lower lip.
Instruct the volunteer to bring the baby to the breast (not the breast to the baby) to simulate achieving
this attachment on the MamaBreast
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
28 | Page
Group Discussion:(support breast feeding)
"We have talked about keeping the baby warm and helping the baby breathe. Now we come to
feeding — and this is where much of what we do out of habit turns out to work against the baby.
I will ask you questions about breastfeeding. Answer from what you see on your ward, not from
what you know is supposed to happen. Some of our routine practices are the problem, so please
be honest about them.
Watch for two things as we go. First, the baby sets the timing, not us — we wait for the baby to
show readiness. Second, almost every mother can feed her baby; when it seems she cannot, the
cause is usually something we have done or something we have given.
I will not correct answers straight away. We will reach them together."
Why is breastfeeding beneficial?
● Breastfeeding is one of the most life-saving interventions we have. Delays in initiation of
breastfeeding are associated with dramatic increase in death and illness. Babies who are fed
formula have 4–6 times the risk of dying compared with exclusively breastfed babies.
Formula is more dangerous to a newborn than is smoking to an adult.
When is a typical baby ready to breastfeed?
● Never immediately after birth. Leave the baby between the mother’s breasts in continuous
skin-to-skin contact. The baby may want to rest for 20–30 minutes or even longer before
showing signs of readiness to breastfeed (i.e. feeding cues). Just under half of all babies
initiate breastfeeding naturally after one hour from birth.
● Note: Please explain to the participants that babies should not be forced onto the breast or
have the nipple forced into the mouth. Wait for the feeding cues.
What are the feeding cues that a baby is ready to breastfeed?
● The earliest sign is drooling, followed by mouth opening, tonguing, licking, rooting and
biting of fingers or hand. Eventually, the baby will locate the breast and open his/her mouth
widely to attach to the breast.
Do some mothers not have enough milk?
● Studies in very poor countries (Ecuador, Nigeria) showed that all mothers (many of whom
were severely malnourished) were able physiologically to breastfeed. A newborn’s stomach
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
29 | Page
is only the size of your thumbnail. It may not be perceived that the baby has gotten enough
milk.
● If the baby appears to be fussy or dissatisfied at the mother’s breast, first ensure that no other
liquids are being given, i.e. formula, sugar water or other fluids.
● Counsel the mother on offering her breasts 8–12 times over the day and night upon her
baby’s early feeding cues.
● Stimulation of the breast by the baby’s sucking results in messages being sent to the mother’s
brain and back to the breast to produce more milk. Thus, milk production will increase if the
mother’s breast is stimulated more frequently through offering the breast more often.
What are signs of good feeding position?
● The baby’s head and body are in a straight line (neck is not twisted or flexed), the baby is
facing the breast, with nose opposite nipple, the mother is holding the baby’s body close to
her body and supporting the baby’s whole body (not just neck and shoulders).
● What are signs of good attachment?
● More areola is visible above the baby’s mouth than below; the mouth is wide open with the
lower lip turned outward, the baby’s chin touching breast; sucking is slow and deep with
occasional pauses.
● Note: Signs of ineffective attachment include in-drawing of newborn’s cheeks, lip-smacking
sounds or mother’s nipple pain
How can we improve attachment?
● By touching the baby’s lips with her nipple, waiting until the mouth is opened wide, moving
baby quickly onto breast, aiming baby’s lower lip well below nipple; also, by trying different
positions, e.g. sitting upright or laying on her side
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
30 | Page
2.3 Care of Normal newborn after 90 minutes of life and discharge home
Demonstrate correct technique for giving vitamin K injections to newborns
● Have participants practice injection technique (simulation) in pairs.
Site
• Anterolateral thigh (vastus lateralis), middle third — the only routine IM site in a
newborn.
• Never the buttock: risk of sciatic nerve injury and unreliable absorption. Never the
deltoid.
• Two injections at the same visit go into different thighs — for example vitamin K 1 mg in
one, hepatitis B vaccine 0.5 mL in the other.
• Maximum 0.5 mL per site.
Technique
● Clean the skin only if visibly soiled; if alcohol is used, let it dry fully.
● Hold the thigh firmly. Bunch the muscle between thumb and finger.
● Insert at 90° with a 23–25G needle, about 16 mm, to the correct depth.
● Do not aspirate — not required, and it increases pain.
● Inject slowly over 3–5 seconds.
● Withdraw, press gently with dry gauze. Do not rub.
Demonstration of GIVING EYE CARE
● Wash your hands.
● Hold one eye open and put a ribbon of Tetracycline 1% eye ointment along the inside of
the lower eyelid.
● Make sure not to let the medicine dropper or tube touch the baby's eye or anything else.
● Repeat this step to put medication into the other eye.
● Do not rinse out the eye medication.

● Purpose: The purpose of this role play is to help you practice counseling skills for

● Scenario The baby has been placed in skin-to-skin contact with Mother. and covered with

● Mother: The mother, 30-year-old, just gave birth to her fifth child, a healthy full-term

Discussion Questions
"You have just watched the role play. Before we go through what happened, I want you to think

FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
32 | Page
covered, then placed beside the mother. Advise on keeping the baby warm beyond the

● How did the health worker show good counseling skills when explaining to mother the

o By speaking in a calm reassuring manner
o By using words that mother understood
o By using the same reassuring manner to answer mother’s questions
o By encouraging mother to ask questions and express her concerns
● How did the health worker reassure Mother that her baby did not need a bath immediately?
The health worker explained that:
o Bathing her baby so soon may cause dangerous heat loss (i.e., the baby may develop
hypothermia or become cold). The new baby needs time to adjust to regulating its
own temperature.
o It is good to wait until at least 24 hours of life for the first bath, but if it is important to
her to bathe her baby early, she should wait until the baby is warm and at least 6 hours
after birth.
● What key messages did the health worker discuss with Mother? The key messages stressed
by the health worker should include the following:
o All newborns lose heat quickly after birth, and this can be dangerous.
o Skin-to-skin contact with the mother is the best way to keep the baby warm.
Skin-to-skin contact helps maternal-infant attachment and early breast-feeding

3.1 Basic Resuscitation of non-breathing baby
Role-play: Resuscitation (Positive Pressure Ventilation) of non-breathing newborn
without coaching
● Health-care providers doing their usual practice for delivering non breathing baby (done in a
large group).
● Ask for three participants, one participant each to play: (1) the woman delivering the baby;
(2) the attending health worker; and (3) the recorder.
● Ask the “mother” to wear the Mamanatalie™ and sit on the delivery bed. A facilitator will do
the set-up of the “baby” to appear to be in the mother’s womb.
● Explain to the attending health worker: “You will deliver a baby and provide care for non-
breathing baby. The woman is fully dilated. You have already weighed her, taken her blood
pressure, and listened for fetal heart tones. There is no more time, she will deliver in about a
minute. Please do exactly as you would during and immediately following the delivery from
the first step immediately before delivery until the last step in the immediate postpartum care
period. Please do not explain what you are doing.
● Explain to the note taker: “You will write down the exact actions/interventions you observe
before, during and immediately after delivery. Please record the time of each activity: to the
second for events occurring within three minutes of delivery, and to the minute for events
occurring thereafter.”
● Ask the other participants to pay close attention to what the attendant is doing, and take key
notes, as they will be asked to comment later. During the demonstration, they should not tell
the demonstrator what to do or how to do it.
● Ask the attendant to start the role-play. When the baby is “born”, a facilitator will make the
baby’s crying sound and make the cord pulsate. The facilitator should record – and time –
exactly what is happening: to the second for events occurring within the first three minutes
after delivery, and to the minute, at other times.

■ Check airway for blockage.
■ Squeeze bag harder.

o Follow-up of successful resuscitation
o Care when a baby needs referral after resuscitation
o Care of the parents when resuscitation fails and a newborn dies
● Demonstrate cleaning and disinfection of resuscitation equipment a
Demonstration: Synchronized Positive pressure ventilation with Chest Compression
Two facilitators are needed — one gives ventilation, one gives compressions. This shows
participants from the very start that this is teamwork, and not a one-person job.
Facilitator Script
“So far you have practised ventilation. Now we come to the baby who does not improve. This is
the baby whose heart rate stays below 60 even after we have given good ventilation. Watch us
carefully — then each of you will do it yourselves.”
Step 1 — Say when to start
“We do not start compressions early. We start only when the heart rate is still less than 60 after at
least 30 seconds of PPV that is moving the chest. If the chest is not moving, the answer is better
ventilation — not compressions.”
Step 2 — Show the position
• Move to the head of the bed to give compressions; the ventilator stays at the side.
• This leaves space for safe insertion of an umbilical venous catheter.
• Baby on a firm flat surface, back supported, neck slightly extended.
Step 3 — Show the landmark
• Thumbs on the lower third of the sternum, just below the imaginary line joining the two nipples.
• Encircle the chest with both hands, fingers supporting the back.
• Show the two-thumb method — state clearly that this is the preferred method.
Step 4 — Show the depth
• Press down about one-third of the antero-posterior diameter of the chest.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
38 | Page
• Allow the chest to come back up fully between compressions, but keep the thumbs resting on
the skin.
Step 5 — Show the oxygen
• Increase oxygen to 100% once compressions begin.
• Check the pulse oximeter signal.
Step 6 — Show the rhythm (the key skill)
Demonstrate slowly first, then at speed. Everyone says it out loud together:
“One – and – Two – and – Three – and – Breathe – and…”
• Compressor says: One, Two, Three
• Ventilator squeezes on: Breathe
• 3 compressions : 1 breath = one cycle
• 15 cycles in 30 seconds
• Per minute: 90 compressions + 30 breaths = 120 events
Let the whole class chant the rhythm twice before anyone touches a manikin.
Step 7 — Show the check
After 60 seconds of coordinated compressions and ventilation, pause and check the heart rate. A
cardiac monitor is preferred; auscultation is acceptable — count the beats in 6 seconds and multiply
by 10.
• HR ≥ 60 → stop chest compressions, continue PPV at 40–60 breaths/min.
• HR still < 60 → continue compressions and PPV, give epinephrine, and obtain emergency
vascular access (umbilical venous catheter).
Step 8 — Common mistakes to name out loud
• Starting compressions when the chest was never moving.
• Compressing and squeezing the bag at the same time.
• Going too fast and losing the 3:1 ratio.
• Lifting the thumbs off the chest between compressions.
• Stopping to check the heart rate before 60 seconds.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
39 | Page
Practical Cases, Positive pressure ventilation Synchronized Chest Compression
Purpose: the purpose of a clinical drill is to help participants practise responding to clinical
situations that require immediate action.
How to Run the Drill
Assign a team of three participants: ventilator, compressor, and recorder / timekeeper.
• Announce the case with a bell or a whistle.
• The team responds in real time.
• The facilitator plays the mother and the midwife, and reads out the heart rate when asked.
• The other participants observe using the skill checklist.
• Rotate the roles so that every participant performs compressions at least once.
Case 1 — Term baby, thick meconium
A 26-year-old mother delivers at term. The liquor is thick meconium. The baby is limp, not
breathing, with no tone.
• Initial steps → still apnoeic → PPV started.
• After 30 seconds of PPV: the chest is not moving.
• Expected action: MR SOPA corrective steps — not compressions yet.
• After correction the chest moves. At 30 more seconds: HR = 5 beats in 6 seconds (50 bpm).
• Expected action: Start synchronized chest compressions at 3:1, increase oxygen to 100%,
move to the head of the bed.
• After 60 seconds: HR = 8 in 6 seconds (80 bpm).
• Expected action: Stop compressions, continue PPV at 40–60/min.
Teaching point: the chest must move before compressions begin.
Case 2 — Preterm, no response
A mother at 34 weeks delivers a small baby. The baby is apnoeic and floppy.
• Initial steps, then PPV with good chest rise for 30 seconds. HR = 4 in 6 seconds (40 bpm).
• Expected action: Chest compressions at 3:1 with PPV, oxygen 100%.
• After 60 seconds: HR is still 40 bpm.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
40 | Page
• Expected action: Recheck the quality of ventilation and compression technique; consider
intubation or laryngeal mask; give epinephrine 0.1–0.3 ml/kg of 1:10,000 IV through the
umbilical venous catheter; establish emergency vascular access.
Teaching point: a heart rate below 60 after 60 seconds of good CPR means medication plus
vascular access.
Case 3 — Team coordination breakdown (deliberate)
The same as Case 1, but the facilitator privately instructs the compressor to go too fast and to
ignore the rhythm.
• The observers must identify the error and correct it.
• Expected action: Stop, restore “One-and-Two-and-Three-and-Breathe-and”, and confirm the
3:1 ratio.
Teaching point: a team that is out of rhythm gives no effective ventilation. Speak the count aloud.
Case 4 — Recovery and post-resuscitation care
A term baby after cord prolapse. The baby is apnoeic, HR = 3 in 6 seconds (30 bpm).
• PPV with chest rise → compressions at 3:1 → after 60 seconds HR = 7 in 6 seconds (70
bpm).
• Expected action: Stop compressions, continue PPV until HR > 100 and the baby breathes
spontaneously.
• The baby starts breathing and becomes pink.
• Expected action: Wean the oxygen to the targeted preductal SpO₂, keep the baby warm,
monitor blood sugar and temperature, explain to the mother, document, and arrange post-
resuscitation care or referral.
Teaching point: resuscitation does not end when the heart rate rises.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
41 | Page

3.3 Oropharyngeal Airway
The oropharyngeal airway is not the first answer to a baby who is not breathing. The first answer is
always position, clear the airway, and good bag-and-mask ventilation. The airway is used when the
tongue or the pharynx is blocking, and reposition has not worked.
Facilitator Script
“You have a baby, you have positioned the head, you have cleared the mouth, and you are
squeezing the bag — but the chest is still not moving. One of the reasons is the tongue falling
back. This small piece of plastic can solve it in seconds. Watch.”
Step 1 — When to use it
• When bag-and-mask ventilation stays ineffective because of airway obstruction, after reposition
and suction have already been tried.
• In a baby with Pierre Robin sequence (small chin, tongue falling back) — here it may be needed
early.
• In choanal atresia, where the nose is blocked and the baby cannot breathe through the mouth.
• In a floppy baby whose tongue and soft tissues collapse the pharynx.
Step 2 — When NOT to use it
• In a baby with an active gag reflex — it may cause vomiting, laryngospasm or bradycardia.
• As a substitute for repositioning the head. Try the correct position first.
• It does not protect the airway from aspiration.
Step 3 — Choose the size
Size Baby Approximate length
000 Preterm / small baby 3.5 cm
00 Term newborn 4 cm
0 Large term baby / infant 5 cm
How to measure: hold the airway along the side of the baby’s face. The flange should be at the level
of the lips, and the tip should reach the angle of the jaw.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
46 | Page
• Too small — it will push the tongue back and make the obstruction worse.
• Too large — it will press on the epiglottis and obstruct the larynx, or cause bradycardia.
Step 4 — Insert it (the newborn way)
1. Position the baby: neck slightly extended, on a firm flat surface.
2. Open the mouth with the finger and thumb.
3. Depress the tongue with a tongue depressor or the blade of a laryngoscope.
4. Insert the airway with the curve following the natural curve of the tongue — concave side
down, the same way it will sit.
5. Slide it gently over the tongue until the flange rests on the lips.
Do NOT insert it upside down and rotate 180° as is done in adults. The newborn palate is soft and
this will damage it.
6. Reapply the mask and give PPV. Look at the chest.
7. If the chest now moves — the obstruction was the tongue. Continue ventilation.
8. If the chest still does not move — recheck the seal, the position, and the secretions; then think
about an alternative airway (laryngeal mask or ET tube).
Step 5 — After insertion
• Watch continuously — the airway can be pushed out or blocked by secretions.
• Suction only if secretions are visible and are impairing ventilation.
• Monitor SpO₂ and heart rate.
• Remove it as soon as the baby is breathing well or a definitive airway is placed.
Step 6 — Common mistakes to name out loud
• Reaching for the airway before trying to reposition the head.
• Guessing the size instead of measuring against the face.
• Rotating it 180° like in an adult.
• Forcing it against resistance.
• Inserting it in a baby who is gagging.
Individual Practice: Oropharyngeal Airway
• Done in small groups, one facilitator per group.
• Break into groups according to the number of manikins / practice stations.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
47 | Page
• Each participant repeats the practice until they perform it perfectly.
• Coach the participants who are having difficulty. Ask those who did well to take over the
facilitator’s role.
Practice Case Scenario
“A term baby is born floppy and not breathing. You dried, positioned and stimulated. You started
PPV — but the chest is not moving. You repositioned and reapplied the mask, and it is still not
moving. Show me what you do now.”
Expected: recognise obstruction → select and measure the correct airway size → insert correctly
→ resume PPV → confirm chest rise → reassess heart rate.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
48 | Page
3.4 Intubation
Prerequisites
• Pre-oxygenate before intubation — PPV with 100% oxygen.
• Deliver free-flow oxygen during intubation.
• Not more than 20 seconds per attempt; not more than 3 attempts.
• Ventilate with bag and mask with 100% oxygen in between attempts.
Preparation
1. Assessment: assess the need for intubation based on the newborn’s clinical condition and
response to initial resuscitation measures.
2. Gather equipment: ensure all necessary equipment for intubation, including a laryngoscope,
endotracheal tube, suction, and securing devices, is readily available and prepared.
3. The intubation set for emergency intubation in the delivery room should always be ready and
checked before receiving the baby.
4. For equipment preparation, see the setup equipment in the mechanical ventilation section.
Equipment
• Bag and mask (sizes 0, 1)
• Oxygen cylinder
• Suction machine (pressure set at 80–100 mmHg) with suction catheters
• Sterile water, sterile gloves
• Laryngoscope with blades (size 00, 0, 1) with extra bulb
• ET tube (size 2.5, 3.0, 3.5, 4.0)
• Pulse oximeter, scissors, tape, stethoscope
• Stylet and end-tidal CO₂ detector
Size of ET Tube
Tube size (ID, mm) Weight (grams) Gestational age (weeks)
2.5 < 1000 < 28
3.0 1000–2000 28–34
3.5 2000–3000 34–38
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
49 | Page
Tube size (ID, mm) Weight (grams) Gestational age (weeks)
4.0 > 3000 > 38
Suction Catheter Size
ET tube size Suction catheter size
2.5 5F or 6F
3.0 6F or 8F
3.5 8F
4.0 8F or 10F
Depth of Insertion (Tip-to-Lip Distance)
Weight (kg) Depth of insertion (cm from upper lip)
1 7
2 8
3 9
4 10
The manual also gives the naso-tragal + 1 cm measurement as the guide to adjust the depth.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
50 | Page
Demonstration: Laryngoscopy & ET Tube Insertion
“I will do this slowly and say every step out loud. Then I will do it again at real speed, in 20
seconds, so you see what it looks like in the delivery room.”
1. Holding the laryngoscope in the left hand, use the right hand to open the mouth wide
(premedication can cause some spasm that may need to be overcome).
2. Gently insert the blade to push the tongue to the left and advance towards the throat.
3. Use the uvula to help maintain position in the midline. If the uvula is not in the midline,
reposition — the scope is usually heading to the left.
4. Advance the blade forward gently. Lift the laryngoscope handle in a forward and upward
motion. Avoid a levering action against the top gum.
5. Instructors’ hands on the infant’s neck can feel the blade tip (to help guide if the scope is too far
in or not far enough).
6. Cricoid pressure can be applied using the little finger of the left hand, or by an assistant (care
should be taken not to exert excessive pressure). With the video laryngoscope, an airway
assistant may also lift the top lip of the infant to allow more space.
7. Use suction only if secretions are impairing the view.
8. Bring the posterior larynx and vocal cords into view.
9. Hold the tube high up and with the natural slope facing forward, as it is easier to direct.
10. Feed the tube in from the side to allow visualisation of it passing through the cords.
11. Use the black marker near the tip of the tube as a guide to the depth of insertion initially.
12. Adjust to the previously measured naso-tragal + 1 cm measurement.
13. Be cautious as to how far to insert the tube, as it is often too far in.
14. Secure the ETT against the roof of the palate or firmly at the lips.
15. Carefully remove the laryngoscope and introducer (if used).
16. Confirm ETT position: chest rise, auscultate for air entry, and connect the CO₂ detector and
ventilation circuit to confirm correct ETT position.
17. Secure the ETT with an appropriate fixation device, ensuring a good seal to the skin and
avoiding traction on the ETT.
18. Provide IPPV once the ET position is confirmed and throughout the securing process.
19. Attach the ETT to the ventilator and adjust settings as required.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
51 | Page
20. Move the infant back into position carefully, ensuring the ET is secure when moving the
infant and using a 2-person technique to avoid accidental extubation.
Signs the ET Tube is in the Oesophagus
• Poor response to intubation (cyanosis, bradycardia, etc.)
• CO₂ detector fails to show presence of expired CO₂
• No audible breath sounds
• Air heard entering the stomach
• Gastric distension may be seen
• No mist in the tube
• Poor chest movements
Signs the ET Tube is Too Far in the Trachea
• Heart rate or colour shows no improvement
• Breath sounds heard over the right and not on the left side of the chest
• Breath sounds are louder on the right side of the chest than on the left
Post-Intubation Care
Continuously monitor the newborn’s vital signs, oxygenation and ventilation after successful
intubation.
1. Chest X-ray is the gold standard in confirming the correct ETT position; the ET tip should be
visible 1 cm above the carina (T2–T3).
2. Consider shortening the ETT to minimise dead space.
3. Ensure blades are sent for sterilisation as per unit protocol.
4. Ensure accurate documentation of the procedure, including the position of the ETT on chest X-
ray and whether or not adjustments were made.
Update the parents.
* intubation is an advanced procedure that should be performed by trained healthcare providers with
expertise in neonatal airway management.

Individual Practice: Intubation
Time: 40 minutes
• Done in small groups, one facilitator per group.
• Use the simulator for training vascular access and intubation in newborns, plus the
intubation kits.
• Each participant repeats the practice until they perform it perfectly.
• Coach those having difficulty. Ask those who did well to take over the facilitator’s role.
The 20-second rule is not a suggestion. If the participant reaches 20 seconds, they must stop,
remove the scope, and ventilate with bag and mask with 100% oxygen before trying again.
Maximum 3 attempts. A participant who ignores the clock has failed the station, even if the tube
went in.
Practice Cases intubation
Case A — Term baby, ineffective ventilation
A 3.2 kg term baby, apnoeic. PPV given with good technique but the chest is not expanding after
MR SOPA.
Expected: tube 4.0, blade size 1, depth 9 cm at the lip, suction catheter 8F or 10F.
Case B — Preterm needing surfactant
A 1.4 kg baby at 30 weeks with worsening respiratory distress; surfactant is required.
Expected: tube 3.0, blade size 0, depth 7–8 cm, suction catheter 6F or 8F.
Case C — Extremely preterm
A 900 g baby at 27 weeks, apnoeic at birth.
Expected: tube 2.5, blade size 00 or 0, depth 7 cm, suction catheter 5F or 6F. Handle gently —
thermal care continues throughout.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
54 | Page
Case D — Failed attempt
The participant intubates. On auscultation, air is heard entering the stomach and there is no
colour change on the CO₂ detector.
Expected: recognise oesophageal placement → remove the tube → ventilate with bag and mask
and 100% oxygen → reattempt. Does not leave the tube “just in case”.

3.5 Circulation: Venous Access (Umbilical Vein Catheter)
venous access should be established at the earliest, with the umbilical vein being the easiest vein to
be cannulated, through which medicines are given. The umbilical venous catheter is inserted 2 to 4
cm (less in preterm babies) until there is free flow of blood.
“Remember the baby from this morning — heart rate still under 60 after 60 seconds of good
compressions and ventilation. You said epinephrine. Now I am asking you: through what? This is the
session that answers that question.”
Demonstration — UVC
See UVC at equipment section
3.6 Giving Medications Through the Line
The medicines are required during resuscitation when, despite effective ventilation and chest
compression, the newborn still has a heart rate below 60 bpm. The medicines required are
epinephrine and normal saline (0.9% NaCl).
Medicine Dose and dilution Route and repeat
Epinephrine 0.1–0.3 ml/kg of 1:10,000 (dilute 1 ml
of 1:1000 with 9 ml normal saline)
IV via UVC. Repeat every 3–5 minutes
if HR stays below 60
Normal saline 10 ml/kg over 5–10 minutes IV / UVC. Not routine — for suspected
hypovolaemia (pale, tachycardic, CRT
> 3 sec). Repeat if no response

Practice Case 1 — Emergency access during resuscitation
The baby from the compression drill: HR still 40 after 60 seconds of coordinated CPR. A second
provider must gain access and give adrenaline while compressions continue.
Expected: rapid aseptic prep → identify the vein → insert 2–4 cm to free blood flow → secure →
give epinephrine 0.1–0.3 ml/kg of 1:10,000 → announce it to the team → flush.
Practice Case 2 — Resistance at 2 cm
The catheter will not advance past 2 cm.
Expected: stop, do not force, loosen the umbilical tape, adjust the angle of the cord to the skin,
apply steady gentle pressure. If still resistant — remove and try again. Never push into the liver.
Practice Case 3 — Wrong vessel
The participant selects a thick-walled vessel.
Expected: recognise it as an artery — two thick-walled arteries, one thin-walled wider vein
towards the head. Reidentify the vein correctly.
Practice Case 4 — Complication after insertion
Six hours later, redness and swelling appear around the umbilicus.
Expected: stop the infusion, remove the catheter, establish peripheral IV access, treat for
infection of the umbilicus, document, inform the parents.

Plenary discussion: discuss with the participants different scenarios for the
management of a non-breathing baby
"We have just practised bag-and-mask ventilation. Now we think through what happens next —
because starting ventilation is only the beginning. The decisions that follow are where most
errors occur.
I will take you through a series of situations. For each one, tell me what you would do and why.
There is a sequence here — each answer leads to the next decision — so listen to each other and
build on what is said.
Answer from your own practice. If you are not sure, say so — that is more useful than a guess."
● What do you do when the baby starts breathing well?
■ Return the baby to the mother in skin-to-skin contact.
■ Explain to the mother that the baby had difficulty breathing but is doing better now
■ Mother should let the health worker know if the baby has problems with breathing again.
■ Counsel her on the baby’s feeding cues, etc.
■ Monitor the baby every 15 minutes for breath-ing and warmth.
● If baby is not breathing, what do you do?
■ Check heart rate after one minute of bag-and-mask ventilation.
● How do we quickly check heart rate?
■ First, without pausing the ventilation, look at the cord. If there are no visible cord pulsations,
either put fingers on umbilical stump near abdomen, or use a stethoscope:
■ Count the number of beats in six seconds, then multiply by 10 to get the beats per minute.
This is accurate enough to indicate if the heart rate is < 100 or > 100 beats per minute (bpm).
● If heart rate is < 100 bpm, what do you do?
■ If heart rate is between 60 and 100 bpm, do ventilation corrective steps, then resume
ventilation and check heart rate again at 3 minutes; continue effective ventilation if heart rate
is < 100 bpm.
■ If heart rate is < 60 bpm, do ventilation corrective steps, resume ventilation.
● If heart rate is > 100 bpm and the baby is not breathing well, what do you do?
■ (Ventilate more slowly.)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
59 | Page
● Why?
■ We ventilated too quickly and did not give the baby a chance to breathe.
● If after 10 minutes of ventilation the baby has a heart rate but is still not breathing (or is having
difficulty breathing; has severe chest in-drawing, for example), what do we do?
■ Continue bag-and-mask ventilation, refer, ensure warm transport, and accompany the baby,
preferably with the mother, to hospital.
● If after 10 minutes of effective ventilation, the baby has no heart rate and is not breathing, what
do you do?
■ The baby is dead. Stop ventilation, provide emotional support to the mother, and record the
event.
● If after 20 minutes of effective ventilation, the baby is still not breathing and the heart rate is < 60
bpm, what do you do?
■ Stop ventilation. Explain to the mother that the baby still has not started to breathe and the
baby’s weak heart beat was not enough to give oxygen to the brain. Babies’ hearts that are as
weak as your baby’s usually stop beating soon.
● How many babies will need bag-and-mask ventilation?
■ If we dry babies well, only a few out of a hundred newborns.
● How do we position the mask?
■ Ask participants to demonstrate by: placing the mask over the baby’s chin, mouth and nose;
do not cover the eyes with the mask. Form a firm seal between the mask and the face (use
mask size 1 for normal-birth-weight newborn and size 0 for low-birth-weight newborn).
Squeeze the bag a few times to observe the rise of the chest.
● How do we know if the ventilation is adequate?
■ Chest is rising, baby’s heart rate rises, and baby gradually becomes pink.
● What do we do if the chest is not rising sufficiently?
■ Reposition head so neither flexed nor hyperextended, and lift chin. Should these fail,
reposition mask to get a better seal, check airway for blockage, squeeze bag harder. The bag
and mask should have been checked prior to resuscitation.
● How often should we squeeze the bag?
■ 40 times per minute (30–50 acceptable).
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
60 | Page
● What should we do when the baby starts breathing?
■ Stop ventilation and observe to ensure that the baby continues to breathe well. Assess
breathing. If breathing 30–60 breaths per minute without severe chest in-drawing, put the
baby in direct skin-to-skin contact on the mother’s chest and do routine newborn care
including observation for feeding cues. Reassure the mother, monitor every 15 minutes.
● How often should we reassess baby’s breathing and heart rate?
■ Every 1–2 minutes.
● Why would a baby have a good heart rate but not breathe?
Bag-and-mask ventilation may be too fast. We need to slow down the number of squeezes per
minute to allow the baby the chance to breathe on her own
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

● Have available: a weighing scale, thermometer, cup/spoon, syringe, and a doll or

Case 1 — Temperature Problem
Baby Nour — female, born at term, 39 weeks gestation
90 minutes of age
Read aloud to the provider:
“Baby Nour is 90 minutes old. She cried at birth and breastfed within the first hour. On
examination she is active, breathing 44 breaths per minute with no chest indrawing, and pink. Her
weight is 2900 g. Axillary temperature is 36.2°C.”
1. Using the Assessment on 90 minutes Quick reference table, which row applies to Baby Nour?
2. What actions does that row list?
3. What should the provider do right now?
Facilitator Answer Key — Part A
● 36.2°C is not in the danger-sign row (Temp <35.5°C or >38°C) and not in the well-baby
row (Temp 36.5–37.5°C).
● It falls in the middle row: Weight 1500–<2500 g / Temp 35.5–38°C / Feeding difficulty /
Risk of infection. Her weight (2900 g) is normal, but the temperature finding on its own
places her in this row.
● Apply the row's actions:
● Maintain skin-to-skin, support breastfeeding, and treat risk of infection.
● The “low weight or low temperature” sub-action applies: provide continuous skin-to-skin,
improve thermal care.
● Continue skin-to-skin contact; if interrupted, clothe/wrap in a clean, dry blanket and cover
the head; avoid drafts and contact with wet or cold surfaces.
● Reassess by touching the foot or forehead; if it feels too cool or too hot, measure with a
thermometer. “if not improved or had danger sign, stabilize for referral” — “if improved,
immunize and advise about home care.”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
65 | Page
Part B — one hour later
Read aloud to the provider:
“One hour later, Baby Nour's temperature is rechecked: 35.3°C. She is still skin-to-skin and
attempting to breastfeed, but her feet feel cool to touch.”
1. Which row of the table applies now?
2. What must the provider do, in order?
Facilitator Answer Key — Part B
• 35.3°C is below 35.5°C — this now meets the top (danger-sign) row of the table. The action
listed is: give antibiotics; stabilize for referral as needed.
• Improve thermal care immediately: remove any wet clothing, cover the head, add a dry
layer/blanket, warm the room, keep continuous, uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact.
• Give antibiotics: give the first dose without delay, per the Sudan neonatal sepsis protocol
(Ampicillin 50 mg/kg + Gentamicin 5 mg/kg). Do not delay waiting for investigations.
• Stabilize for referral as needed: explain to the parents why urgent treatment and referral are
needed; keep mother and baby together; maintain warmth and skin-to-skin during transport;
complete a referral note.
• Recheck temperature once thermal care is improved. Do not use uncontrolled heat sources —
the manual warns against placing a baby close to heat sources and against over-wrapping.
Debrief questions:
• Who in your facility is authorized to give the first antibiotic dose, and how quickly can this
actually happen after a danger sign is identified?
• What are the risks of overheating a baby while trying to correct hypothermia?
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
66 | Page
Case 2 — Birth Weight Problem
Baby Yasin — male, born at 32 weeks gestation
Read aloud to the provider:
“Baby Yasin was born at 32 weeks gestation by spontaneous vaginal delivery at a health centre.
He cried weakly at birth. At 90 minutes of age he is weighed: 1350 g. Temperature is 36.6°C. He
is breathing 48 breaths per minute with no chest indrawing. He has not yet attempted to
breastfeed.”
1. Which row of the Section 3.2 table applies to Baby Yasin?
2. What does Section 3.4 say for a baby this size?
3. What immediate care is required?
Facilitator Answer Key
• Weight <1500 g is listed on its own in the top row of the table — action: stabilize for referral
as needed. This applies even though his temperature and breathing are currently normal.
babies <1500 g “should receive advanced care and prolonged skin-to-skin (kangaroo mother
care).”
• Begin prolonged skin-to-skin (Kangaroo Mother) care with the mother or another family
member.
• Complete essential newborn care if not yet done: eye care, cord care, vitamin K.
• Support attempts at the breast; if he cannot attach, plan for expressed breast milk by cup or
tube, per the table's feeding-difficulty sub-action.
• Prepare for referral if this facility cannot provide the advanced care,Complete the newborn
record and fill a referral note; keep mother and baby together.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
67 | Page
Case 3 — Feeding Problem
Baby Salma — female, born at 35 weeks gestation
Read aloud to the provider:
“Baby Salma is 4 hours old, born at 35 weeks, weighing 1950 g. Temperature is 36.8°C and
breathing is normal. She attempted to breastfeed at 1 hour of age but has not latched. She licks at
the nipple but falls asleep after a few minutes without sucking.”
1. Which row of the Section 3.2 table applies to Baby Salma, and why?
2. What does that row say to do about her feeding difficulty specifically?
Facilitator Answer Key
• Weight 1950 g (1500–<2500 g) with feeding difficulty places her in the table's middle row.
• The row's feeding-difficulty sub-action lists:
• Support attachment keep mother and baby together; encourage breastfeeding whenever she
shows signs of readiness; help the mother into a comfortable, semi-reclined position, baby
held close, supported along the whole body, head and body in line, facing the breast.
• Express breast milk and feed with cup or tube, exactly as the table's sub-action states.
• Reassess using signs of good attachment: more areola seen above the lips than below; mouth
wide open; lower lip turned outward; chin touching the breast.
• If not improved, or a danger sign appears, stabilize for referral
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
68 | Page

Baby Mohammed — male, born at 33 weeks gestation
90 minutes of age

of age his temperature is 36.7°C, breathing is 40 breaths per minute with no chest indrawing, and

1. Which row applies to Baby Mohammed and why?
2. List every action the provider should take now.

5. Selected Neonatal Emergencies
Introduction
This section focuses on time-critical neonatal emergencies that commonly cause preventable death
or brain injury in Sudan if recognition or treatment is delayed.
Core principle to emphasize:
Recognize early → Treat immediately → Reassess → Refer if needed
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
By the end of this section, participants should be able to:
Knowledge
● Identify early and late signs of neonatal shock
● Define neonatal hypoglycemia and seizure emergencies
● Describe first-line emergency management
Skills
● Perform rapid emergency assessment
● Initiate correct immediate treatment
● Stabilize newborns prior to referral
Attitudes
● Act without delay
● Prioritize safety and reassessment
● Communicate clearly with the team and family
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

5.1 SHOCK IN NEONATES
teaching Methods: Case-based discussion + algorithm review + simulation
Facilitator Preparation
● Review shock section in Sudan MSSN manual
● Prepare:
o Shock recognition chart
o IV fluids (simulation)
o Glucose chart
Interactive session: neonatal shock
Ask participant to open Chrtbooklet
. Interactive Introduction
Ask the participants:
• “How does shock present in adults?”
• “Why is shock difficult to recognize in newborns?”
Guide the discussion toward: subtle signs, and late hypotension in neonates.
“In an adult you wait for the blood pressure to tell you. In a newborn, by the time the blood pressure
falls, you have lost the argument. The newborn tells you earlier — but quietly. Tachycardia and a
slow capillary refill are the whole message.”
Key Teaching Points
Neonatal shock often presents with:
• Tachycardia
• Poor perfusion
• Prolonged capillary refill
• Lethargy
Blood pressure falls LATE. A normal blood pressure does not exclude shock.
Common causes in Sudan:
• Sepsis
• Hypovolemia
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
71 | Page
• Hypoglycaemia
• Birth asphyxia
Cold Shock vs Warm Shock — Teach the Difference
COLD shock WARM shock
Extremities Cold, mottled Warm, flushed
Capillary
refill
Prolonged (> 3 sec) Flash (< 1 sec)
Pulses Weak, thready Bounding
Commonest
in
Hypovolaemia, cardiogenic, late sepsis Early septic shock
What it
means
Vasoconstricted — the body is fighting to
hold pressure
Vasodilated — the vessels have
given up
Both are shock. Both need fluid. Cold shock is what you will see most often in Sudan.
Case Study: Neonatal Shock Management
A 5-day-old newborn is brought into the emergency unit.
System Findings
Weight 3 kg
History Poor feeding, vomiting
General Lethargic, low temperature
Respiratory Fast breathing, mild grunting
Cardiovascular Fast heart rate, cold extremities, mottled skin, poor capillary refill
Metabolic Point-of-care test shows hypoglycaemia
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
72 | Page
Question 1: What is your primary assessment?
Answer: Neonatal shock (cold type), complicated by respiratory distress and hypoglycaemia.
Question 2: What is your immediate and targeted management using the ABC approach?
Airway (A) & Breathing (B):
• Secure the airway.
• Administer oxygen to treat the hypoxaemia and respiratory distress.
Circulation (C):
• Establish IV access immediately and draw labs (blood culture, metabolic panel).
• Fluids: give normal saline or Ringer’s lactate.
– Calculation: 20 ml/kg × 3 kg = 60 ml. Give over 15 to 30 minutes. (Reduce to 10 ml/kg =
30 ml if cardiogenic shock is suspected.)
• Antibiotics: give broad-spectrum empirical antibiotics immediately after drawing the cultures.
• Support: prepare vasopressors (epinephrine or dopamine) if hypotension persists after the 60
ml fluid bolus.
Dextrose / Disability (D):
• Treat the hypoglycaemia immediately to prevent further neurological deterioration.
• Dextrose: give 10% dextrose (D10W).
– Calculation: 2 ml/kg × 3 kg = 6 ml IV bolus.
Question 3: What indicators show the baby is improving?
• Normalized heart rate and blood pressure.
• Stronger pulses, warm extremities, improved skin perfusion.
• Improved alertness and return of normal reflexes — no longer lethargic.
Follow-up labs showing normal blood glucose, corrected metabolic acidosis, and reduced lactate.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
73 | Page
Case 2 — The Preterm with Cold Shock
A preterm baby is lethargic and feeding poorly. On assessment:
Parameter Value
Heart rate 180 / min
Capillary refill time 5 seconds
Hands and feet Cold
Respiratory rate 60 / min
Oxygen saturation 92% on oxygen
Blood glucose 2.1 mmol/L
• What signs suggest poor circulation?
• Is this baby in shock?
• What type of shock is most likely?
• What is the first life-saving action?
• What investigations should NOT delay treatment?
Expected Participant Responses
Signs of shock:
• Tachycardia
• Prolonged capillary refill
• Cold extremities
• Lethargy
Likely causes:
• Sepsis
• Hypovolaemia
• Hypoglycaemia contributing
Appropriate Actions
1. Immediate stabilization
• Ensure airway and breathing are supported.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
74 | Page
• Keep the baby warm.
2. Correct hypoglycaemia
• Give IV 10% dextrose bolus if indicated.
• Start a glucose infusion.
3. Fluid management
• Give a careful fluid bolus if there are signs of hypovolaemia: 10 ml/kg normal saline over 30–60
minutes.
• Avoid rapid or repeated boluses without reassessment.
4. Start empirical antibiotics
• As per the Sudan neonatal sepsis protocol.
• Do NOT delay antibiotics waiting for labs.
5. Close monitoring
• Reassess: heart rate, capillary refill, urine output, respiratory status.
6. Escalation
If no improvement: suspect septic shock, prepare for referral, consider inotropes where available.
FACILITATOR HINT
notice the two different fluid volumes in these two cases — 20 ml/kg for the 5-day-old in the
first case, and 10 ml/kg here for the preterm. That is not an error. Ask the group why. Guide
toward: prematurity, the risk of IVH, and a heart that may already be failing. The volume is a
decision, not a reflex.
Key Teaching Points
• Shock in newborns is often subtle.
• Tachycardia + poor perfusion = danger.
• Treat first, investigate later.
• Fluids must be careful and controlled.
Common Errors
Giving large or repeated fluid boluses rapidly
Ignoring glucose
Delaying antibiotics
Failure to reassess after intervention
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
75 | Page

Teaching Methods: Short presentation + skills stations + case scenarios

Interactive session: Neonatal Hypoglycemia
Ask participant to open Chrtbooklet
The Action Threshold — Say It First
The operational threshold for hypoglycaemia is typically < 2.5 mmol/L (45 mg/dL). The presence
of ANY clinical symptom — even jitteriness — immediately confirms the need for clinical
intervention, rather than just continued observation.
“A number alone does not decide. A number plus a symptom decides. And a symptom with a
borderline number still decides.”
Case Study: Neonatal Shock
Divide into three groups. Each group rotates through all three stations. 20 minutes per station.
Station A — Recognition & Screening
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION
Scenario: an at-risk newborn requires screening. The group must identify who to screen, when,
and what the reading means.
Facilitator Q&A — Station A
Debrief: action threshold.
• Discuss with the trainees that the operational threshold for hypoglycaemia is typically < 2.5
mmol/L (45 mg/dL).
• Emphasize that the presence of any clinical symptom (like jitteriness) immediately confirms
the need for clinical intervention, rather than just continued observation.
Who to Screen
• Infants of diabetic mothers (IDM)
• Large for gestational age (LGA)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
78 | Page
• Small for gestational age (SGA) and IUGR
• Preterm and low birth weight
• Any sick, septic, asphyxiated or hypothermic baby
• Any baby with poor feeding, jitteriness, lethargy, apnoea or seizures
Station B — Moderate Hypoglycaemia Management
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION
Scenario: a term infant of a diabetic mother (IDM) is breastfeeding poorly. The current blood
glucose reads 2.3 mmol/L (41 mg/dL).
Equipment provided: breastmilk substitute, NG tubes, feeding syringes.
Facilitator Q&A — Station B
Task 1: Identify and classify.
Question: Based on the glucose reading of 2.3 mmol/L and the baby’s current clinical
presentation, how do you classify this condition?
Answer: This is moderate hypoglycaemia. The reading is slightly below the target threshold of
2.5 mmol/L (45 mg/dL). Because there are no severe neurological signs (such as seizures or
coma), an immediate enteral feeding trial is appropriate rather than jumping straight to IV
dextrose.
Task 2: Initiate NG feeding.
Question: What is your immediate intervention using the provided equipment?
Answer: Initiate a nasogastric (NG) tube feed. Since the baby is feeding poorly and expending
energy, the NG tube guarantees immediate caloric intake. Administer expressed breastmilk or the
provided substitute.
Task 3: Document.
Question: What key details must be recorded in the patient’s chart right now?
Answer: Document the initial blood glucose level (2.3 mmol/L), the clinical presentation (poor
feeding), and the exact time, type and volume of the feed given via the NG tube.
Task 4: Plan monitoring.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
79 | Page
Question: When is the appropriate time to recheck this baby’s blood glucose?
Answer: Recheck 30 to 60 minutes after the NG feed is completed, to confirm the level has risen
to a safe threshold (≥ 2.5 mmol/L).
Teaching point for Station B: not every low glucose needs a needle. A baby who can be fed,
should be fed. Reaching for IV dextrose in a feeding baby is over-treatment.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
80 | Page
Station C — Severe Hypoglycaemia Emergency
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION
Scenario: a large for gestational age (LGA) newborn, estimated weight 4 kg, presents with
clinical seizures. The current blood glucose reads 1.2 mmol/L (21 mg/dL).
Equipment provided: IV arm simulator, infusion pumps, medication labels, 10% dextrose (D10W)
syringes and bags.
Facilitator Q&A — Station C
Task 1: Identify and classify.
Question: Given the glucose reading of 1.2 mmol/L and the presentation of seizures, how do you
classify and approach this condition?
Answer: This is a severe, symptomatic hypoglycaemic emergency. The presence of acute
neurological signs (seizures) combined with profoundly low glucose mandates immediate
intravenous intervention. Enteral feeding is contraindicated at this moment.
Task 2: IV access & bolus calculation.
Question: You have established IV access on the simulator. Calculate the exact dose of the
immediate rescue medication for this 4 kg baby.
Answer: 10% dextrose at 2.5 ml/kg. Calculation: 4 kg × 2.5 ml/kg = 10 ml of D10W IV bolus.
The medication role must draw up exactly 10 ml.
Task 3: Set up the continuous infusion.
Answer: Program the infusion pump for an initial glucose infusion rate (GIR) of 8 mg/kg/min, or
a baseline of 10% dextrose at 60 ml/kg/day.
Task 4: Reassess.
Answer: Recheck the blood glucose exactly 30 minutes after the intervention, then follow the
traffic-light pathway below.
Halt the drill at the 5-minute mark. Review team communication, role clarity, and time-to-
medication delivery. In a seizing hypoglycaemic baby, the clock is the assessment.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
81 | Page
The Traffic-Light Decision Pathway — at 30 Minutes
Pathway Blood glucose at 30 min Action
RED < 1.0 mmol/L OR
abnormal clinical signs
persist
Repeat the entire cycle:
• Give another bolus
• Increase the GIR
• Recheck in 30 minutes
YELLOW 1.0 – 2.5 mmol/L AND
no abnormal clinical
signs
• Increase GIR by 2 mg/kg/min
• Continue oral feeds
• Recheck blood glucose in 30 minutes
• An IV bolus is NOT indicated — say this out loud
GREEN > 2.5 mmol/L AND no
abnormal clinical signs
• Continue enteral feeds
• Begin a slow wean of the IV infusion
Resolution and discharge: continue monitoring blood glucose until the infant is on full enteral
feeds AND the blood glucose is > 2.5 mmol/L (> 3.0 in hyperinsulinism) over several feed cycles for
at least 24 hours.
