5.3 Neonatal Seizures
Teaching methods: Video examples + case discussion + simulation
Objectives
1. Differentiate true neonatal seizures from common mimics using the STOP protocol.
2. Initiate appropriate emergency management within 10 minutes of recognition.
3. Administer correct first-line anticonvulsant therapy safely.
4. Document seizure events comprehensively using standardized forms.
5. Determine appropriate referral timing and prepare for safe transport.
Facilitator Preparation
• Review the neonatal seizure guide.
• Prepare: a case-based interactive presentation; seizure videos or descriptions; medication
charts (phenobarbital); documentation forms.
• Load the videos offline before the session and test the sound.
Interactive session: Neonatal Convulsion
Ask participant to open Chrtbooklet Discuss:
• What is a seizure?
• Common mimics: jitteriness, startle reflex, normal movements.
Introduce and repeat: the importance of a glucose check BEFORE anticonvulsants.
The STOP Test — Seizure or Mimic?
Use the S-T-O-P mnemonic at the cot side. It takes 20 seconds and it decides whether you give a
drug.
Ask Seizure if…
S Stimulus — did I
provoke it?
It happens on its own. A mimic is often provoked or made worse
by handling.
T Touch — what
happens if I hold the
limb gently?
It carries on; you feel the muscle contracting. A mimic stops
immediately with gentle restraint.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
84 | Page
Ask Seizure if…
O Observe — the pattern
and the eyes
Rhythmic and slow (< 4 jerks/sec), localized; there may be
nystagmus or tonic eye deviation. A mimic is fast, tremor-like,
oscillatory, generalized, with normal eyes.
P Presence — is the
baby there?
Consciousness is often altered. In a mimic the baby is awake, or
asleep but arousable.
Seizure vs Mimic
Feature Seizure (e.g. focal clonic) Non-seizure event (jitteriness,
benign myoclonus)
Stimulus
sensitivity
Not provoked by stimulus Often provoked or enhanced by
handling / stimulation

Stops immediately with gentle

Eye signs May have nystagmus or tonic
deviation
Normal
Demonstrate the T of STOP on the manikin, then on a volunteer's hand. Let every participant feel the
difference between a tremor that stops when you hold it, and a contraction that does not. This single
test prevents unnecessary phenobarbital.
The SHOT Mnemonic — Rapid Etiologic Evaluation
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
85 | Page
Means Do
S Sugar & Sepsis Bedside glucose immediately — treat hypoglycaemia
(< 2.6 mmol/L) with 10% dextrose 2–5 ml/kg IV bolus
then maintenance infusion. Assume sepsis/meningitis
until proved otherwise: blood culture, broad-spectrum
antibiotics plus acyclovir if HSV possible.
H Hypoxia & History Assess for perinatal asphyxia (low Apgars, need for
resuscitation). Consider HIE as the leading cause.
Quick history: maternal fever, diabetes, drug use,
difficult delivery, family history of neonatal seizures.
O Osmolality &
Electrolytes
Check electrolytes, calcium, magnesium. Correct
urgently. Hypocalcaemia: 10% calcium gluconate 1–2
ml/kg IV over 10 min with cardiac monitoring.
Hypomagnesaemia: 50% MgSO₄ 0.2 ml/kg IM/IV
slowly.
T Trauma & Toxins /
Treatable metabolic
Consider intracranial haemorrhage, especially after
traumatic delivery. Consider pyridoxine-dependent
seizures in refractory cases — empiric trial pyridoxine
100 mg IV while monitoring EEG and respiration (risk
of apnoea).
Video session Neonatal convulsion
• Play each clip once without comment.
• Each participant writes: SEIZURE or MIMIC — and which letter of STOP decided it.
• Then reveal, and discuss only the disagreements.

Case Study: Neonatal Convulsions
A premature newborn weighing 1.5 kg begins to exhibit clinical convulsions in the NICU.
• Vitals & access: the infant has secure IV access and is currently stable on respiratory
support.
• Stat labs: a point-of-care test reveals an ionized calcium level of 0.7 mmol/L. Blood glucose
and magnesium are normal.
Facilitator Q&A Guide
Task 1: Immediate intervention (0–15 minutes)
Question: According to Step 1 of the immediate intervention protocol, what is your first action
based on the lab results?
Answer: You must correct the reversible cause first. The infant has hypocalcaemia (ionized Ca <
0.8 mmol/L). Action: treat immediately with 10% calcium gluconate IV.
Calculation: 1 ml/kg × 1.5 kg = 1.5 ml IV over 10 minutes, under cardiac monitoring. Avoid
extravasation — tissue necrosis risk.
Task 2: First-line antiseizure medication
Question: The seizures continue despite correction of the calcium level. What is the first-line
antiseizure medication, and what is the exact loading dose for this 1.5 kg infant?
Answer: The first-line medication is phenobarbital. The loading dose is 20 mg/kg IV over 20
minutes.
Calculation: 1.5 kg × 20 mg/kg = 30 mg IV.
Follow-up question: What side effects must you monitor for during this infusion?
Answer: Monitor closely for respiratory depression and hypotension.
Task 3: Phase 2 escalation (15–60 minutes)
Question: It has been 20 minutes since the phenobarbital loading dose was completed, but the
infant is still seizing. What is your next immediate step?
Answer: Administer an additional dose of phenobarbital: 10 mg/kg IV over 10 minutes.
Calculation: 1.5 kg × 10 mg/kg = 15 mg IV.
Task 4: Further escalation (if seizures persist)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
87 | Page
Question: The infant has now received a total of 40 mg/kg of phenobarbital in the first 24 hours
(the maximum loading dose), but seizures persist. What are your pharmacological options now?
Answer: Choose one of: Option A Levetiracetam · Option B Fosphenytoin/phenytoin · Option C
Midazolam infusion · Option D Lidocaine infusion.
FACILITATOR HINT
notice that this baby got calcium before phenobarbital. Ask the group why. Guide toward: a
seizure with a correctable cause does not need a drug that stops breathing. Fix the cause and the
seizure may simply stop.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
88 | Page
Case 2 — The Baby Who Was Never Seizing
A 2-day-old term baby, 3 kg, is referred for “fits”. The midwife describes shaking of both arms.
On your examination the baby is awake, looking at you, and the arms are trembling rapidly.
When you gently hold the wrist, the trembling stops. Blood glucose is 3.4 mmol/L. Temperature
36.9°C.
Ask the group:
• Apply STOP out loud. What do you find?
• Does this baby get phenobarbital?
• What would you tell the referring midwife?
Facilitator Answer Key
This is jitteriness, not a seizure. S — provoked by handling. T — stops with gentle restraint. O
— fast, tremor-like, generalized; eyes normal. P — the baby is awake and alert.
No phenobarbital. It would sedate a well baby and risk apnoea for nothing.
But still: check the glucose (done — normal), check the calcium, and ask about maternal drug
use. Jitteriness is not always innocent — it just is not a seizure. Thank the midwife for referring.
She was right to be worried; the answer is teaching, not blame.
Teaching point: the commonest error in this session is not missing a seizure. It is treating a
tremor. Both errors are dangerous — STOP protects you from the second one.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
89 | Page

& referral
Documents the seizure event on the standardized form;
determines referral timing and prepares for safe transport

6. Neonatal referral and transport
Learning objectives
1. Identify type of transfer and babies who need referral.
2. Prepare and organize referral.
3. Provide counseling and family support.
4. Provide pre-referral stabilization and enroute care.
5. Documentation and handover.
Facilitator Script — Opening
"unplanned or non-organized newborn transfer from the labour room or delivery facility to the
NICU may increase mortality and morbidity, leading to worse outcomes. A simple, timely,
efficient approach without any panic is required".
“An unstable baby who is moved will deteriorate on the way and may arrive in a moribund state —
which defeats the whole purpose of the referral. The ambulance is not the treatment. What you do in
the 30 minutes before the ambulance leaves is the treatment. Watch me do it, step by step.”
Discussion what challenges facing neonatal transport
Ask the group to mention challenges of neonatal transport, ensure they cover the following which
of these they face: Write the group’s answers on the flip chart. Keep them visible — you will
come back to them in the drill.
• Facilities are scarce and not easily available.
• Families have poor resources.
• Organized transport services are not available — at times the baby may have to be
transported on foot or by cart.
• No health provider is available to accompany the baby.
• Facilities are not fully geared up to receive sick neonates.
• Communication systems are non-existent or inefficient.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
91 | Page
Demonstration of transferee process
Facilitator: set up the manikin, transport incubator, oxygen cylinder, bag and mask, and
monitoring equipment before the session. Run the demonstration in real time with a co-facilitator
playing the team member. Participants observe first, then each group runs the sequence
themselves.
"A baby needs to move. Watch what we do — and in what order. The order is not a suggestion. A
baby who leaves before Step 3 is complete arrives worse than he left."
Demonstrate each step, narrating as you go. Pause at Steps 3, 4 and 7 for the group to practise
the specific skills below.
Step 1 — Assess
The attending health worker or midwife carefully assesses the baby to determine:
• Vital signs
• Severity of distress
• Degree of maturity
• Presence of congenital anomalies or other complications
“Make sure there is a genuine indication for referral. Do not move a baby because you are anxious.
Move a baby because the care they need is not here.”
Step 2 — Decide
• The reason or indication for admission.
• The level of care needed — intensive care, HDU or SCBU.
• The type of respiratory support needed — nasal cannula, HHFNC, CPAP or ventilator.
Whenever possible, the transport team communicates and discusses their assessment and decision
with the SCBU/NICU medical staff before moving.
Step 3 — Stabilize (STABLE): Maintain Warmth, A, B, C
It is of utmost importance that a neonate is stabilized before the transport is begun. Say this sentence
out loud to the class.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
92 | Page
Letter Assess Act
Temperature By touch or
thermometer
Normalize BEFORE leaving. Warmer, KMC, plastic
or polyethylene bag, cover, hat. Increase incubator

Airway Neck position,

Correct position with a shoulder roll. Suction.

Step 4 — Check Equipment
Transport equipment must be checked by assigned staff before moving:
• Oxygen source (cylinder or concentrator)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
93 | Page
• CPAP
• T-piece
• PPV bag
• Transport ventilator
• Transport incubator
• Monitors
• Infection control equipment
Whenever a high-risk delivery is anticipated and transfer is expected, all equipment should be
checked early and the transport incubator prewarmed. The team leader must confirm that the
equipment check was done and assure readiness before moving. Equipment failure during transport
may lead to deterioration and loss of valuable time.
Oxygen — Do the Calculation Out Loud
Portable oxygen needed = 2 × [FiO₂ flow (L/min) × expected journey time (minutes)]. A full transfer
cylinder contains 250 L.
“The baby needs 2 litres a minute. The journey is 45 minutes. Two times two times forty-five is 180
litres. My cylinder holds 250. I have enough — but only just. What if we are delayed at a
checkpoint? Take a second cylinder.”
Step 5 — Organize the Team and Assign Duties
• Assemble a qualified and experienced team — nurses and medical staff, or a trained community
midwife.
• A team leader decides the number of staff, distributes duties between members, and supervises
them.
• A stable newborn may be transferred on a cot with a nurse.
• A sick intubated preterm needs 2 or more medical and nursing staff.
Duties include the preparation actions (assessing and deciding — usually by the most senior or the
team leader, and stabilization) and the duties during transport: maintaining the airway or head
position, ventilating or bagging, monitoring, and maintaining safe transport.
Step 6 — Documentation
Assign one team member to document and record. This includes:
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
94 | Page
• Newborn data: GA, weight, diagnosis
• Apgar score at 1, 5 and 10 minutes
• Serial recording of vital signs and temperature before and after transfer
• The equipment check
• Parental and staff communication
• Timing of moving to the SCNU
• Any event during transport
Step 7 — Communication
The manual lists five communication channels:
• Between the neonatal unit and the obstetric department or primary facility staff
• With the parents
• With the receiving team in the NICU
• Between team members
• With the ambulance, for outborn cases
With the parents:
• Explain the need for transfer and the reasons for it. Outline the initial plan if there is time.
• Explain the condition and the prognosis.
• Explain where to go and whom to contact.
• Encourage the mother to accompany the baby.
With the receiving team: establish a clear channel, usually by phone. The GA, diagnosis, level of
care and type of respiratory support needed should be relayed clearly and re-read back by the
receiving team.
“We are going to transfer an ex-preterm, 28 weeks, RDS, intubated by size 3 ETT with stable vitals,
to level 3 NICU for ventilatory support.”
Between team members: the team should work in harmony. Orders from the team leader must be
clear and address a specific member by name; the member gives clear feedback.
“What is the baby’s temperature now, Ms. Mona?” — “Baby’s temperature now is 36.8 degrees,
Doctor.”
Demonstrate this exchange with a co-facilitator. Then have the whole class practise one closed-loop
call-and-response before moving on.
Step 8 — Transfer
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
95 | Page
1. Inform the SCNU of the anticipated time of arrival.
2. Use the safest available method.
3. Avoid delays and interruptions.
4. Keep monitoring and observing the baby during transport.
5. On arrival, transfer the baby to an SCNU bed and give a detailed handover to the SCNU staff.
The aim is a transfer that is simple, comfortable, safe, stable and closely monitored.
Care During Transport
Explain these to the accompanying person, whoever they are:
Element What to do
No noxious stimuli Minimal handling. Reduce noise, light and painful procedures en

Emergent Stabilize and arrange for early referral — do not delay once the
decision is made.
No sepsis Infection control practices during transport, with minimal handling.

Case study – Transport Sick and small newborn
Purpose: the purpose of a clinical drill is to help participants practise responding to clinical
situations that require immediate action.
How to Run It
• Assign a team of four: team leader, nurse (airway and warmth), nurse (monitoring and
documentation), and one who makes the phone calls.
• Announce the case. The team has 10 minutes to prepare the baby and “leave”.
• The facilitator plays the mother, the ambulance driver, and the receiving NICU on the
phone.
• The other participants observe using the Transfer Checklist.
• Rotate roles so that every participant leads once.
FACILITATOR HINT
be a difficult receiving unit at least once. Say the bed is not confirmed. Say the ambulance has no
oxygen. This is what the manual means by ‘facilities are not fully geared up’ and
‘communication systems are inefficient’ — practise it here, not for the first time at 2 a.m.
Case 1 — The Preterm Needing Ventilation
A baby born at 28 weeks, 1.1 kg, in a facility with no ventilator. Grunting, chest indrawing, SpO₂
84% on nasal cannula. Temperature 35.8°C. The nearest level 3 NICU is 45 minutes away.
Ask the team:
• Is there a genuine indication for referral? What level of care and what respiratory support?
• What must be corrected before you leave?
• How much oxygen do you need for the journey?
• Who accompanies the baby?
Expected: genuine indication — ventilatory support not available here. Correct the temperature
FIRST (polyethylene bag, hat, prewarmed incubator) — do not leave at 35.8°C. Secure the
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
97 | Page
airway; consider elective intubation if in doubt. Check glucose. Calculate oxygen: 2 × (flow ×
45). Sick preterm needs 2 or more staff. Phone the NICU with a clear read-back handover.
Document the temperature before and after.
Teaching point: the commonest killer on the road is not the disease — it is the cold. Normalize
the temperature before you commence transportation.
Role Play: Communication with the Family
Use Case 2 or Case 3 above as the scenario.
Before You Start
• Define the objective of the role play.
• Define the setting.
• Define the time limit.
• Define the observers’ task.
“This scenario is taken from situations you have shared in the group discussion. It gives you the
chance to practise what you might say when faced with this situation at your workplace. It’s a
great way to explore what works and what doesn’t. There is no right or wrong way of
approaching it — just a deepening of understanding, and the chance to practise some new ways
of putting words together when dealing with parents.”
Cast
• A health service provider, a mother, and a father.
• Be creative and add more characters if you wish — a second provider, a mother-in-law, an
ambulance driver.
• The remaining participants observe.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
98 | Page
The Eight Counselling Points
The provider should counsel the family on:
1. The reason for the need for transfer — non-availability of higher facilities in the SNCU.
2. What is likely to happen if treatment is continued at the SNCU.
3. What is likely to happen during transport.
4. What means of transport are available.
5. The approximate cost of transport.
6. The approximate time transport will take.
7. What care or treatment is likely to be given at the tertiary level.
8. The likely management after going to the tertiary level.
Stress the importance of KMC during transport, and of educating the primary caregiver about the
baby’s needs during the journey.
Debriefing
• Appreciate the efforts of the role players first.
• Ask the players for their own feelings and their assessment of how they approached it.
• Then invite the observers: what was addressed well — non-verbal cues, simple language,
answering the parents’ queries?
• What could be improved, and what are the strategies for that?
Effective communication reduces parental anxiety and helps parents make informed decisions on
behalf of the newborn. It maintains a healthy association between providers and parents, and
prevents conflict. It should preferably be done by a senior person or a person with expertise.

7. Admission of newborn
7.1 Triage of the Newborn at the SCNU
Lecture (Triaging Newborn at Entry of Neonatal unit)
What Triage Is?
Triage is a quick assessment and sorting process used to evaluate and categorize sick newborns
shortly after they arrive at the special care newborn unit. It helps prioritize care and treatment based
on the severity of the condition and the resources available.
Triage is about priority and speed. Triage should take less than 60 seconds.
“Triage is not diagnosis. You are not asking ‘what is wrong with this baby?’ You are asking one
question only: ‘can this baby wait?’ That question must be answered in less than a minute.”
The Three Groups
The provider carries out a brief, focused assessment and places the newborn in one of three groups:
1. Emergency signs (danger signs) — the newborn is critically ill and at immediate risk of death if
urgent intervention is not provided.
2. Priority signs — the baby needs prompt attention and should be moved ahead in the queue for
timely assessment and treatment.
3. Non-urgent signs — signs that do not fall under emergency or priority, and can wait for further
evaluation without immediate risk.
Demonstration — What You Assess in 60 Seconds
Demonstrate on the manikin. Say each one out loud as you check it:
• Temperature
• Respiration
• Colour
• Capillary refill time
• Look for seizures and level of consciousness
The Triage Chart — Classify and Act
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
102 | Page

• Apnoea or gasping

Common Errors

• Waiting for the full history

After Triage — Further Assessment
Once emergency care has been given and the baby is stabilized, carry out a detailed assessment: a
comprehensive history, and identification of at-risk neonates based on maternal characteristics.
• Maternal age: under 16 or over 40 years
• Personal factors: poor nutritional status, smoking, drug or alcohol use, trauma
• Maternal medical conditions: diabetes, thyroid, renal, UTI, cardiac and lung disease;
hypertension, anaemia, thrombocytopenia
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
104 | Page
• Obstetric history: bleeding in early pregnancy or third trimester; PROM, fever, TORCH
infection; maternal medications, trauma
• Past history of an infant with prematurity, jaundice, RDS, or congenital anomalies
Triage Case Scenarios
Facilitator Prompt
How to Run It
• Read each scenario aloud. Give the group 60 seconds — use a watch.
• Each participant writes down one word only: Emergency, Priority, or Non-urgent.
• Then ask: what is your first action? Where should this baby be managed?
• Do not correct immediately. Let the group discuss first.
Case 1 — The Cold Baby from the Rural Health Centre
A 2-hour-old male newborn is brought to the neonatal unit by a midwife from a rural health
centre. He was born at 38 weeks by spontaneous vaginal delivery, but the delivery room was cold
and immediate drying and warming were inadequate. The baby did not cry at birth and required
brief stimulation, after which he had irregular breathing.
On arrival he appears lethargic and unresponsive to handling. His skin feels cold, and there is
blue discolouration of the lips and tongue.
Vital signs: HR 160 bpm, RR 77/min, SpO₂ 72%, axillary temperature 35.2°C. The extremities
are cold, capillary refill time is 4 seconds, and the pulse is weak and rapid. He has apnoeic
episodes with occasional gasping respirations, severe chest retractions and an audible expiratory
grunt.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
105 | Page
Facilitator Answer Key
Classification: EMERGENCY. He has almost every emergency sign at once — hypothermia
below 36°C, apnoea and gasping, severe respiratory distress (rate above 70 with severe
retractions and grunt), central cyanosis, and shock (cold periphery, CRT 4 seconds, weak fast
pulse).
First actions: manage the airway, tactile stimulation, PPV for the gasping, give oxygen, start re-
warming, insert an IV line and give 10 ml/kg normal saline over 30 minutes, check the glucose.
Stabilize, then admit to the SCNU.
Teaching point: this baby is cold because of what happened in the delivery room two hours ago.
Everything else on this list follows from that. The golden minute and the golden hour are not
separate from triage — they are why this baby is in front of you.
Case 2 — The Baby with the Omphalocele
A 6-hour-old male newborn is brought to the unit after delivery at a rural health facility. He was
born by spontaneous vaginal delivery to a 20-year-old primigravida with no antenatal care. The
mother reports the baby cried weakly at birth and has been “not looking well” since.
On arrival he appears small and fragile, birth weight 1.6 kg. He has a large omphalocele with
bowel loops visible, covered by a thin sac.
Findings: the baby is cold and irritable. PR 120 bpm, SpO₂ 90% on room air, temperature 36.2°C,
RR 68/min, no chest retractions or grunting. The abdomen is distended and the omphalocele is
prominent. The skin and sclera are deeply jaundiced. There is severe pallor with pale mucous
membranes and delayed capillary refill, oozing of blood from the umbilical stump, and mild
bleeding from a heel-prick site.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
106 | Page
Facilitator Answer Key
Classification: PRIORITY — and read the list carefully, because this baby collects almost
every priority sign there is: cold stress (36.2°C, between 36 and 36.5), respiratory distress with
rate above 60 and no retractions, irritable, abdominal distension, severe jaundice, severe pallor,
bleeding from more than one site, a major congenital malformation, and weight under 1.8 kg.
But: check the capillary refill and the pulse volume carefully. ‘Delayed capillary refill’ with
severe pallor and active bleeding is the edge of shock. If CRT is over 3 seconds with a weak fast
pulse, this becomes an EMERGENCY.
Actions: assess and act rapidly, admit to the SCNU. Cover the omphalocele with warm sterile
saline gauze and plastic wrap — do not let it dry, and remember it is a huge route of heat and
fluid loss. NG tube for the distended abdomen. Vitamin K and investigate the bleeding. Check
the glucose and the haemoglobin. Warm the baby.
Teaching point: a baby can be ‘priority’ on paper and slipping into ‘emergency’ while you write.
Triage is not done once. Re-triage the baby who is waiting.
Case 3 — The One-Day-Old from the Maternity Ward
A 1-day-old newborn arrives from the maternity ward. The baby is cold, not feeding, and
breathing fast.
Ask:
• Is this an emergency?
• What action must be taken immediately?
• Where should this baby be managed?
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
107 | Page
Facilitator Answer Key
Emergency. Immediate stabilization. SCBU / NICU admission.
Note how little information you were given — and note that you did not need any more. Cold,
not feeding, breathing fast is enough to act on. Waiting for the full history is one of the three
common errors.
Case 4 — The Baby Who Can Wait
A 3-day-old term baby, birth weight 3.1 kg, brought by the mother because he is “yellow”. He is
feeding well and is active. Temperature 36.8°C, RR 48/min, no indrawing, no grunting. There is
mild jaundice of the face and chest, and a small cephalhaematoma. The cord is clean and dry.
Facilitator Answer Key
Classification: NON-URGENT. Jaundice and minor birth trauma are both on the non-urgent
list. He is feeding, warm, and not distressed.
Action: assess fully and counsel the parents. That does not mean send them away — it means
take a proper history, examine the baby, measure the bilirubin according to your protocol, and
teach the mother the danger signs and when to come back immediately.
Teaching point: ‘non-urgent’ does not mean ‘not important’. It means this baby does not need
your hands in the next 60 seconds. It still needs your attention today.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
108 | Page
7.2 Admission Process — Welcoming and Preparation
Interactive session Admission Process — Welcoming and Preparation
Goals of the Admission Process
1. To ensure a high standard o
2. f neonatal care on admission at all levels of the SCNU.
3. To minimize stress, conserving the energy and time of the staff.
4. To enhance awareness and ensure readiness of the SCNU team for an organized and smooth
admission process.
Why It Matters
The admission process can be challenging and involves several key steps to ensure the baby's
immediate stabilization and comprehensive care. These steps need to be well organized, smooth and
time-effective, aiming to increase Golden Hour success rates, decrease staff stress, and ultimately
improve patient outcomes.
Admission of newborns, particularly sick newborns, is a crucial part of the Golden Hour of the
baby's life. The clock does not stop at the door of your unit.
Demonstration and Role Play: Receiving the Newborn Baby
Once the baby has arrived at the SCNU, the next interventions have to be done
SIMULTANEOUSLY — not one after the other. This is the single most important teaching point
of this session. Demonstrate it with three facilitators working at the same time on one manikin.
(a) Endorsement or Handover
• A thorough handover from the transferring unit to the SCNU team is crucial, including
details about the baby's condition, medications, and any special needs.
• Each staff member — medical and nursing — should receive their own endorsement.
• Maternal, antenatal and perinatal history is reviewed and documented.
• Admission orders should be reviewed carefully by the admission team.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
109 | Page
Use SBAR
Letter Means Example
S — Situation Who is this baby, right now? “This is a 2-hour-old male, 38 weeks, 2.9
kg, admitted for respiratory distress and
hypothermia.”
B —
Background
How did we get here? “Born at a rural health centre, cold delivery
room, did not cry, needed stimulation.
Mother 20, primigravida, no antenatal
care.”
A — Assessment What did you find? “Temperature 35.2, saturation 72%,
gasping, CRT 4 seconds. He is shocked and
cold.”
R —
Recommendation
What do you need? “He needs PPV, warming, IV access and a
fluid bolus now. I need a second nurse.”
(b) Assessment
• Assessment, monitoring and maintenance of stability are ongoing processes during all the steps
of the golden hour of life.
• A rapid assessment, including evaluation of vital signs (heart rate, breathing, admission
temperature), a physical examination, and potentially some initial lab tests such as blood gas
analysis.
• Growth parameters are measured immediately; however, the measurement may be delayed until
the baby is stable enough.
• A checklist for endorsement and assessment helps organize the admission process and prevents
confusion or missing important actions.
‘Growth parameters may be delayed until the baby is stable enough’ — say this out loud to the
group. Do not undress and weigh a cold, shocked baby to fill a box on a form. Stabilize first. The
scale can wait; the temperature cannot.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
110 | Page
(c) Maintaining Stability and Monitoring
Once in the SCNU, the baby is closely monitored:
• Vital signs, oxygen saturation, body temperature and other physiological parameters.
• Maintenance of warmth to prevent serious hypothermia is crucial.
• Incubator temperature (servo or manual) and humidity adjustment is very vital on admission.
• Wrapping the baby with plastic bags may be needed, especially for extreme and VLBW infants.
• The degree of respiratory distress and the efficacy of respiratory support should be monitored
and adjusted as needed.
• Blood glucose monitoring and judicious management of hypoglycaemia is crucial to prevent
serious CNS complications of hypoglycaemia.
Writing and Reviewing Admission Orders
The Rule: The most senior paediatrician or neonatologist may need to give some verbal orders
during the assessment. However, the final admission orders should be written and recorded
immediately after finishing the assessment. The orders should be very clear and in a systematic
manner.
“Verbal orders are for the first five minutes only. If it is still verbal after the assessment is finished, it
is not an order — it is a rumour.”
The Systematic Order Set
Teach the participants to write in this exact order, every time, so that nothing is forgotten:
System What to order
Level of care Decide and state it first — intensive care, HDU or SCBU
CNS Sedation, imaging
CVS Monitoring, imaging, inotropes
Respiratory Type of support and machine settings, frequency of blood gases,
imaging

FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
111 | Page
System What to order
Procedures Lines (UVC, UAC), LP, needle aspiration
Medications Antibiotics, others
Reviewing the Orders — The Safety Step
• The orders must be recorded and reviewed by nursing staff — at least 2 nurses.
• Any confusion or unclear order should be clarified with the medical staff immediately.
• All requested blood samples should be withdrawn as soon as possible.
• IV medications are administered as soon as the IV line is ready.
• The nursing staff prepares for all procedures and assists the medical staff during procedures
such as fixation of central lines.
Two nurses reviewing is not bureaucracy. It is the last barrier between a decimal point and a dead
baby. Apply patient and medication safety practices: correct patient ID, double-check doses, use of
order sheets.
Exercise : Writing admission order
• Give each small group the clinical details of Case 1 or Case 2 from Session 2.
• Each group writes a full admission order set on a blank order sheet, using the nine headings
above.
• Groups then swap sheets. The receiving group plays the two reviewing nurses.
• The reviewing group must find and challenge: anything unclear, any missing system, any
dose they cannot verify.
• Debrief on what was found. The errors the group finds are the curriculum — not the errors
you predicted.

7.3 Golden hour of life
Goals
1. To understand the concept of the Golden Hour.
2. To know the significance of Golden Hour interventions.
3. To gain an approach that uses the best available evidence, aiming to implement safe, effective
and timely interventions.
4. Prevention of hypothermia.
5. Support of respiration.
6. Support of the cardiovascular system.
7. Support of nutrition and hydration.
8. Prevention of sepsis.
What the First Hour Includes
1. Early essential newborn care and neonatal resuscitation.
2. Post-resuscitation care.
3. Transportation of the sick newborn to the neonatal intensive care unit.
4. Respiratory and cardiovascular support, and nutrition support.
5. The initial course in the SCNU.
Look at that list again. Everything you have trained on this week is in it — the golden minute,
resuscitation, compressions, intubation, vascular access, transport, triage, admission. The Golden
Hour is not a new topic. It is the thread that ties the whole course together.
Why It Matters
Neonatal management in the first hour of life has an important effect on both the immediate and the
long-term outcomes of all neonates.
The studies that evaluated the concept of the golden hour in preterm neonates showed a marked
reduction in:
1. Hypothermia
2. Hypoglycaemia
3. Intraventricular haemorrhage (IVH)
4. Bronchopulmonary dysplasia (BPD)
5. Retinopathy of prematurity (ROP)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

8. Prematurity & low birth weigh
8.1 Prematurity & low birth weight complications
Learning Objectives
By the end of this module, participants will be able to:
• Define prematurity and classify preterm newborns according to gestational age and birth weight.
• Describe the basic pathophysiological concepts underlying prematurity.
• Identify common complications of prematurity, associated risk factors, and their impact on
neonatal health.
• Apply evidence-based management protocols for prematurity and its major complications.
And for the admitted baby specifically, participants will be able to:
• Decide the appropriate place of care by birth weight.
• Describe the care of a LBW baby and the daily monitoring required.
• Describe Kangaroo Mother Care, and help a mother start skin-to-skin care.
• Help a mother breastfeed a LBW baby.
• Recognise danger signs and know when to escalate or refer.
Interactive Lecture: complication of prematurity
• Ask participants to raise their hands if they have cared for a LBW baby.
• Ask several who raised their hands to describe the problems they have seen.
• List these on the flip chart.
• Explain the objectives for the session.
“Keep that list on the wall for the whole session. Every problem you have just named is in this
chapter. By the end of today you will have an answer for each one — and you will notice how many
of the answers are free.”
Set the Scale of the Problem
• Preterm birth complications are the leading cause of death among children under 5 years of age.
Three-quarters of these deaths could be prevented with current, cost-effective interventions.
• Across countries, the rate of preterm birth ranges from 4–16% of babies.
• Approximately 900,000 children died in 2019 of complications of preterm birth.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
116 | Page
• Preterm births account for approximately 70% of neonatal deaths and 36% of infant deaths, as
well as 25–50% of cases of long-term neurologic impairment in children.
In low-income settings, half of the babies born at or below 32 weeks die due to a lack of feasible,
cost-effective care such as warmth, breastfeeding support, and basic care for infections and breathing
difficulties. In high-income countries, almost all these babies survive.
Sudan: in 2023 the neonatal mortality rate was estimated at 24.8 per 1,000 live births — nearly
41,690 newborns dying annually. The stillbirth rate is estimated at 21.51 per 1,000 births, translating
into 36,973 annual deaths.
FACILITATOR HINT
read the sentence about low-income settings out loud, slowly, twice. Then ask: ‘warmth,
breastfeeding support, basic care for infection and breathing — which of those four needs a
machine?’ Let the silence sit. That is the whole argument for this training.
Definitions and Classification
Preterm babies are defined as babies born alive before 37 weeks of pregnancy are completed.
Low birth weight (LBW) babies are those with a birth weight less than 2.5 kg, irrespective of their
period of gestation. LBW infants have a 2–3 times increased risk of mortality.
Classification by Gestational Age
Category Gestational age Note
Extremely preterm < 28 weeks Some references classify 23–25
weeks as micro preemie (birth
weight should be < 500 g)
Very preterm 28 to 31 6/7 weeks
Moderate to late preterm 32 to < 36 6/7 weeks The largest group — and the
one most often underestimated

Category Birth weight Abbreviation
Low birth weight 1500 – 2500 g LBW
Very low birth weight 1000 – 1500 g VLBW
Extremely low birth weight 500 – 1000 g ELBW
Micro preemie < 500 g —

Two Clinical Types of LBW — Do Not Confuse Them
Type Means
Preterm / premature Born before 37 weeks of gestation. Small because he arrived early.
Small for gestational
age (SGA) / IUGR
Poor fetal growth during pregnancy. Small because he did not grow
— he may be term.
A preterm SGA infant has a combination of clinical features suggestive of both prematurity and
IUGR — and the risks of both.
“Two babies, both 1.8 kg. One is 32 weeks and grew perfectly. The other is 39 weeks and starved in
the womb. Same number on the scale — completely different babies, different problems, different
risks. The scale does not tell you which one you have. The examination does.”

Ponderal Index = weight (g) / length (cm)³ × 100
EXERCISE / PRACTICE
hand out the growth chart. Give the group three babies — 34 weeks/2.4 kg, 34 weeks/1.4 kg, 40
weeks/2.2 kg. Have them plot all three and name each one: preterm AGA, preterm SGA, term
SGA. Twenty minutes, and the classification is theirs for life.
Why the Preterm Baby Is Different, The Prematurity Challenges
• Need for advanced respiratory support and care
• Maintenance of body temperature
• Haemodynamic stability
• Nutrition and feeding
• Maintenance of growth
• Dealing with very delicate and sensitive immature organs
The Rule That Explains Everything
Complications are inversely proportionate to gestational age. The smaller and younger the baby, the
more, and the worse.
These complications can range from devastating intracranial haemorrhage, sepsis, severe
bronchopulmonary dysplasia and necrotising enterocolitis totalis, down to minor feeding-related
issues.
Ask the Group — Then Build the Table Together
“Do not let me give you this table. Build it. Tell me: what does a 28-week baby not have that a term
baby has?”
Let them answer; write it on the flip chart; then show this:

“Before you treat anything, decide where this baby belongs. Getting this wrong wastes a bed, or
costs a life. There is no third outcome.”

Prematurity Case Scenarios
Read each case aloud. Give the group 10 minutes. Encourage discussion before correction. End each
case with the key message and the referral threshold.
Case 1 — Where Does He Belong?
A baby is born at 35 weeks, birth weight 1.9 kg. He is pink, active, temperature 36.8°C, RR
48/min with no indrawing. He attaches at the breast and sucks for a few minutes, then tires.
Ask:
• Is he preterm, LBW, or both? Classify him by both systems.
• Where should he be cared for?
• What exactly do you do?
ANSWER KEY
Classification: moderate to late preterm (32 to < 36 6/7 weeks) AND low birth weight (1500–
2500 g). Both.
Place of care: birth weight > 1.8 kg → postnatal ward WITH the mother. He does not need a
nursery bed.
What you do: monitor — and train the mother. KMC and temperature assessment by touch;
breastfeeding and milk expression; LBW feeding content and technique; recognition and
reporting of danger signs. Vitamin K 1 mg IM (he is ≥ 1000 g). Delay bathing.
The catch: ‘he tires’. Watch him. If he becomes unable to feed from breast or cup, or becomes
sick, he is admitted to the nursery immediately — regardless of the 1.9 kg.
KEY MESSAGE
the weight decides the starting place. The baby decides whether he stays there.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
121 | Page
Case 2 — The 1.2 kg Baby at 29 Weeks
A baby is born at 29 weeks, 1.2 kg, in a facility with CPAP but no ventilator and no surfactant.
At 10 minutes of age he is grunting, with indrawing, RR 70, SpO₂ 86% in air. Temperature
35.6°C.
Ask:
• Classify him by gestation and by weight.
• What is the FIRST thing to correct?
• Does he get prophylactic surfactant? Why not?
• Where does he belong, and what do you do about that?
ANSWER KEY
Classification: very preterm (28 to 31 6/7 weeks); very low birth weight (1000–1500 g).
First: the temperature. T comes before A in this baby. 35.6°C — polyethylene bag, hat,
prewarmed incubator. Then the airway and breathing.
Respiratory: early CPAP — started here, continued during any transfer. Bubble CPAP is fine.
Target SpO₂ 85–92%: start low oxygen and titrate.
Prophylactic surfactant: NO. It is no longer indicated — it showed a trend toward increasing
mortality and BPD. Early CPAP reduces the NEED for surfactant.
Place of care: < 1.5 kg → NICU Level III. This facility is not it. Stabilize — warm, CPAP,
glucose, vitamin K 1 mg IM — and arrange transfer. If he ends up intubated before transfer, he
should get surfactant before he travels.
Caffeine: < 30 weeks — he gets caffeine routinely.
KEY MESSAGE
the answer to ‘we have no surfactant’ is not despair. It is early CPAP, which the evidence says
you should be doing first anyway.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
122 | Page
Case 3 — Same Weight, Different Baby
Two babies arrive in your unit in the same hour. Both weigh 1.7 kg.
Baby A Baby B
Gestation 32 weeks 39 weeks
Appearance Red thin skin, lanugo, floppy, head
looks large, creases only on the
front of the sole
Long and thin, alert, loose skin
folds on the buttocks, marasmic,
creases across the whole sole,
meconium staining
Mother Twin pregnancy, spontaneous
preterm labour
Pre-eclampsia, no antenatal care,
short stature
Head circumference Proportionate Normal for term — exceeds chest
by 4 cm
Ask:
• Name each baby.
• Which one is at higher risk of RDS? Of hypoglycaemia? Of hypothermia?
• Do they go to the same place?
ANSWER KEY
Baby A: preterm, appropriate for gestational age. LBW because he is early.
Baby B: term SGA with asymmetrical IUGR — long, thin, alert, marasmic, excess skin folds,
ponderal index < 2, head circumference exceeding chest by more than 3 cm, meconium staining,
placental insufficiency from the pre-eclampsia. LBW because he did not grow.
RDS: Baby A — immature lungs. Baby B has term lungs.
Hypoglycaemia: BOTH — but for different reasons. A has not laid down glycogen yet; B has
used his up. B may be the more brittle.
Hypothermia: BOTH — A has no fat and thin skin; B has no fat either. Little surface insulation
on a large surface area.
Place: both are 1.5–1.8 kg → Level II. Same place, same weight — completely different watch
lists.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
123 | Page
KEY MESSAGE
never let the scale do your thinking. Two babies at 1.7 kg are not the same problem.
Case 4 — Day 5, Something Has Changed
A 28-week baby, now 5 days old, 1.1 kg, has been stable on CPAP in your unit. This morning the
nurse reports three apnoeic episodes in the last hour, each needing stimulation. He had been
having none. He is on caffeine. Temperature 36.9°C. Feeds have been going in but there were 2
ml of green aspirate this morning and the abdomen looks fuller.
Ask:
• Is this ‘just apnoea of prematurity’?
• What is your list? What do you check?
• What is the green aspirate telling you?
ANSWER KEY
No. He was not having apnoeas; now he is. A CHANGE in a stable preterm is never ‘just
prematurity’. Apnoea of prematurity does not start on day 5 in a baby already on caffeine.
The list to exclude: sepsis, hypoxaemia, shock · IVH, seizures · PDA · anaemia · drugs ·
hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia · temperature · obstruction · reflux.
The green aspirate plus a fuller abdomen plus new apnoea: think NEC and think sepsis. Stop
feeds, NG tube on free drainage, blood culture, antibiotics, abdominal X-ray, check glucose,
check haemoglobin. Look for a PDA murmur.
The trap in this case: increasing the caffeine dose and going back to the ward round. Every group
has someone who does this. Let the debrief say it, not you.
KEY MESSAGE
new apnoea in a preterm is a symptom, not a diagnosis. Find out what it is a symptom OF.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
124 | Page
8.2 Kangaroo Mother Care
Learning Objectives
By the end of this session, participants will be able to:
• Define KMC as a comprehensive care package, not a single intervention.
• State the benefits of KMC for newborns, mothers, families and the health system.
• State where and when KMC is provided — and that weight is not a barrier.
• Distinguish immediate, continuous and intermittent KMC.
• Demonstrate correct skin-to-skin positioning, step by step.
• Monitor a baby in KMC and teach a mother the danger signs.
• Support feeding in the KMC position — breast, cup and tube.
• Coach a mother and family, and hand over the caregiving role to them.
• Apply the criteria for transfer, down-referral, and discharge.
Training Methods
• Facilitated group discussion and experience sharing
• Demonstration and return demonstration
• Skills coaching in small groups
• Case scenarios
• Clinical practice with real mother-baby pairs, where available
Materials Required
• Newborn manikin or doll, plus KMC ties / wraps / pouches (bring more than one design)
• Hats and nappies
• Chairs with armrests; two pillows; a bed if possible
• Privacy screen
• Feeding cups, feeding tubes, syringes
• Thermometer and baby weighing scale
• Growth chart, monitoring chart, discharge documents, immunization card
• Flip chart and markers
• KMC video, if available (load it offline beforehand)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
125 | Page
Interactive session: KMC
What KMC Is, and What It Is Not
Open With a Question
“What is Kangaroo Mother Care?”
Let them answer. Write every answer on the flip chart. Do not correct anything yet.
Most groups will say ‘skin-to-skin’ and stop there. That is exactly the gap this session closes.
The Definition — Present It as Written
Kangaroo Mother Care (KMC) is: care of preterm and low-birth-weight newborns in prolonged (as
close as possible to 24 hours a day, with a minimum of eight hours a day) skin-to-skin contact
initiated as soon as possible after birth, with exclusive breast milk feeding. An additional feature
where KMC is initiated in health facilities is timely discharge from the neonatal intensive or special
care unit to a lower level of care within the facility or at home, with continued skin-to-skin contact
and close monitoring.
Now go back to the flip chart and ask them to find, in their own answers, the four things the
definition contains:
1. Prolonged skin-to-skin contact — as close to 24 hours as possible, minimum 8.
2. Initiated as soon as possible after birth.
3. Exclusive breast milk feeding.
4. Timely discharge to a lower level of care, with continued skin-to-skin and close monitoring.
The Sentence to Underline
KMC is a comprehensive care package that includes the mother-baby pair and their family,
supported by healthcare workers, aimed at minimizing separation between mother and baby
during hospitalization. It is NOT merely an intervention — it is a fundamental standard of care
for all preterm and LBW newborns.
“Read that last part again. Not ‘a good idea if we have time’. Not ‘a nice thing for the mother’. A
fundamental standard of care. That means a preterm baby who did not receive KMC today received
substandard care today — the same way a baby who did not receive his antibiotics did.”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
126 | Page
The Key Points — Teach These as the Spine of the Whole Session
• KMC is a comprehensive care package that minimizes separation between mother and baby,
involving prolonged skin-to-skin contact and exclusive breast milk feeding.
• KMC should be initiated as soon as possible after birth for all preterm and low-birth-weight
newborns, regardless of weight or gestational age.
• The goal is continuous KMC (as close to 24 hours as possible, minimum 8 hours daily) with
zero mother-baby separation.
• KMC provides immediate and long-term benefits for newborns, mothers, families and the health
system — including improved survival, neurodevelopment, breastfeeding outcomes, and
reduced hospital stay.
• KMC is safe for both stable and unstable babies, can be practised everywhere (labour room,
NICU, postnatal ward, and home), and requires no additional staff.
• Transfer of babies should ideally occur in the skin-to-skin position, and discharge criteria focus
on feeding competence and maternal readiness rather than weight alone.
• KMC is equally important for late preterm infants (34–36+6 weeks), who are often cared for in
postnatal wards and may not receive adequate skin-to-skin support.
That last point deserves a full minute. Ask: ‘who in this room has a 35-week baby on the postnatal
ward right now?’ Then ask: ‘is that baby in skin-to-skin?’ The late preterm is the baby everyone
forgets, because he looks fine.
Benefits: Building the Case
Do Not Give Them the List
• Ask participants if they are aware of the benefits of KMC.
• List their responses on the flip chart.
• Complete their list only afterwards.
“You will name the baby benefits easily. Now name one benefit for the mother. Now name one for
the health system. Now name one for you personally, as a nurse on this ward.”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
127 | Page
Key Evidence Highlights
• Immediate KMC significantly reduces neonatal mortality, particularly within the first 72 hours
of life.
• KMC reduces the incidence of severe infections, including sepsis and necrotizing enterocolitis
(NEC).
• KMC improves thermoregulation, reduces hypoglycaemia, and stabilizes vital signs.
• Long-term benefits include improved neurodevelopmental outcomes, academic performance,
and reduced behavioural disorders.
• Mothers practising KMC experience less anxiety, reduced postpartum depression, and improved
breastfeeding success.
The Full Benefits Table
Category Benefits
Immediate
benefits for
newborns
• Better chance of survival
• Better immunity
• Decreased incidence of severe infections or sepsis (including NEC)
• More stable vital signs, from improved overall physiological regulation
• Improved thermoregulation (less hypothermia)
• Fewer episodes of hypoglycaemia
• Earlier initiation of breastfeeding, better feeding, longer exclusive
breastfeeding
• Better growth and weight gain
• Decreased length of hospital stay
• Protection from pain and decreased pain with procedures
• Less crying and decreased stress (lower cortisol levels)

• Optimal developmentally supportive care (neuroprotective)

FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

FACILITATOR HINT
point at ‘likely to receive higher hourly wages’ and let the room laugh. Then say: that is a real
finding, from real follow-up studies, of babies who were held. What you do at 3 a.m. on this ward
is still being measured thirty years from now.
Where and When to Provide KMC
Place What happens there
Labour room Initiate immediately after birth
Postnatal ward Continue KMC as the standard of care
Newborn care unit and
NICU
Mothers should have a bed at the baby's bedside, to enable continuous
KMC
Home After discharge, KMC continues until at least 40 weeks corrected
gestational age, or until the baby reaches 2.5–3 kg
No additional staff is required to implement KMC. The mother or caregiver provides the care, with
support from healthcare workers.
In humanitarian and conflict settings, KMC is particularly valuable: it requires no sophisticated
equipment, can be practised in temporary shelters, and empowers families to provide life-saving care
even when health facilities are disrupted.
FACILITATOR HINT
that paragraph is written for Sudan. Read it out loud, then ask the group where they have worked
in the last two years, and what they had. Let them talk. KMC is the one intervention on this
whole course that a displaced mother in a tent can still receive.
The Barriers Conversation — Do Not Skip It
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
130 | Page
“Name the challenges to carrying out KMC in your facility. Not in theory — in your ward, this
month.”
Write every barrier on the flip chart. Then work through them as a group. The commonest are:
Barrier they will name What the protocol answers
“We have no staff” No additional staff is required. The mother is the caregiver.
“We have no KMC ward” KMC is practised everywhere — labour room, postnatal ward,
NICU, home.
“The baby is too small” Weight is not a barrier to initiation.
“The baby is unstable” KMC is safe for unstable babies. The OMWaNA trial tested
exactly this.
“The mother is on another
ward”
Mothers should have a bed at the baby's bedside. Zero
separation is the goal.
“We are too busy” KMC reduces length of stay, readmissions, and expenditure. It
makes you less busy.
“Cultural resistance” A real barrier — brainstorm this one properly. Fathers and
family members can also provide KMC.
Keep this flip chart. It goes to the unit manager at the end of the course.
When — As Soon As Possible After Birth
KMC should be initiated as soon as possible after birth for ALL preterm or LBW newborns. Weight
is NOT a barrier to initiation. KMC is recommended for 8–24 hours per day — as many hours as
possible.
Immediate KMC
Place the baby skin-to-skin immediately after birth, unless:
• The baby is haemodynamically unstable or unable to breathe.
• The mother is critically ill, and no substitute caregiver is available.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
131 | Page
Then:
• Attempt direct feeding at the breast within the first hour.
• If unsuccessful, provide colostrum within the first hour of birth.
• Delay weighing and measuring the newborn for 60–90 minutes after birth, to allow
uninterrupted skin-to-skin contact.
Stop on that last one. Ask: ‘in your labour room, how many minutes after birth is the baby weighed?’
The answer is usually ‘immediately’. Ask why. There is no clinical reason. The scale can wait 90
minutes; the first skin-to-skin hour cannot be repeated.
Continuous KMC
• Continuation of immediate KMC — for BOTH stable and unstable babies.
• Provide skin-to-skin care for at least 18 hours per day, with ZERO separation between mother
and baby.
• For stable babies ≥ 32 weeks, continue KMC in a step-down low-care ward where mother and
baby room together with the baby in the KMC position.
• The baby should be on enteral and oral feeds — direct breastfeeding with supplementary
expressed breast milk via cup and/or gastric tube.
Intermittent KMC
• Practise intermittent KMC when immediate continuous KMC is not possible in the NICU or
newborn care unit.
• The mother or other caregiver should provide skin-to-skin sessions of at least 60–70 minutes or
longer, as many times as possible each day.
• Sessions should coincide with feeding times, with the baby in the skin-to-skin position when
receiving gastric tube feeding.
FACILITATOR HINT
intermittent KMC is the fallback, not the plan. If your unit does 60 minutes twice a day and calls
it KMC, you are doing the fallback. Name that honestly — then ask what would have to change
to reach 18 hours.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
132 | Page

Equipment and Supplies
Use available, affordable equipment and supplies to help mothers stay more comfortable during
KMC.
Item Recommendation
Beds A comfortable bed for each mother if continuous KMC space is
available. An adjustable bed is not necessary; two pillows allow a
semi-recumbent position.
Chairs Comfortable chairs with armrests facilitate good posture during
breastfeeding. A chair next to the mother's bed is desirable. Chairs
should have wipeable fabric for infection control.
Privacy Foldable mobile medical privacy screens with solid or washable
surfaces are practical. Traditional curtains may have infection
prevention challenges.
Feeding supplies Feeding tubes, syringes, feeding cups.
Monitoring equipment Thermometer, baby weighing scales.

Item Recommendation
Resuscitation
equipment
Basic resuscitation equipment, oxygen where possible.
Medications Drugs for preventing and treating frequent problems of preterm
newborns may be added according to local protocols.
Walk the group around your own unit with this list in hand. Ten minutes. Tick what you have. The
point is not to shame the unit — it is to notice that the entire list costs less than one incubator, and
that two pillows and a chair with armrests are on it.
Demonstration: Practice KMC: Positioning
Demonstrate First — Slowly, Naming Every Step
Step Action
1 Place the baby in the fetal position (flexed arms and hips with slight hip abduction)
on the mother's or caregiver's bare chest, in an upright position.
2 Baby should only wear a hat and a nappy.
3 Turn the baby's head to one side in a 'sniffing' position, to keep the airway open.
4 Secure the baby to the mother's or caregiver's chest with a cloth or wrap.
5 The baby's head (at ear level) should be secured to prevent hyperextension or flexion
of the neck, and avoid obstructive apnoea.
6 The baby's stomach (diaphragm) must be able to move freely, to enable unrestricted
breathing.
7 Baby and parent may be covered with a shirt or blanket.
8 The parent may lie down if the baby is firmly secured.
The Two Steps That Kill — Emphasize Them Hard
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
134 | Page
Step 5: the head at EAR LEVEL, secured, neither hyperextended nor flexed. A flexed neck in a
preterm baby is obstructive apnoea. Step 6: the diaphragm must move FREELY. A wrap tied over
the abdomen is a baby who cannot breathe.
“Everything else on this list is comfort. These two are the airway. If you remember nothing else from
today: ear level, and let the belly move.”
Show the Different Wraps
• Demonstrate putting the baby in the KMC position and making use of a pouch, tari, mbeleko, or
whatever wrap is used locally.
• Discuss the different ways you can tie a baby in the skin-to-skin position.
• Emphasise the importance of continuous skin-to-skin KMC.
Bring at least two different wrap designs. Ask the group what mothers actually use at home in their
area — and practise with that.
Return Demonstration
• Divide participants into groups of 4–5. One facilitator monitors each group.
• Each participant must perform the full sequence on the manikin, or with a real mother-baby
pair where available.
• Each participant repeats until they perform it correctly.
• If a participant cannot get it, have them watch another participant do it — this usually shows
them what they are doing wrong.
• Coach those having difficulty. Ask those who did well to take over the facilitator's role.
Where you have willing mothers on the ward: let participants practise assisting real mothers to
put babies in the skin-to-skin position. Start with mothers who are already doing intermittent
KMC.

Feeding and Growth during KMC
The Feeding Order — Every Session, Every Time
Always start a feeding session by feeding DIRECTLY AT THE BREAST, then cup feeding, then
tube feeding — with the baby in the skin-to-skin position.
“Breast, then cup, then tube. In that order, every session. Not ‘he has a tube so we use the tube.’ The
tube is what finishes the feed — not what starts it. Every session begins at the breast, even if he only
manages a few sucks. That is how he learns.”
Feeding and Growth
Action Details
Feeding instructions Always start a feeding session by feeding directly at the breast, then
cup feeding, then tube feeding (with baby in skin-to-skin position)
Feeding frequency 3-hourly feeds × 8 feeds in 24 hours
Supplementary
feeding
Provide expressed breast milk via cup and/or tube
Weight monitoring Monitor daily and plot on the growth chart
Weight loss > 10% Baby must receive supplementary expressed breast milk via cup
and/or tube
Supplementary
volume
Calculate 40–100% of a single feed volume, depending on the baby's
ability to suckle and gestational age
Transition from tube
to cup
Slowly decrease tube milk volume and increase cup volume until the
baby can finish the full volume via cup. Only then remove the tube
Cup Feeding Technique — Demonstrate It
• The baby is in an upright or semi-upright position.
• The cup is held to the baby's lower lip.
• The baby LAPS the milk with the tongue.
Do NOT pour milk into the baby's mouth. Every participant cup-feeds the manikin before leaving
this session. Watch for the pour — it is the commonest error, it looks efficient, and it aspirates
babies. Make them hold the cup at the lip and wait.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
137 | Page
Transfer in the Skin-to-Skin Position
Transfer to a higher level of care in the skin-to-skin position whenever possible:
• From labour ward to newborn care unit or NICU.
• From NICU or newborn care unit to a lower level of care (intra-hospital or inter-hospital).
• A baby born at home should be transferred in the skin-to-skin position to a health facility.
Link this back to the transport session: the manual says the transport incubator must be prewarmed
and the baby must not leave cold. The mother IS a prewarmed transport incubator, and she never
runs out of battery.
Intra-Hospital Transfer
• Baby is secured in the skin-to-skin position; the mother may walk or be transferred in a
wheelchair, or another caregiver may carry the baby skin-to-skin.
• Mother and baby are accompanied by a healthcare worker.
• A summary of the mother's and baby's health history should be available.
Inter-Hospital Transfer
• Baby is transferred secured skin-to-skin to the mother or other caregiver, in an ambulance or an
ordinary motor vehicle.
• A healthcare worker accompanies the mother and baby.
• A summary of the mother's and baby's health history and follow-up arrangements should be
available.
Criteria for Down-Referral Transfer
Destination Criteria
Rural, locality, or secondary
general hospital with KMC-
trained staff
Gestational age ≥ 32 weeks · baby on enteral (gastric tube)
AND oral feeds (breast and cup) · mother healthy and skilled
in feeding
Primary Health Care (PHC)
unit with KMC-trained staff
Gestational age ≥ 35 weeks · baby on oral feeds (breast and
cup) · mother healthy and skilled in feeding
Notice that both rows require KMC-trained staff at the destination, and both require a mother who is
skilled in feeding. You are not transferring a baby — you are transferring a mother-baby pair, to
somewhere that knows what to do with them.

138 | Page
Discharge Criteria from KMC ward
Babies who have been receiving CONTINUOUS KMC can be discharged if these criteria are met:
Category Criteria
All small babies Able to breastfeed directly AND finish all expressed breast milk given
by cup. Normal vital signs and no danger signs. Note: almost all small
newborns need supplementary cup feeds until weight reaches 3 kg.
Babies ≤ 1.8 kg Regained birthweight or reached 1.8 kg · > 34 weeks gestational age ·
consistent weight gain for ≥ 3 days (average 20 g/day) · off gastric
tube and doing well on cup feeds.
Babies > 1.8 kg Late preterm (34 to < 37 weeks) must be observed for ≥ 72 hours to
establish feeding and KMC skills · weight loss < 10% of birthweight
(if > 10%, introduce cup feeding and keep until weight loss < 10% or
birthweight regained).
Mother Practises KMC consistently · has good breast milk supply · skilled in
direct breast- and cup-feeding · handles baby safely · recognizes
stress signs · knows how to calm the baby · comfortable as primary
caregiver · educated on danger signs and CPR.
Notice: there is a whole row for the MOTHER. She is a discharge criterion. A baby whose mother
cannot cup-feed is not ready for discharge, whatever his weight is doing.
The discharge criteria must be confirmed by the attending doctors. Nurses may assess readiness and
schedule follow-up visits.
Follow-Up Schedule
1 week · 2 weeks · 1 month · then monthly — until the baby reaches 3 kg or 40 weeks corrected
gestational age, or earlier if any concerns arise.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
139 | Page
Arrangements Before Discharge
• If the mother is very young or inexperienced, identify a supporting family member.
• Baby should be immunized, with an immunization document and a date for the next
immunization.
• Follow-up visits should be arranged and communicated effectively.
The mother should receive the following IN WRITING:
5. Prescription for multivitamins and iron supplementation for the baby.
6. Discharge date and weight, on the discharge documents or immunization card.
7. Feeding instructions for home.
8. Date and place of the first follow-up visit.
‘In writing’ is not a formality. She has just spent weeks being told things by tired people in a noisy
ward. Discuss the discharge plan with her and the family BEFORE the day of discharge — including
the expectation that the baby will usually be cared for in the KMC position at home until at least 40
weeks gestational age or 2.5–3 kg, or according to the baby's needs and cues.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
140 | Page
Case Scenarios : KMC
Read each case aloud. Give 8 minutes. Encourage discussion before correction. End each case
with the key message.
Case 1 — “He Is Too Small For That”
A baby is born at 30 weeks, 1.1 kg. He is breathing with grunting, on CPAP, and has an IV line.
At 40 minutes of age the mother asks whether she can hold him. The doctor says: “not yet — he
is only 1.1 kg and he is not stable. Maybe in a week, when he is bigger.”
Ask:
• Is the doctor right? Quote the protocol.
• What are the ONLY two reasons to delay skin-to-skin at birth?
• What has this baby lost in the last 40 minutes?
ANSWER KEY
The doctor is wrong on both counts. Weight is NOT a barrier to initiation. KMC is safe for
both stable and unstable babies. ‘When he is bigger’ is exactly backwards — the smallest, sickest
babies gain the most.
The only two exceptions: the baby is haemodynamically unstable or unable to breathe; or the
mother is critically ill and no substitute caregiver is available. He is breathing, on CPAP, with a
line. He does not meet either exception.
What he has lost: immediate KMC significantly reduces mortality, particularly within the first
72 hours. He is in the window right now, and he is spending it alone.
What should happen: skin-to-skin now, with the CPAP and the line arranged without tangles.
Attempt the breast; if not, colostrum within the first hour.
KEY MESSAGE
‘too small’ and ‘too sick’ are the two commonest reasons given for withholding KMC — and
they are the two strongest reasons for providing it.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
141 | Page
Case 2 — The 35-Week Baby Nobody Is Watching
A baby is born at 35 weeks, 2.3 kg. He is on the postnatal ward with his mother. He looks well.
He is dressed in clothes and lying in a cot beside her bed. He has fed twice in 12 hours and the
nurse notes he is “sleepy”. His temperature is 36.3°C.
Ask:
• Does this baby qualify for KMC?
• Why is nobody doing it?
• What are the two problems already visible?
ANSWER KEY
Yes. He is late preterm (34–36+6 weeks). The protocol says explicitly: KMC is equally important
for late preterm infants, who are often cared for in postnatal wards and may not receive adequate
skin-to-skin support. He is the textbook case.
Why nobody is doing it: because he looks fine. He is 2.3 kg, he is pink, he is on the postnatal
ward. He does not look like a KMC baby — which is precisely the problem the protocol is
warning about.
The two problems already visible: (1) temperature 36.3°C — below 36.5, that is a danger sign
the mother should have been taught to report. (2) ‘Sleepy’ and fed twice in 12 hours — poor
feeding is a danger sign, and he needs 8 feeds in 24 hours, 3-hourly.
What should happen: undress him to hat and nappy, skin-to-skin with his mother now. Start the
feeding sequence — breast, then cup. Teach her the four checks and the danger signs. Recheck
the temperature.
KEY MESSAGE
the late preterm on the postnatal ward is the baby this protocol was written for. He does not look
sick until he is.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
142 | Page
Case 3 — The Wrap Is Wrong
You walk into the KMC room. A mother is sitting with her 1.5 kg baby skin-to-skin. She has tied
the wrap firmly and neatly — it is snug across the baby's back and abdomen. The baby's chin is
resting down on his own chest. He is quiet. She is proud of how well she has secured him and
says he has not moved in an hour.
Ask:
• What do you see?
• Which two positioning steps have been broken?
• What do you say to her?
ANSWER KEY
Two errors, both dangerous: (1) The chin is down on the chest — the neck is FLEXED. Step 5:
the head must be secured at ear level, preventing hyperextension or flexion, to avoid obstructive
apnoea. (2) The wrap is snug across the abdomen — Step 6: the diaphragm must be able to move
freely to enable unrestricted breathing.
‘He has not moved in an hour’ is not reassurance. In this position, it is the finding.
What you say: not ‘you did it wrong’. She has done something hard and she has done most of it
well. Reposition WITH her — head to the side in the sniffing position, ear level, loosen over the
belly, watch the abdomen move together. Then have her show you. Praise the securing; fix the
airway.
The trap in this case: a participant who says ‘tell her to redo it’ and walks on. Coaching is done
with your hands next to hers, not from the doorway.
KEY MESSAGE
a neat wrap and a safe wrap are not the same thing. Ear level, and let the belly move.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
143 | Page
Case 4 — The Alarm at 2 a.m.
A mother in the KMC step-down ward wakes at 2 a.m. Her 1.6 kg baby, 34 weeks, feels limp and
she thinks he stopped breathing for a few seconds. She has been taught the danger signs. She is
frightened.
Ask:
• What should she do — exactly?
• Which danger signs are present?
• What is the one thing she should NOT do?
ANSWER KEY
Danger signs present: apnoea (any episode requiring stimulation, or > 20 seconds) and
lethargy/floppiness (baby feels limp). Two of the six.
What she does: call immediately for medical assistance WHILE KEEPING THE BABY IN
THE SKIN-TO-SKIN POSITION.
What she must NOT do: take him off her chest and carry him to find a nurse. That makes him
colder and less stable. The position stays unless he needs emergency resuscitation.
And note: she has been coached in CPR for apnoea or sudden collapse. That is in the skill
coaching row. If he needs stimulation, she can give it in the first seconds — which is where the
seconds matter.
Then the team: this is new apnoea in a preterm — it is a symptom, not a diagnosis. Exclude
sepsis, hypoglycaemia, temperature, anaemia, IVH, PDA.
KEY MESSAGE
teaching the mother the danger signs is not a formality on a discharge form. She is the monitor
for 18 hours a day. Teach her properly and she will find things before your machine does.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
144 | Page
Case 5 — Ready to Go?
A 1.9 kg baby, born at 34 weeks, now 10 days old. He has regained his birthweight and gained 20
g/day for the last 4 days. He is off the gastric tube and finishing his cup feeds. Vital signs normal,
no danger signs. He breastfeeds for a while then tires, and finishes by cup.
His mother is 17 years old. She practises KMC when reminded. She can breastfeed but has not
cup-fed him herself — the nurses do it. She has not been taught CPR. Her own mother lives three
hours away.
Ask:
• Does the BABY meet the discharge criteria?
• Does the PAIR meet the discharge criteria?
• What must happen before he goes?
ANSWER KEY
The baby: yes. ≤ 1.8 kg row — regained birthweight, > 34 weeks, consistent weight gain ≥ 3
days averaging 20 g/day, off the gastric tube and doing well on cup feeds. Able to breastfeed
directly and finish expressed milk by cup. Normal vitals, no danger signs. He is ready.
The pair: NO. Read the mother row. She must practise KMC CONSISTENTLY — she does it
when reminded. She must be skilled in direct breast- AND cup-feeding — she has never cup-fed
him. She must be educated on danger signs and CPR — she has not been. She is not a discharge
criterion that has been met.
What must happen: she cup-feeds him herself, repeatedly, until she is skilled. She is taught the
danger signs and CPR. She is taught the four checks. And — because she is very young —
identify a supporting family member. Her mother is three hours away; who else?
Before he goes she gets, in writing: the multivitamin and iron prescription, the discharge date
and weight on the documents or immunization card, the feeding instructions for home, and the
date and place of the first follow-up visit. He is immunized with a document and a next date.
Follow-up at 1 week, 2 weeks, 1 month, then monthly to 3 kg or 40 weeks corrected.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
145 | Page
The trap: every group discharges this baby. He looks perfect on the weight chart. Discharging
him is discharging a 17-year-old who has never fed her own baby, three hours from her mother,
into a home where he must stay in KMC until 2.5–3 kg.
KEY MESSAGE
discharge criteria focus on feeding competence and maternal readiness — NOT on weight alone.
Read the mother's row before you sign.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
