8.3 Feeding the Preterm Newborn
Learning Objectives
Knowledge
• State when to start enteral feeds and what trophic feeding is for
• State the advancement rates by birth weight
• State when to fortify, when to stop, and what to do when no fortifier exists
• State the vitamin, iron and mineral supplementation schedule
• Recognise the signs of feeding intolerance and grade the response
Skills
• Give a gravity bolus feed correctly — never by plunger
• Assess gastrointestinal and oral feeding readiness
• Measure and interpret abdominal girth
• Calculate a daily feed volume and an iron dose
Attitudes
• Treat the mother's milk supply as a clinical priority from hour one
• Stop an oral feed when the baby says stop
• Advance feeds by protocol, not by mood or by shift
Materials
• Manikin or doll · OG and NG tubes in preterm sizes · 60 mL syringes WITH removable
plungers · milk substitute (water)
• Tape measure for abdominal girth · weighing scale · Fenton growth charts · feeding charts
• Human milk fortifier sachet (or photo) · ferrous sulphate and ferrous gluconate bottles (labelled,
demonstration)
• Breast pump or hand-expression teaching aid · milk storage containers and labels · refrigerator
thermometer
• Calculators · the colour-graded intolerance table printed A3 · copies of all four checklists
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
147 | Page
Interactive session: Nutrition Is Treatment
Facilitator Script
“We give antibiotics on time because we know what happens if we do not. We give surfactant on
time for the same reason. But feeds get postponed — he is too small, he is on CPAP, we will start
tomorrow. Read the first line of this section: failure to meet the growth needs of a preterm baby
causes IRREVERSIBLE deficits. Not delayed. Irreversible.”
Open With a Question
• “In your unit, what usually delays the first feed of a preterm baby — honestly?”
Write every answer on the flip chart. The commonest are: he is on CPAP; he is too small; no one has
expressed yet; the doctor has not written it. Keep the list — you will answer each one during the
session.
The Goal
To achieve postnatal growth approximately that of a normal foetus of the same postmenstrual age.
Failure to do this results in IRREVERSIBLE DEFICITS and LONG-TERM SEQUELAE.
Supporting the Mother — The First Feeding Intervention
• Provide special breastfeeding support to mothers who have delivered a preterm newborn.
• Breast milk production relies on SUPPLY AND DEMAND. If the baby cannot feed directly,
frequent expression and complete emptying of the breasts are essential.
• Practice skin-to-skin position and kangaroo care.
• Provide early lactation counselling and maternal nutritional support.
“The milk supply is established in the first days, or it is not established at all. A mother whose baby
went to the nursery and who was not helped to express by hour six will struggle for weeks.
Expressing support is not comfort care — it is the manufacture of the medicine.”
Ask: ‘who in your unit teaches a mother to express, and when?’ If the answer is ‘the nurses, when
they have time’, that is a system finding. Put it on the barriers list.
Starting and Advancing Feeds
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
148 | Page
Facilitator Script
“Two numbers to hold: within 24 hours, and no later than day 4. Trophic feeds start within the
first 24 hours in a stable baby. Progression starts no later than day 4. If your unit routinely misses
those two, the rest of this session is academic.”
Trophic Feeding — Gut Medicine, Not Food
What it is Small volumes of enteral milk (10–24 mL/kg/day) to promote
development of the immature GI tract
What it is NOT It is NOT considered to contribute to nutrition
When to start Within the FIRST 24 HOURS of life in clinically stable infants
Review Evaluate feeding tolerance DAILY
When to progress No later than the FOURTH DAY of life, unless clinical reasons
prevent it
“It is not food and it is not meant to be. Its job is to keep the gut alive — the villi, the motility, the
hormones — while the TPN does the feeding. That is why the volume looks uselessly small. It is not
nutrition; it is exercise for the bowel.”
Advancement Rates
Birth weight Start Increase by
< 1 kg and high-risk 15–20 mL/kg/day 15–20 mL/kg/day. After 2–3
days tolerance, consider 30–40
mL/kg/day
≥ 1 kg 30 mL/kg/day 30 mL/kg/day
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
149 | Page
What to Feed
Facilitator Script
“Mother's milk first, always. But mature breast milk alone does not contain enough protein,
calcium or phosphorus to grow a 1200-gram baby at the rate the womb would have grown him.
That is not a criticism of breast milk — it is a statement about how extraordinary the third
trimester is.”
Human Milk — Why It Is First
• Reduces rates of life-threatening conditions (NEC, sepsis)
• Shortens hospital stay
• Improves long-term neurodevelopmental outcome
• Colostrum has a positive effect on immune function of preterm infants
Pumped breast milk should be REFRIGERATED rather than FROZEN — it retains more anti-
infective properties.
Storage Use within
Room temperature 4 hours
Refrigerated 5–7 days
Frozen 3–4 months
Cross-reference Patient Safety (Section 9): feeding a mother's expressed breast milk to the WRONG
newborn is one of the named misidentification errors. Every container is labelled with the baby's
identity and the time of expression — and checked against the baby, not the cot.
Substitutes
Special preterm formulas (22, 24, 26, 30 kcal/oz) may be used. Diluted milk, dextrose, and oral
rehydration solutions are NOT recommended.
Fortification
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
150 | Page
Question Answer
Why Mature breast milk lacks sufficient protein, calcium and
phosphorus for a < 1500 g baby to grow at intrauterine rates
Who Very preterm and VLBW infants fed on human milk
START when Tolerating at least 120 mL/kg/day
STOP when At least 2 kg, or established at breastfeeding and growing well
Longer May continue in nutritionally compromised infants
When There Is No Fortifier — The Sudan Reality
● Fortify expressed breast milk using newborn standard formula or premature formula.
● If nothing is available: increase expressed breast milk volume to 200 mL/kg/day in 8 feeds,
UNDER CLOSE MEDICAL SUPERVISION, to avoid fluid overload.
● Even 220 mL/kg/day may be considered IF PDA, congenital cardiac conditions and chronic
lung disease are EXCLUDED.
Before you write 200–220 mL/kg/day, actively exclude PDA, congenital cardiac disease and chronic
lung disease — and WRITE THE EXCLUSION IN THE NOTES. In those three conditions, high
volumes cause decompensation.

How to Feed: The Gravity Bolus
Facilitator Script
“This is the most important twenty minutes of the session and it is about a piece of plastic. The
plunger. If you push a feed into a preterm baby you can put milk in his lungs. Gravity is not the
slow option — it is the only option.”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
153 | Page
The Four Rules
1. Enteral feeds are given by gastric tube to deliver expressed breast milk to the stomach.
2. OROGASTRIC tubes are preferred in preterms with respiratory complications or on nasal
CPAP — nasogastric tubes interfere with ease of breathing.
3. All preterm babies should receive enteral feeds UPRIGHT IN THE KMC (SKIN-TO-SKIN)
POSITION, to improve absorption.
4. INTERMITTENT BOLUS feeds are preferred to continuous feeds.
Why bolus is preferred: more physiological — mimics normal feeding, promotes natural cycles of
gut hormone release (gastrin, insulin) and normal digestion; allows smaller stomach volumes at any
time, reducing reflux and associated apnoea or desaturation. Continuous feeding may be acceptable
in certain circumstances.
The Gravity Rule
The milk MUST RUN IN BY GRAVITY. Speed it up or slow it down by RAISING OR
LOWERING THE SYRINGE. IT IS DANGEROUS TO PUSH THE MILK USING THE
PLUNGER — pushing may lead to respiratory aspiration and severe complications.
“The plunger comes out and stays out. The barrel is a funnel. Your hand controls the height, and the
height controls the speed. If your thumb is on a plunger, stop — you are doing a different
procedure.”
Demonstration — Step by Step
● Wash hands. Confirm the baby's identity with two identifiers (cross-reference Patient
Safety).
● Check the milk: correct baby, correctly labelled, within storage time, at the right
temperature.
● Position the baby UPRIGHT in the KMC position on the mother's chest where possible.
● Confirm the tube position by your unit's method before every feed.
● REMOVE THE PLUNGER from the syringe. Attach the barrel to the tube.
● Pour the measured milk into the barrel.
● Hold the syringe so the milk runs in by GRAVITY. Raise to speed up, lower to slow down.
● Watch the BABY, not the syringe — colour, breathing, tone, comfort.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
154 | Page
● If the baby shows stress, LOWER the syringe or pause. Do not push.
● Allow the feed to finish. Flush as per unit protocol.
● Keep the baby upright afterwards.
● Document volume, time, tolerance and any observations.
Common Errors at This Station
• The plunger stays in ‘just to get it started’ — that is the error, and it always starts that way.
• Holding the syringe too high — the feed runs in fast, the baby regurgitates.
• Watching the syringe instead of the baby.
• Baby lying flat rather than upright in KMC position.
• NG tube in a baby on CPAP.
• Not confirming which baby the milk belongs to.
Interactive session: Readiness of feeding
Facilitator Script
“Two different readinesses, often confused. Gut readiness asks: can I put milk in the stomach?
Mouth readiness asks: can he take it himself? A baby can be ready for gavage on day one and not
ready for the breast for six weeks. Do not test the second when you mean the first.”
Gastrointestinal Readiness
READY NOT READY
SOFT, non-tender, non-distended abdomen —
the key indicator of GI tolerance and readiness
RIGID or DISTENDED abdomen with
PROFUSE VOMITING — signals intolerance
or obstruction
The Practice Change — Routine Gastric Residuals
Routine checks of gastric residuals OFTEN DO MORE HARM THAN GOOD in premature babies.
● Disrupts acid production — drawing out stomach contents stops natural hydrochloric acid
creation.
● Increases infection risk — without strong acid, dangerous bacteria grow and multiply.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
155 | Page
● Harms milk digestion — low acid deactivates pepsin, the enzyme that breaks down milk
proteins.
● Causes feeding problems — inactive enzymes plus bacterial overgrowth lead to worse
intolerance.
ONLY check GRV if there are CLEAR SIGNS of gastrointestinal pathology. Do NOT stop feeding
because of a GRV alone unless the baby ALSO shows bile-stained vomit, severe distension, bloody
stools, or systemic instability.
FACILITATOR HINT
this reverses what many participants were taught, and it differs from the Level II manual which
describes routine pre-feed checks. Name that openly. Then ask: does your feeding chart have a
pre-printed ‘aspirate’ column? If so, the chart is teaching the old practice every shift, and the
chart needs changing.
“The finding drives the test, not the timetable. You check residuals when the baby is telling you
something is wrong — not because it is four o'clock.”
Oral Feeding Readiness — The Three Domains
A baby must be able to MANAGE THEIR OWN SALIVA before oral feeding can be considered.
Domain Signs
Behavioural
(alertness)
Quiet alertness — awake, eyes open, engaged, not fussy · increased
activity of arms, legs, face · rooting · mouth movements: lip smacking,
opening and closing, tongue out
Motor and suckling Hand-to-mouth movements · gently flexed normal resting tone — not
limp, not rigid · STRONG non-nutritive suckle on a gloved finger
Physiological
stability
Stable breathing, usually 40–60/min, no increased effort or nasal flaring
· stable heart rate, no bradycardia, no desaturation
Feeding cues may be REDUCED OR ABSENT in sick or very preterm newborns. Their absence is
not proof of unreadiness — it means look harder at the other two domains.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
156 | Page
The Suck–Swallow–Breathe Pattern
Even with cues present, the baby is only ready if the body can coordinate SSB. Observe DURING
active feeding.
If a baby struggles to coordinate SSB, they are at risk of CHOKING or ASPIRATING milk into the
airways.
Category Incoordination signs during or after feeds
Stress cues Forehead furrowing, splayed fingers, watery eyes, turning away
Work of breathing Flaring nostrils, rapid breathing, chest retractions during breaks
Clinical distress Coughing, gagging, wheezing during the feed
Fatigue Falling asleep very quickly, or taking over 30 minutes for a minimal
amount of milk
STOP Signs — Not Ready
If the baby shows stress signals during a feeding attempt, STOP THE ORAL FEED ENTIRELY.
Colour changes — pale, bluish, greyish. Respiratory distress — gulping, coughing, choking, apnoea.
Agitation and fatigue — excessively floppy, immediate sleep, panicked strained fussing.
“Stopping a feed is not a failure. Pushing on through those signs is how a baby aspirates. The baby is
allowed to say no, and your job is to hear it the first time.”
Observation Practice
• Where you have consenting mothers on the ward: observe a real feed together, and have each
participant call out the domain signs as they see them.
• Where you do not: use the manikin and have one facilitator role-play the stress cues while
participants name them and decide to stop.

157 | Page
Interactive session: Feeding Intolerance and Monitoring Growth
Facilitator Script
“Every preterm baby will have a difficult feeding day. The skill is not avoiding intolerance — it
is grading it. Most of what frightens us needs the feed reduced. A small amount of it needs
everything stopped and an X-ray. Knowing which is which is this session.”
The Signs
Sign What counts as significant
Vomiting Small volume of altered milk is usually benign. Significant,
frequent, BILE- or BLOOD-stained must be assessed. Continued
BILE-STAINED vomiting may mean BOWEL OBSTRUCTION
OR ILEUS
Abdominal distension Significant if MORE THAN 2 cm compared with the previous
measurement, or with bilious residual or abdominal wall erythema.
Mild fullness < 2 cm after bolus feeds or on CPAP is not uncommon
Gastric residuals > 30% (50%) of the previous feed · blood or bile stained · with
systemic manifestations
Bloody stools Always significant
Systemic Thermal instability · apnoea · bradycardia · lethargy
‘More than 2 cm compared with the PREVIOUS measurement’ requires that a previous
measurement exists. MEASURE the girth — same point, same time in the feed cycle, every shift. An
estimated abdomen is not a measurement.
Demonstrate girth measurement on the manikin: mark the point, measure at the same phase of the
feed cycle, record it. Then have two participants measure the same manikin and compare — if they
differ by more than a few millimetres, discuss why technique matters.

“Notice that only the bottom row stops everything. The other three adjust. A unit that stops feeds

Too much phosphate, or too high a dose, causes HYPOCALCAEMIA WITH TETANY. Give it
divided with feeds — never as a single large volume, which also causes vomiting.
Ask: who screens phosphate at 3–4 weeks in your unit? Metabolic bone disease of prematurity is
invisible until the baby fractures. This one blood test, at one point in time, is the whole screening
programme.

Case 1 — The Feed That Was Never Started
A 1.1 kg baby, 29 weeks, now 4 days old. He is stable on CPAP. He has had IV fluids only since
birth. The notes say each day: “Nil by mouth — on CPAP.” His mother has not been shown how
to express and says her breasts are now soft and comfortable.
Ask:
• What has gone wrong, and on which day?
• Does CPAP prevent feeding?
• What has happened to the milk supply, and what do you do about it?
ANSWER KEY
What went wrong: trophic feeding should have started within the FIRST 24 HOURS in a stable
baby, and progression no later than DAY 4. He has had neither. Four days of gut rest in a 29-
weeker is four days of villous atrophy and lost time.
CPAP does not prevent feeding. It changes the TUBE — orogastric is preferred in preterms on
nasal CPAP, because nasogastric tubes interfere with ease of breathing. ‘Nil by mouth — on
CPAP’ is not a clinical decision, it is a habit.
The milk supply: ‘soft and comfortable’ on day 4 means the supply is failing. Breast milk
production relies on supply and demand; without frequent expression and complete emptying, it
stops. This is urgent — start intensive expression support today, every 2–3 hours including
overnight, with skin-to-skin.
What you do now: start trophic feeds via OG tube today; begin expression support immediately;
put him in KMC; document a daily feeding plan so it cannot drift again.
The trap: groups focus on the baby and miss the mother. The milk supply is the harder problem
and the one with the shorter window.
KEY MESSAGE
‘Nil by mouth’ written four days running is not a plan. Every day without a feeding decision is a
decision.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
161 | Page
Case 2 — The Feed That Was Pushed
A nurse is feeding a 1.4 kg baby by OG tube. The ward is busy. She attaches the syringe with the
plunger in, pushes the 18 mL feed in over about 30 seconds, and moves on. Ten minutes later the
baby is found desaturated with milk at the mouth.
Ask:
• What happened?
• What are the immediate actions?
• What do you say to the nurse — and what do you say to the unit?
ANSWER KEY
What happened: the feed was PUSHED. The manual is explicit — it is dangerous to push milk
using the plunger; it may lead to respiratory aspiration and severe complications. A rapid bolus
into a small stomach caused reflux and probable aspiration.
Immediate actions: position, suction the mouth, assess airway and breathing, oxygen and
support as needed, call for help. Stop feeds and assess. Consider chest X-ray. Watch for
aspiration pneumonia.
To the nurse: not blame — the manual says this happens under time pressure. Show her the
technique WITH the plunger out, hands together, and let her do it. She is more likely to change
with a demonstration than a reprimand.
To the unit: this is a system finding, not an individual one (cross-reference Patient Safety,
Swiss-cheese model). Why was the ward so busy? Are the syringes the right type? Is the gravity
rule in the written feeding policy? Is it taught at orientation?
The single most preventable feeding injury in any newborn unit. It takes 30 seconds to cause and
it is entirely avoided by removing a piece of plastic.
KEY MESSAGE
gravity, not pressure. The plunger stays out.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
162 | Page
Case 3 — The Residual
A 1.2 kg baby, day 9, on 100 mL/kg/day by OG tube and tolerating well. At the 6 a.m. feed the
nurse aspirates routinely and finds 4 mL of milky residual from a 15 mL feed. The abdomen is
soft, the baby is pink, active and afebrile. She withholds the feed and documents “feed
intolerance”.
Ask:
• Was the aspiration indicated?
• Is 4 mL from a 15 mL feed significant?
• Was withholding correct?
ANSWER KEY
The aspiration was NOT indicated. Routine gastric residual checks do more harm than good —
they disrupt acid production, increase infection risk, deactivate pepsin, and worsen intolerance.
Check GRV ONLY if there are clear signs of GI pathology. This baby had none.
The volume: 4 mL of 15 mL is about 27% — below the 30% threshold, not bile or blood stained,
with a benign abdominal exam. By the grading table this is LOW: continue feeding, monitor and
follow up.
Withholding was WRONG on both counts: the test should not have been done, and the result
did not meet the threshold for action. Do not stop feeding a baby because of a GRV unless the
baby also shows clear signs of illness — bile-stained vomit, severe distension, bloody stools, or
systemic instability.
The system question: why did she aspirate? Almost certainly because the feeding chart has an
‘aspirate’ column. The chart is teaching the old practice every shift. Change the chart.
Every group will debate the 4 mL. The more important answer is that the number should never
have been generated. The finding drives the test, not the timetable.
KEY MESSAGE
a missed feed is a real harm. Do not create a reason to miss one.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
163 | Page
Case 4 — No Fortifier
A 1.3 kg baby, 31 weeks, day 14, on full expressed breast milk at 150 mL/kg/day and tolerating
well. Weight gain is 8 g/day. Serum urea is 1.4 mmol/L. The unit has no commercial human milk
fortifier and none is expected.
Ask:
• What is the urea telling you?
• Is his weight gain adequate?
• What are your options, in order?
ANSWER KEY
The urea: 1.4 mmol/L is below 1.8 — which indicates TOO LITTLE PROTEIN in the diet. The
action stated in the manual is to consider increasing milk volume or fortifying feeds.
Weight gain: 8 g/day is below the preterm target of 15–20 g/day. The biochemistry and the
growth chart agree — he is underfed.
Options in order: (1) fortify EBM using newborn standard formula or premature formula; (2) if
nothing is available, increase EBM volume to 200 mL/kg/day divided into 8 feeds, under close
medical supervision to avoid fluid overload; (3) 220 mL/kg/day may be considered ONLY IF
PDA, congenital cardiac conditions and chronic lung disease are excluded.
Before increasing volume: examine for a murmur and signs of PDA, review the respiratory
history for chronic lung disease, and document the exclusion. And note the ceiling — do not
exceed 200 mL/kg/day without senior specialist consultation.
KEY MESSAGE
no fortifier is not no options. But the volume route has an explicit safety gate, and that gate is
three diagnoses you must actively exclude and write down.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
164 | Page
Case 5 — The Baby Who Said No
A 1.9 kg baby, 34 weeks, has been on tube feeds and is now being offered the breast. He roots
and latches. After two minutes he goes quiet, then pale; his nostrils flare and he coughs. The
mother is anxious and asks whether she should keep trying — “he was doing so well.”
Ask:
• What are you seeing?
• Do you continue?
• What do you say to the mother?
ANSWER KEY
What you are seeing: suck–swallow–breathe incoordination with STOP signs — colour change
(pale), increased work of breathing (nasal flaring), and clinical distress (coughing). He rooted and
latched, which are readiness CUES, but cues alone are not enough; the physiological stability is
what failed.
Do you continue? NO. If the baby shows stress signals during an attempt, STOP THE ORAL
FEED ATTEMPT ENTIRELY. Continuing risks choking and aspiration.
What you say to the mother: not ‘he failed’. He showed you exactly what he could manage
today and told you when to stop — which is a good sign, not a bad one. Complete this feed by
tube in the KMC position, keep offering non-nutritive suckling at the emptied breast, and try
again at the next feed. Reassure her the supply is protected by continuing to express.
The trap: ‘he was doing so well’ is the sentence that makes people push on. Rooting and latching
are the easy part; the coordination is what matures last.
KEY MESSAGE
stopping is not failure. The baby is allowed to say no, and hearing it the first time is the skill.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
165 | Page
9. Neonatal Jaundice
Learning Objectives
Knowledge
• Distinguish physiological from pathological jaundice
• State the red flags that make jaundice pathological
• Describe the causes, grouped by mechanism
• Explain why kernicterus is the outcome the whole session exists to prevent
Skills
• Assess jaundice visually under natural light
• Plot TSB against age in HOURS on a nomogram and act on the result
• Set up and monitor phototherapy safely
• State the indications for, and steps of, exchange transfusion
Attitudes
• Act on the 24-hour rule without waiting for a number
• Treat a rising bilirubin as time-critical
• Communicate clearly with the family about follow-up
Materials
• Printed phototherapy and exchange threshold charts — ENOUGH COPIES FOR EVERY PAIR
• Blank bilirubin plotting sheets · calculators · pens
• Phototherapy unit · eye covers · nappies · manikin or doll · thermometer
• Exchange transfusion kit (or photographs) · UVC set · syringes · three-way tap · blood-warming
demonstration
• 10% calcium gluconate ampoule (labelled, for demonstration) · cardiac monitor
• The colour-coded three-level chart, printed A3
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
166 | Page
Interactive session: Neonatal Jundice
Ask participant to open Chrtbooklet
Facilitator Script
“Sixty to eighty per cent of term babies go yellow. Almost all of them are fine. Your job in this
session is not to learn to spot jaundice — you can all do that. It is to learn to spot the ONE baby
in that yellow crowd who is on his way to permanent brain damage, and to spot him today rather
than tomorrow.”
Open With Questions
• “How many jaundiced babies did you see last month? How many did you treat?”
• “How did you decide which ones to treat?”
• “Has anyone here seen a baby with kernicterus?”
Let the last question sit. In most Sudanese units at least one person has. That memory is the
reason for this session, and it is worth naming before any teaching starts.
Teach the Physiology — Briefly
Three factors, all normal, all newborn:
1. Higher red blood cell mass with a shorter lifespan
2. Immature hepatic conjugation (UDP-glucuronyl transferase)
3. Enhanced enterohepatic circulation
“Every newborn is built to go a bit yellow. More red cells, dying faster, hitting a liver that has
never worked before. That is why 60–80% of them do it — it is not a disease, it is a design.”
The Central Distinction
PHYSIOLOGICAL PATHOLOGICAL
Onset AFTER 24 hours WITHIN 24 hours
Peak Day 3–5 Rises rapidly
Resolution By 1–2 weeks Lasts > 2 weeks
Level Modest Excessively high
What you do Monitor Investigate and treat
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
167 | Page
THE 24-HOUR RULE. Jaundice in the first 24 hours of life is NEVER physiological. Not
sometimes. Never. Any baby yellow on day one is a baby being investigated — that is the single
highest-yield sentence in this whole session.
The Red Flags
Write these on the flip chart. Then close the manual and ask the room to give them back to you.
1. Onset < 24 hours
2. Bilirubin rises > 5 mg/dL (85 µmol/L) per day
3. TSB > 17 mg/dL (290 µmol/L) in a term infant, OR > 15 mg/dL (255 µmol/L) in the first 48
hours
4. Jaundice lasting > 2 weeks (unconjugated) or > 3 weeks (conjugated)
5. Conjugated bilirubin > 20% of total
Run this as an oral drill in a circle, the way the ToT module describes: ask one participant, they
answer quickly, move to the next. Not a test — practice for speed and confidence. Five flags, twenty
participants, four times round.
Why It Matters — Say It Plainly
Severe unconjugated hyperbilirubinaemia crosses the blood-brain barrier and causes BILIRUBIN
ENCEPHALOPATHY or KERNICTERUS — preventable, potentially devastating, permanent.
“Preventable is the important word in that sentence. Not treatable — preventable. Which means
every case of kernicterus that walks out of a hospital is something a system failed to do, not
something a disease did.”
The Risk Factor Drill
Give the list. Then give the babies, and ask which risk factors apply.
Risk factors — primary level screening
Preterm (< 37 weeks) Bruising or cephalohaematoma
Low birth weight (< 2500 g) Poor feeding / dehydration
Sibling with neonatal jaundice G6PD deficiency
ABO / Rh incompatibility
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
168 | Page
Demonstration Nomograms: Plotting and Deciding
Facilitator Script
“A bilirubin number on its own means nothing. Fifteen at six hours old is an emergency. Fifteen
at five days old in a term baby may be nothing at all. The number only becomes information
when you plot it against the age in HOURS. This session is about that one habit.”
Before Anything — The Unit Trap
Some chart use µmol/L. 1 mg/dL = 17.1 µmol/L. A TSB of 17 mg/dL is 290 µmol/L. If you plot
‘17’ on a µmol/L chart, you will read it as safe when the baby needs urgent treatment.
• Ask every participant: what unit does YOUR laboratory report in? Have them say it out loud.
• Have them write the conversion at the top of their plotting sheet before they plot anything.
This is the highest-consequence, lowest-difficulty error in the entire session. Do not move on
until every person in the room has stated their own lab's unit.
The Two Charts
Chart Tells you
A. Phototherapy threshold When to START phototherapy — by gestational age, age in
hours, and TSB
B. Exchange transfusion
threshold
When urgent exchange is required. Immediate treatment if
rising > 8.5 µmol/L/hr (> 0.5 mg/dL/hr) or
approaching/exceeding the threshold
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
169 | Page
Practical Case: Neonatal Jaundice
Every participant plots all four, on paper, individually. Then compare in pairs before the facilitator
gives the answers.
Case 1 — Baby 1
Term, 38 weeks, 40 hours old. TSB 240 µmol/L. No risk factors.
Answer: Plot 240 µmol/L at 40 hours on the phototherapy threshold chart for ≥ 38 weeks with no
risk factors. This sits BELOW the phototherapy line. Action: no phototherapy; repeat TSB and
reassess. The teaching point — a number that sounds high is not high for this age.
Case 2 — Baby 2
Term, 38 weeks, 18 hours old. TSB 180 µmol/L.
Answer: The plot matters less than the CLOCK. This baby is jaundiced at 18 hours — under 24
hours — which is pathological by definition, whatever the chart says. Action: start phototherapy
per the chart AND investigate the cause (Coombs, blood groups, FBC, retics, G6PD). Every
group that only plots and does not notice the age has missed the point.
Case 3 — Baby 3
35 weeks, 30 hours old, TSB 220 µmol/L, Coombs positive, mother O negative.
Answer: 35 weeks WITH risk factors — use the lower (risk-factor) threshold line, not the term
line. Coombs positive means isoimmune haemolysis. Action: start phototherapy at the lower
threshold; recheck TSB in 6 hours; watch the RATE of rise; consider IVIG (500–1000 mg/kg
over 2 hours) if TSB keeps rising despite phototherapy; prepare for possible exchange.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
170 | Page
Case 4 — Baby 4
Term, 60 hours old. TSB was 280 µmol/L at 54 hours, now 340 µmol/L. Already on
phototherapy.
Answer: Calculate the RATE: 60 µmol/L in 6 hours = 10 µmol/L/hr. That exceeds 8.5 µmol/L/hr
(0.5 mg/dL/hr) DESPITE intensive phototherapy — an exchange transfusion indication. Action:
escalate to tertiary now; cross-match blood; do not wait for the next level to be crossed.
Baby 4 is the case that teaches the rate. Two numbers and a clock give you a slope, and the slope
decides before the threshold does. Any group that reports ‘340, still under exchange threshold,
continue phototherapy’ has missed the most important calculation in the session.
Demonstration: Phototherapy
Facilitator Script
“Phototherapy is the most effective treatment on this course that requires no drug, no needle and
no consent. It is also the one most often set up badly — baby dressed, lights too far, eyes
uncovered, and a family wondering why the number is not falling.”
How It Works — One Minute, No More
Light of specific wavelengths converts bilirubin in the skin into more water-soluble forms that
can be excreted without requiring hepatic conjugation. The mechanisms are
photoisomerization and structural isomerization.
“It bypasses the liver. That is the whole idea — the newborn's liver is the bottleneck, and the
light goes round it.”
The Five Device Types — Show Whatever You Have
Type What it is When
Conventional Fluorescent blue/white tubes, overhead Available and cheap; less
intense; longer exposure

Ask the room: what do you actually have in your unit? Then teach to that. If a unit has one
conventional overhead and a biliblanket, the useful lesson is that using BOTH together is double
phototherapy — which they may not have realised they can do.
Setting Up Phototherapy
● Confirm the indication against the nomogram, and document the TSB, the age in hours, and the
threshold.
● Explain to the mother what is happening and why — including that the baby will be undressed
and that she can still feed.
● Undress the baby to a nappy only — MAXIMUM SKIN EXPOSURE is the point.
● Apply EYE COVERS. Check they fit, are secure, and do not occlude the nostrils.
● Position the light source at the manufacturer's specified distance (see Module 2 Section 8.6 —
45 cm).
● Start the unit. Note the time.
● Ensure adequate hydration and feeding — phototherapy increases insensible water loss.
● Monitor temperature 2–4 hourly — hyperthermia AND hypothermia both occur.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
172 | Page
● Turn the baby regularly to expose all surfaces.
● Remove eye covers during feeds so the parents see the baby's eyes and bonding continues.
● Repeat TSB every 6–12 hours initially.
● Document: device type, start time, TSB trend, temperature, fluid status.

9.1 Exchange Transfusion
Facilitator Script
“This is the procedure nobody in this room wants to do, and the one that saves the brain when
everything else has failed. Most of you will do it rarely. Which is exactly why we walk through it
slowly now — because the night you need it, you will not have time to read.”
The Three Indications
● TSB ≥ threshold DESPITE intensive phototherapy — thresholds vary by age in hours,
gestation, and risk factors.
● Signs of ACUTE BILIRUBIN ENCEPHALOPATHY — lethargy, hypotonia, poor feeding,
high-pitched cry, opisthotonus, apnoea or seizures.
● SEVERE HAEMOLYSIS — rapidly rising bilirubin (≥ 0.5 mg/dL/hr or ≥ 8.5 µmol/L/hr)
despite intensive phototherapy, or haemolytic disease with anaemia plus high bilirubin.
Indication 2 has no number attached. If the baby shows signs of acute bilirubin encephalopathy,
EXCHANGE IS URGENT REGARDLESS OF BILIRUBIN LEVEL. The baby is the test result.
“A high-pitched cry and opisthotonus in a jaundiced baby is not a sign to document and recheck
in the morning. It is the brain being injured while you watch. That is a call-the-consultant-now
finding.”
Preparation — Walk It
● Confirm the indication.
● Obtain informed consent from parents.
● Arrange cross-matched, screened, FRESH blood (< 5 days old).
● CMV-negative, irradiated, leukocyte-depleted — usually O negative, Rh and ABO compatible
with infant AND mother.
● WARM the blood to 37 °C.
● Calculate the exchange volume: ≈ 2 × blood volume = 160–180 mL/kg (term blood volume ≈
80–90 mL/kg).
● Correct hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia and acidosis BEFORE starting.
Ensure aseptic technique, resuscitation equipment, and monitoring are ready.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
175 | Page
Worked Volume Calculation — Do It Together
Weight Blood volume (80–90
mL/kg)
Double volume
exchange
Aliquot size
1.5 kg (preterm) 120–135 mL 240–270 mL 2–5 mL
2.5 kg 200–225 mL 400–450 mL 5–10 mL
3.0 kg (term) 240–270 mL 480–540 mL 5–10 mL
3.5 kg (term) 280–315 mL 560–630 mL 5–10 mL
Have every participant calculate the 3 kg row independently before you show it. 160–180 mL/kg × 3
kg = 480–540 mL. Then ask how many aliquots of 10 mL that is (about 50) and how long at 1–2
minutes each. The arithmetic makes the 60–90 minute duration real.
Route, Rate and the Procedure
Parameter Specification
Route Umbilical venous catheter PREFERRED; or peripheral line +
arterial access
Volume Double volume, 160–180 mL/kg — replaces ~85% of the infant's
blood
Technique PUSH–PULL (manual syringe) or continuous (pump / automated
device)
Aliquot 5–10 mL term, 2–5 mL preterm — withdrawn slowly over 1–2
minutes
Replacement Equal volume of donor blood over 1–2 minutes
Total duration 60–90 minutes
The duration is bounded on BOTH sides. Too fast → haemodynamic instability. Too slow →
rebound bilirubin. Not a target to beat — a window to stay inside.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
176 | Page
During the Exchange
• Continuous cardiorespiratory monitoring.
• Frequent checks: glucose, calcium, electrolytes, haematocrit, bilirubin.
• Observe for arrhythmias, apnoea, bradycardia, hypotension, seizures.
CALCIUM: 10% calcium gluconate 1 mL/kg SLOW IV after every ~100 mL exchanged, WITH
CARDIAC MONITORING. Cross-reference the convulsions session — calcium gluconate requires
a monitor there too, for the same reason.
• Provide glucose support (5–10% dextrose infusion if needed).
After the Exchange
● Flush the line with saline to clear residual donor blood.
● Continue INTENSIVE PHOTOTHERAPY — rebound is common.
● Monitor vital signs, urine output, acid–base, electrolytes, calcium, glucose.
● Recheck bilirubin at 2 hours, then every 4–6 hours.
● A second exchange may be required if bilirubin rebounds.
● Treat the underlying cause — e.g. IVIG for isoimmune haemolysis.
● Document: total volume, aliquot size, duration, complications, blood details (type, donor unit,
expiry, irradiation).
● Watch for late complications: NEC, sepsis, portal vein thrombosis, infection.
Complications — Name Them All
Category Complications
Metabolic Hypocalcaemia · hypoglycaemia · acidosis · hyperkalaemia
Haematologic Thrombocytopenia · coagulopathy · anaemia · polycythaemia
Infectious Sepsis (blood product related)
Catheter-related Arrhythmias · embolism · perforation
Mortality < 1–2% in experienced centres — HIGHER in sick or preterm
infants
That mortality figure is why indication matters. This procedure has a real death rate. It is done
because kernicterus is worse — but it is never done casually, and never without the indication
written down.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
177 | Page
Cases study Neonatal jaundice
Case 1 — The Day-One Baby Who Looks Well
A term baby, 3.2 kg, normal delivery, is noticed to be slightly yellow at 20 hours of age. He is
feeding, active, afebrile. The midwife says: “He is only a bit yellow and he is feeding beautifully
— all babies go yellow, we will look again tomorrow.”
Ask:
• Is the midwife right?
• What does the timing tell you?
• What happens next — exactly?
ANSWER KEY
The midwife is wrong. Jaundice within the first 24 hours is NEVER physiological. ‘He looks
well’ is not reassurance — he looks well now, and bilirubin rises.
What happens next: measure TSB now and plot against age in hours. Investigate the cause —
blood group (mother and baby), Coombs, FBC and reticulocytes, G6PD. Start phototherapy per
the nomogram. Do not wait for tomorrow.
The thinking error: using the baby's appearance instead of the clock. The 24-hour rule is a rule
precisely because well-looking babies with early jaundice are the ones who get missed.
KEY MESSAGE
in the first 24 hours, the clock overrules the eye.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
178 | Page
Case 2 — The Referral That Did Not Happen
A 36-week baby, 2.2 kg, born at a health centre. Day 3. Deep jaundice including the palms and
soles. Feeding poorly, sleepy. The health centre has no bilirubinometer. The nearest district
hospital is two hours away and the family has no transport money.
Ask:
• Which referral criteria are met?
• What can the health centre do while arranging transfer?
• What must be communicated to the district hospital?
ANSWER KEY
Referral criteria met — FOUR: deep jaundice especially palms and soles; poor feeding and
lethargy; preterm; low birth weight. Any ONE of these was enough.
While arranging transfer: keep the baby warm; support feeding/hydration; document the age in
HOURS and the time jaundice was first seen; do not delay transfer to ‘observe’.
Communicate: age in hours, when jaundice was first noticed, feeding history, risk factors
(preterm, LBW), and that no TSB is available. Use SBAR (cross-reference Patient Safety).
The real issue: ‘no transport money’ is a clinical problem here, not a social one. This is the
Sudan-specific delay the manual names. Ask the group how their own units solve it — and
whether anyone has a written referral-support pathway.
KEY MESSAGE
at primary level you cannot measure bilirubin — but you can measure the clock, the feeding and
the palms. All three said refer, on day one of noticing.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
179 | Page
Case 3 — The Rate, Not the Number
A 38-week baby on intensive phototherapy since 48 hours. At 54 hours TSB was 280 µmol/L. At
60 hours it is 340 µmol/L. The registrar says: “Still under the exchange line — keep the lights on
and recheck in the morning.”
Ask:
• Calculate the rate of rise.
• Is the registrar right?
• What do you do now?
ANSWER KEY
The rate: 340 − 280 = 60 µmol/L over 6 hours = 10 µmol/L per hour.
The registrar is wrong. A rise of ≥ 8.5 µmol/L/hr (≥ 0.5 mg/dL/hr) DESPITE intensive
phototherapy is itself an exchange transfusion indication. Ten exceeds 8.5. He does not need to
cross the line — the slope has already answered.
What you do now: escalate to tertiary; cross-match fresh, irradiated, leukocyte-depleted blood;
continue intensive phototherapy meanwhile; check for haemolysis (Coombs, G6PD) and consider
IVIG if isoimmune; correct glucose, calcium and acidosis in preparation.
The trap: ‘still under the line’ is the most dangerous sentence in jaundice management. The
threshold is where you must act; the slope is where you should act.
KEY MESSAGE
two numbers and a clock give you a slope. The slope decides before the threshold does.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
180 | Page
Case 4 — The Baby Who Is Telling You
A 4-day-old term baby, deeply jaundiced, brought from home. He is lethargic, will not feed, has a
high-pitched cry, and arches backwards when handled. The laboratory is closed and no TSB is
available until morning.
Ask:
• What are you looking at?
• Does the absence of a bilirubin level change anything?
• What is the immediate management?
ANSWER KEY
This is ACUTE BILIRUBIN ENCEPHALOPATHY. Lethargy, poor feeding, high-pitched cry
and opisthotonus are the named signs. This is bilirubin in the brain, now.
The missing TSB changes NOTHING. Exchange transfusion is urgent REGARDLESS OF
BILIRUBIN LEVEL when there are signs of acute bilirubin encephalopathy. Waiting for a
number until morning is waiting while the injury completes.
Immediate management: intensive phototherapy immediately, while everything else is
arranged. Call the consultant. Arrange urgent cross-matched blood. Establish access (UVC
preferred). Correct hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia and acidosis. Prepare for exchange. Treat any
underlying cause — sepsis screen, Coombs, G6PD.
Also: this baby needs long-term follow-up — hearing test and neurodevelopmental assessment
— whatever happens tonight.
Every group will ask about the bilirubin level. That question is the teaching moment: the baby IS
the result. Signs of encephalopathy authorise action on their own.
KEY MESSAGE
when the brain is already showing you the diagnosis, the laboratory is not the decision-maker.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

10. Neonatal Sepsis Surveillance System
Facilitator Script
“Eight babies in three weeks. Two died. Every one of you has lived through a run like that — and
every one of you knows what happened next: nothing. Because nobody counted. Surveillance is
not a form. Surveillance is the difference between eight tragedies and one outbreak you can
stop.”
10.1 Surveillance system
A surveillance system for neonatal sepsis is essential to monitor and prevent healthcare-associated
infections and to ensure the health and safety of newborns.
Aim: to systematically detect, report and analyze cases of neonatal sepsis in order to guide
prevention, control and quality improvement interventions, and to contribute to reduction of neonatal
morbidity and mortality.
Scope: this system surveys sepsis (blood stream infection) among preterm and sick neonates
admitted to Sudanese paediatrics and obstetrics hospitals' NICUs. It targets ALL neonates born
inside or outside hospital, whatever their clinical cause of admission. It works by identifying cases
clinically or by laboratory confirmation, collecting appropriate clinical samples (blood, CSF, swab).
The Six Objectives — Teach in This Order
1. Monitor the incidence and prevalence of neonatal sepsis.
2. Identify risk factors associated with neonatal sepsis.
3. Detect antimicrobial sensitivity and resistance patterns.
4. Evaluate the effectiveness of IPC interventions.
5. Provide data for benchmarking and quality improvement in neonatal care.
6. Facilitate timely response to outbreaks or clusters of neonatal infections.
FACILITATOR HINT
put these six on the flip chart and leave them up ALL DAY. Every session that follows serves
one of them. Blood culture technique serves objective 3. Hand hygiene serves objective 4.
Stewardship serves objective 3. At the end of the day, point back.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
183 | Page
Why Objective 3 Is the One That Bites
“Detect antimicrobial sensitivity and resistance patterns. Which of you can tell me, right now, what
your unit’s Klebsiella is sensitive to? Not what the textbook says — what YOUR unit’s isolates said
last quarter. If nobody can answer, then every empirical antibiotic you start is a guess, and
stewardship is impossible. Surveillance is what makes stewardship possible. That is why it comes
first today.”
Role Play — The Surveillance Process
Scenario: over three weeks, eight babies in your unit were treated for sepsis and two died.
Roles:
● NICU nurse in-charge
● Medical officer
● Laboratory focal person
● Infection control focal person
The team demonstrates the surveillance workflow: what data are collected · how cases are
detected and reported · how laboratory samples (blood, CSF, swab) contribute to confirmation ·
how findings guide IPC actions and quality improvement.
Facilitator Prompts — Ask These If the Team Stalls
● “Who noticed it was eight and not three? How?”
● “Were all eight cultured? If not, what are they in your data — clinical sepsis, or nothing?”
● “What grew? Was it the SAME organism? How would you know?”
● “Lab focal person: how long did the results take to come back, and to whom?”
● “IPC focal person: what was your first action — and what was your first action based
on?”
● “Who outside this unit was told?”
A N S W E R K E Y What a Good Team Produces
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
184 | Page
Objective What the team should have shown
1 — Incidence They counted. Eight cases and two deaths in three weeks is a NUMBER, and
it is compared against something
2 — Risk factors They looked for what the eight shared — gestation, weight, lines, ventilation,
day of onset, which nurse, which incubator
3 — Sensitivity Cultures were taken BEFORE antibiotics; isolates and sensitivities were
collated, not just filed
4 — IPC
effectiveness
They asked what changed before the run started, and what they will change
now — and how they will know if it worked
5 —
Benchmarking
Data go somewhere. To the hospital, to the state, and back to the unit
6 — Outbreak
response
Same organism in a cluster = outbreak until proved otherwise. Report to the
hospital infection control committee to search for cases in other departments
Close the role play by linking each of the group's outputs back to a numbered objective on the flip
chart.
Common Errors in This Session
● The team investigates the two deaths and ignores the six survivors — the survivors are the
data.
● Nobody asks whether the eight had cultures taken before antibiotics.
● The group blames a nurse. Errors result from multiple system failures; individual blame is
insufficient — link this to the Swiss-Cheese model in Section 9.
● They design a beautiful form nobody will fill in at 3 a.m.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
185 | Page
10.2 Case Definitions and Classification
Facilitator Script
“Four definitions. They exist because ‘the baby looks septic’ is not data. If your unit and mine
both say ‘we had eight cases’ but we mean different things, neither number means anything, and
nobody can compare us, help us, or fund us.”
Suspected Sepsis
WHO defines suspected sepsis in a hospitalized infant aged 0–59 days as the presence of ONE
OR MORE of the following clinical signs:
1. Not feeding well or not able to feed at all 7. Fast breathing (≥ 60 breaths per minute) in infants
aged 0–6 days
2. Movement only when stimulated or no
movement at all
8. Drowsiness or unconsciousness
3. High body temperature (≥ 38 °C) 9. Grunting
4. Low body temperature (< 35.5 °C) 10. Central cyanosis
5. Severe chest indrawing 11. Severe jaundice
6. Convulsions 12. Severe abdominal distention
ONE sign is enough. This is the lowest bar of the four definitions, and deliberately so — it is a
trigger to act, not a diagnosis.
Maternal risk factors also suggest sepsis:
● PROM > 18 hours and mother not given antibiotics 4 or more hours before labour
● Positive GBS screening
● History of fever more than 38 degrees, or offensive vaginal discharge
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
186 | Page
Practical Case Drill — With Answer Key
Case A: A day-2 newborn who is not feeding well, RR 66/min with grunting.
Answer: SUSPECTED SEPSIS. Three criteria present — (1) not feeding well; (7) fast breathing
≥ 60/min in an infant aged 0–6 days; (9) grunting. Only one was needed.
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION
Case B: A day-1 newborn with mild respiratory distress whose mother had PROM > 18 hours
and did not receive antibiotics 4 or more hours before labour.
Answer: SUSPECTED SEPSIS. The maternal risk factor is explicitly listed as suggesting sepsis, and
the baby has respiratory distress. Do not wait for him to look worse — that is what ‘suspected’
means.
FACILITATOR HINT
after Case B, ask ‘how many of you would have started antibiotics on this baby?’ Then hold that.
You will come back to it in stewardship this afternoon. Suspected sepsis triggers investigation
and treatment — it does NOT commit you to ten days.
Clinical Sepsis — No Positive Culture But Meets Criteria
All THREE must be present:
● Absence of positive microbiological blood and/or cerebrospinal fluid culture, AND
● Treatment with FIVE or more days of intravenous antibiotics was initiated, AND
● Patient has at least TWO of the following clinical or laboratory features of generalized
infection:
Category Features
CNS Convulsion, bulging fontanel, irritability, lethargy, apathy
GIT Refusal of feeding, feeding intolerance, abdominal distension or ileus
Temperature Unexplained instability, or fever (> 38 °C), or hypothermia (< 36.5 °C)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
187 | Page
Category Features
Heart rate Unexplained tachycardia (> 160/min) or bradycardia episodes (< 100/min)
Perfusion Capillary refill time > 3 seconds, or skin mottling
Respiratory New or more frequent episodes of apnoea (> 20 s)
Local Local infection — umbilical discharge, eye discharge
Laboratory (at
least one)
Platelets < 100 × 10³/µL · WBC < 4 × 10³/µL or > 20 × 10³/µL · CRP > 1
mg/dL · hyperglycaemia > 140 mg/dL or hypoglycaemia < 46 mg/dL
Group Exercise — clinical sepsis
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION
Scenario: A preterm baby has no positive culture, has received IV antibiotics for 6 days, and has
lethargy plus feeding intolerance. CRP is > 1 mg/dL.
Answer: YES — this meets the definition of clinical sepsis. Map it: (1) no positive culture ✓ (2) five
or more days of IV antibiotics — he has had six ✓ (3) at least TWO features — he has three: CNS
(lethargy), GIT (feeding intolerance), and a laboratory finding (CRP > 1 mg/dL) ✓
FACILITATOR HINT
notice what criterion 2 does. ‘Five or more days of IV antibiotics’ is not a clinical feature of the
baby — it is a feature of YOUR decision. A baby only becomes ‘clinical sepsis’ because
somebody kept treating him. Ask the group what that means for the numbers. It means this
category counts your uncertainty, not just his illness — and it is exactly the category stewardship
is trying to shrink.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
188 | Page
Laboratory Confirmed Sepsis — Recognized Pathogen
A recognized pathogen is recovered from a blood and/or cerebrospinal fluid culture. ONE culture is
enough.
Gram-POSITIVE bacteria Gram-NEGATIVE bacteria
Staphylococcus aureus (including MRSA) Escherichia coli — most common in early- and late-
onset neonatal sepsis
Group B Streptococcus (GBS) Klebsiella pneumoniae
Streptococcus pneumoniae Enterobacter cloacae
Enterococcus faecalis / faecium (including
VRE)
Pseudomonas aeruginosa
Beta-haemolytic streptococci (Groups A, C,
F, G)
Serratia marcescens
Listeria monocytogenes — especially in
early-onset sepsis
Acinetobacter baumannii
Citrobacter spp.
Proteus spp.
“One culture. One organism from that list. That is all it takes — because none of these belong in a
newborn’s blood, ever. Contrast that with the next definition, and you will understand why.”
Laboratory Confirmed Sepsis — Common Commensals
If blood cultures show growth of one or multiple common commensals (e.g. coagulase-negative
staphylococci), BOTH are required:
● A common commensal is recovered from TWO blood culture and/or CSF culture specimens,
AND
● Patient has at least TWO of the clinical or laboratory features of generalized infection (the
same list as 10.3).
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
189 | Page
TWO cultures. Not one. A commensal in one bottle is your skin, not his blood — unless you have a
second bottle and a sick baby to prove otherwise.
Case Discussion — sepsis by common commensal
Case 1: One blood culture grows a common commensal. There is no second culture.
Answer: NO — criteria are NOT met. The definition requires the commensal from TWO
specimens. With one bottle you cannot distinguish infection from contamination at the moment
of collection. This is why blood culture technique this afternoon matters: a re-palpated site
produces exactly this result, and this argument.
Case 2: Two cultures grow the commensal, and the baby has temperature instability
plus apnoea.
Answer: YES — criteria are met. Two specimens ✓ and at least two clinical features —
unexplained temperature instability, and new/more frequent apnoea > 20 s ✓
FACILITATOR HINT
this pair is the best teaching moment in the whole section. Same organism, same lab, different
answer — and the difference is entirely in how the sample was taken and whether anyone looked
at the baby. Say that out loud.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

10.3 Blood Culture Collection Technique
Facilitator Script
“Everything you did this morning — the four definitions, the surveillance objectives, the
resistance patterns — all of it rests on this one skill. A badly taken culture does not just fail this
baby. It puts a false organism into your unit’s resistance data, and that wrong number will change
what the next hundred babies are prescribed.”
The Technique
Prepare the patient
● Verify identity.
● Calm the neonate.
● Position for access — peripheral or umbilical vein.
● Clean the site with 70% alcohol — 30-SECOND SCRUB, then AIR DRY.
Confirm indication
● Suspected neonatal sepsis. No absolute contraindication; avoid sites with oedema or
infection.
Prepare materials
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
191 | Page
● Sterile gloves · 70% alcohol or chlorhexidine · sterile syringes and needles (23–25G) ·
aerobic blood culture bottles · labels · transport bag
Collect
● Use appropriate aseptic technique and follow IPC protocols — wear gloves, and DO NOT
RE-PALPATE the cleaned site.
● Collect the sample in sterile, labelled containers.
● BLOOD VOLUME: 1–2 mL per bottle — critical for detection.
● CSF VOLUME: 1 mL per bottle — critical for detection.
● If the baby is ALREADY receiving antibiotics, take the blood culture immediately BEFORE
the next dose.
Label, send, record
● Label with the neonate's name, file ID, date/time, collector's name and sample type.
● Complete the culture request form and send to the public lab using the standard transferee
procedure.
Record the sample collection in the main case record registry.

Demonstration and Return Demonstration
● Demonstrate the full technique ONCE with narration.
● Repeat SLOWLY, highlighting the common errors above as you deliberately nearly-make
them.
● Participants then perform return demonstration in PAIRS while facilitators observe using
the checklist.
Facilitator trick: on your slow repeat, clean the site properly, then casually re-palpate it with a
gloved finger while talking about something else. See who stops you. Whoever does gets to run
the next station.
Role Play — Filling Forms
● Roles: nurse collecting the sample · doctor ordering · laboratory receiver.
● Scenario: a suspected sepsis case.
● Participants complete the blood culture request form and record the collection in the main
case record registry.
The facilitator reviews for: identity accuracy · date and time · sample type · legibility — and all
identifiers and timestamps complete.
“Laboratory receiver: you are allowed to reject this sample. Say why. That is your job, and doing
it politely at the hatch saves a baby ten days of the wrong antibiotic.”
Other Investigation
There is NO role for CRP measurement in the golden hour. The half-life of CRP is 24 to 48 hours
and it takes around 10 to 12 hours for the level to increase — making CRP in the golden hour
unreliable.
Lumbar puncture for CSF culture, biochemical and microbiological analysis is usually NOT
recommended as a routine investigation for early-onset sepsis screening. It should be done in:
● Neonates whose blood culture is positive
● Infants with a strong clinical suspicion of sepsis
● Neonates presenting with seizure activity, apnoea, or depressed sensorium

10.4 Management of sepsis
Facilitator Script
“This morning you learned to find sepsis. Now the harder half: how to treat it without creating
the organism that kills the next baby. Every antibiotic you start is a decision. Every antibiotic you
fail to STOP is also a decision — and it is the one nobody remembers making.”
The Core Regimen and Decision Pathway
● START: Ampicillin + Gentamicin for 3 days awaiting culture results.
● If NO GROWTH, repeat CRP/PCT: if negative and clinically stable, STOP ANTIBIOTICS.
● Otherwise continue: ampicillin IM/IV 50 mg/kg every 12 hours in the first week of life, and
every 8 hours after the first week, for a total of at least 10 days; PLUS gentamicin IM/IV 5
mg/kg once a day in the first week, and 7.5 mg/kg once a day after the first week, for a total
of at least 10 days.
● If CULTURE POSITIVE, continue as indicated for at least 10 days.
● Alternative: third-generation cephalosporins for a total of 2–17 days — but no or trivial
difference.
Step 2 is the whole of stewardship in one line. NO GROWTH + negative CRP/PCT + clinically
stable = STOP. Not ‘finish the course’. Not ‘just to be safe’. STOP
The Dose Table — Read It Twice
Drug First week of life After first week Duration
Ampicillin 50 mg/kg every 12 hours 50 mg/kg every 8 hours At least 10 days
Gentamicin 5 mg/kg once a day 7.5 mg/kg once a day At least 10 days
FACILITATOR HINT
note what changes with age. Ampicillin changes its FREQUENCY (12 → 8 hourly), same dose.
Gentamicin changes its DOSE (5 → 7.5 mg/kg), same frequency. Participants reliably get this
backwards. Say it twice, and put it on the flip chart.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
195 | Page
Other Management Points
● Fungal prophylaxis: in very preterm or VLBW infants, fluconazole prophylaxis within the
golden hour — provided the incidence of fungal sepsis in the unit is significant (> 5% at
baseline; European guidelines suggest 2% as the threshold).
● Congenital infection at time of birth (viral infection like TORCH) should be treated promptly
during the golden hour of life.
“Read the fluconazole line carefully. ‘Provided the incidence in the neonatal care unit is significant.’
That is a surveillance question. You cannot answer it without this morning’s session. Stewardship
without surveillance is just guessing with extra steps.”
10.5 Antibiotic Stewardship
Antibiotic stewardship limits the development of antimicrobial-resistant organisms, and it can be
done by improving the use of antibiotics. The components:
● Use NARROW-SPECTRUM antibiotics.
● Have FIXED PROTOCOLS for starting AND stopping antibiotics.
● Start antibiotics ONLY WHEN CLINICALLY INDICATED.
● DOWNGRADE antibiotics after seeing the blood culture sensitivity pattern.
● Consider LOCAL antibiotic resistance patterns.
There is high risk of development of antibiotic resistance with the widespread use of antibiotics —
thus making careful and selective use of antibiotics to the highest-risk patients a UNIVERSAL
GOAL.
The Five Components, Turned Into Five Questions
Put these on a card. They are the ward round.
Component The question to ask at the cot
Only when indicated “Why did we START this? Is that reason still true?”
Fixed protocols for stopping “What is the STOP date? Who set it? Is it written?”
Downgrade after sensitivity “The culture is back. Have we narrowed?”
Narrow spectrum “Is this the narrowest drug that covers what we have?”
Local resistance patterns “What does OUR unit's data say about this organism?”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
196 | Page
“Notice that four of those five questions are about STOPPING or NARROWING, and only one is
about starting. That ratio is not an accident. Starting an antibiotic takes ten seconds and nobody is
ever criticised for it. Stopping one takes courage, and this is the room where we practise it.”
Practical Session — Dose Calculation and Double-Check
● Provide three newborn weights and two ages (within the first week, and after).
● Participants calculate ampicillin and gentamicin dose and interval using the manual schedule.
● Run a DOUBLE-CHECK routine: a second participant independently verifies the calculation
before ‘administration’.
● The facilitator corrects calculation AND INTERVAL errors immediately.
Weight Age Ampicillin Gentamicin
1.2 kg Day 3 60 mg every 12 hours 6 mg once a day
1.2 kg Day 10 60 mg every 8 hours 9 mg once a day
2.5 kg Day 3 125 mg every 12 hours 12.5 mg once a day
2.5 kg Day 10 125 mg every 8 hours 18.75 mg once a day
3.4 kg Day 3 170 mg every 12 hours 17 mg once a day
3.4 kg Day 10 170 mg every 8 hours 25.5 mg once a day
The commonest error is not the arithmetic — it is the interval. A participant who gives the right
milligrams at the wrong frequency has made a bigger error than one who is 10% out on the dose.
Score the interval.
Specific Case Management
● Use one scenario for each category in the manual table.
● Participants select the regimen DIRECTLY from the manual table without modification.
This is deliberate. The exercise is finding the right row, not inventing a regimen. Improvisation is the
opposite of a fixed protocol.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
197 | Page
Appropriate Action According to Culture Isolate
Teach the organism-linked actions exactly as listed in the manual:
Isolate Action
Resistant
organism
Shift antibiotic per sensitivity · ISOLATE the neonate · assign ONE nurse and
DEDICATE equipment · trace the source of infection — mostly transmitted by
DIRECT CONTACT · monitor and improve adherence to hand hygiene · monitor and
improve environment and surface disinfection · REPORT to the hospital infection
control committee to search for cases in other departments
Fungi ALARMING ORGANISMS · shift to antifungal treatment · isolate the neonate ·
assign one nurse and dedicate equipment · use CHLORHEXIDINE as hand rub agent
fungi ‘alarming organisms’ and changes your hand rub. That is the only place in this entire manual
where the agent itself changes. Ask the group why — alcohol is unreliable against some fungal
spores. If you grow a fungus, your hand rub has been failing.
Tabletop Exercise
● Give each group an isolate.
● They choose actions and explain HOW they would implement isolation and dedicated
equipment IN THEIR UNIT.
“Do not tell me what the manual says. Tell me where in YOUR unit the isolated baby goes. Which
incubator. Which nurse, given tonight’s rota. Which stethoscope. If you cannot answer, you have not
implemented isolation — you have written it down.”
The Stewardship Ward Round
Run it as the last thing before the tea break, when everyone is tired — which is when real ward
rounds happen.
Four beds. Each group gets all four. Five minutes per bed. Every bed gets the five questions and a
decision: CONTINUE · NARROW · STOP.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
198 | Page
Bed 1
Day 4. Started on ampicillin + gentamicin day 1 for suspected sepsis (PROM > 18 hours, mother
no antibiotics). Blood culture: NO GROWTH at 72 hours. CRP negative. Baby is feeding,
afebrile, active.
Answer: STOP. This is step 2 of the pathway, exactly. No growth + negative CRP + clinically
stable = stop antibiotics. Every group will find a reason to continue. Make them read step 2
aloud.
Bed 2
Day 6. Blood culture grew E. coli, sensitive to ampicillin AND to gentamicin AND to
ceftriaxone. Currently on ceftriaxone, started by the night team ‘because he looked bad’.
Answer: NARROW. Downgrade after seeing the sensitivity pattern; use narrow-spectrum
antibiotics. He goes back to ampicillin + gentamicin, and completes at least 10 days. Continuing
the cephalosporin because it is working is the single commonest stewardship failure in any unit.
Bed 3
Day 8. No culture was ever taken — the bottles had run out. On ampicillin + gentamicin since
day 1. Baby has had feeding intolerance and one apnoea. CRP 2 mg/dL.
Answer: This baby is now CLINICAL SEPSIS by definition (no positive culture; ≥ 5 days IV
antibiotics; ≥ 2 features — GIT, apnoea, CRP). Continue to at least 10 days. But the real answer
is upstream: he has no culture because the bottles ran out, which means he will be treated blind
for 10 days and contribute nothing to your resistance data. THAT is the finding. Who orders the
bottles?
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
199 | Page
Bed 4
Blood culture grew coagulase-negative staphylococcus — ONE bottle. Baby is well, feeding,
normal temperature, normal CRP.
Answer: This does NOT meet the commensal definition — one bottle, and no clinical features.
This is a contaminant. It is a blood culture technique failure, and it is about to cost this well baby
a course of vancomycin if nobody stops it. STOP. Then go and look at who took the sample and
whether they re-palpated.
Bed 4 closes the loop of the entire day: bad technique (10.6) → a false result (10.5) → an
unnecessary antibiotic (10.7) → resistance (10.1, objective 3). One re-palpated finger, four
sessions of consequence. End the exercise there.

Multiple forms are used for documentation of neonatal sepsis, including the blood culture request
form and the blood culture result form.
Facilitator Script
“This is the least popular half hour of the day. It is also the half hour that turns eight sick babies
into an outbreak report that gets you the bottles you ran out of in Bed 3. The form is not
administration. The form is advocacy.”
Practical Exercise — Documentation Drill

10.7 Prevention of Sepsis
Facilitator Script
“Everything today has been about what happens after. This is the only session about what
happens instead. And the whole of it fits in one sentence from the manual: the most important
interventions are hand washing and asepsis. Not a drug. Not a machine. Your hands.”
The Core Statements
● Many interventions are done to prevent neonatal sepsis, but the MOST IMPORTANT are
the standard infection control precautions — mainly hand washing and using asepsis
precautions while handling the newborn.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
201 | Page
● The newborn should be handled with strict asepsis starting FROM THE TIME OF
BIRTH. All invasive procedures — umbilical line or peripheral cannula insertion,
administration of surfactant, preparation of IV fluids, TPN, and antibiotics — should be
done using aseptic precautions, and a BUNDLE APPROACH should be used.
● A clear WRITTEN POLICY for infection control should be maintained and strictly
implemented during all SCNU procedures.
Do NOT wear artificial fingernails or extenders when having direct contact with patients. Natural
nails should be kept SHORT — ≤ 0.5 cm long, approximately ¼ inch.
FACILITATOR HINT
read the nail rule and then look at the room. Do not shame anyone. Just let it be quiet for a
moment. This is the cheapest intervention in the entire manual and it is written down because it
does not happen.
The Eight Interventions — Teach in This Order
1. Hand hygiene
2. Personal protective equipment (PPE)
3. Safe injections and sharps injury prevention
4. Environmental cleaning
5. Appropriate handling and transport of linen
6. Waste management
7. Decontamination and reprocessing of reusable patient care items and equipment
8. Aseptic technique
Note the order. Hand hygiene is first because it is first — not alphabetically, but in importance.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
202 | Page
Practical Session — Four Skills Stations
Four supervised stations, 4–6 participants per group, rotating. 20 minutes per station.
Station 1 — Hand Hygiene
Situation Method and duration
Hands NOT visibly soiled Alcohol-based hand rub — 20–30 SECONDS
Hands VISIBLY soiled Soap and water — 40–60 SECONDS
Turning off the tap Use a SINGLE-USE TOWEL — never your clean hand
Fungal isolate on the unit Chlorhexidine as the hand rub agent
● Time them. Every participant, with a watch. Almost nobody reaches 20 seconds unaided.
● Have them state WHEN — before touching the baby, before a procedure, after body fluid
exposure, after touching the baby, after touching the baby's surroundings.
“Twenty seconds feels like a very long time when someone is watching you do nothing else. That
feeling is why this fails on a busy ward. Learn the feeling now.”
Station 2 — PPE and Glove Use
● Correct selection based on RISK ASSESSMENT.
● Hand hygiene BEFORE donning and AFTER doffing.
● Correct glove-use timing.
● Examples of UNSAFE glove use, as listed in the manual.
Gloves are not a substitute for hand hygiene. The commonest unsafe glove use in any unit is the
same pair moving from one baby to the next — which is not protection, it is transport.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
203 | Page
Station 3 — Safe Injection and Sharps Safety
● Clean workspace preparation.
● Sterile syringe and needle use.
● Vial handling guidance.
● Skin disinfection with 60–70% alcohol.
● Sharps container use AT POINT OF CARE.
● NO RECAPPING.
● Discard sharps containers when THREE QUARTERS full.
Ask at this station: ‘where is the sharps container in your unit?’ If the answer is ‘by the door’
rather than ‘at the cot’, you have found a needlestick waiting to happen. Point of care means
point of care.
Station 4 — Decontamination of Reusable Equipment
● Use the decontamination station and the FULL manual sequence of steps.
● 0.1% CHLORINE working solution.
● 20-MINUTE contact time.
Cross-reference: device-specific reprocessing (incubator, CPAP, warmer, oximeter) is scored on
the disinfection checklists in the Equipment volume. This station teaches the general principle;
those checklists score the device.
Twenty minutes means twenty minutes. An item pulled out at eight minutes because the round is
starting is a contaminated item with a clean reputation — which is more dangerous than a visibly
dirty one.

11. Essential Neonatal Procedures
Procedures and equipment are NOT taught as standalone sessions. Every procedure and every device
is taught inside the clinical session that gives the reason for using it. This document tells you where
each one lives.
LEARNING OBJECTIVES
By the end of this neonatal procedures module, participants should be able to:
● Demonstrate correct preparation and aseptic technique for neonatal procedures.
● Perform selected neonatal procedures safely and competently according to standard
protocols.
● Apply appropriate pain prevention and comfort measures before, during, and after
procedures.
● Identify indications, contraindications, and potential complications of common neonatal
procedures.
● Document procedures accurately, including consent, technique, and monitoring.
ROLE OF THE FACILITATOR
Facilitators play a central role in ensuring safe learning and patient protection during procedural
training. Prior to each session, facilitators should:
● Review all procedural steps and equipment requirements.
● Identify appropriate neonates or simulation alternatives.
● Coordinate with neonatal unit staff to ensure clinical safety.
● Ensure informed consent is obtained from caregivers where applicable.
● Supervise all procedures closely, intervening immediately if patient safety is compromised.
Facilitators must emphasize patient-centered care, adherence to infection prevention standards, and
reflective learning throughout all procedural demonstrations and practice sessions
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
205 | Page
GENERAL PRINCIPLES FOR NEONATAL PROCEDURES
Procedure Documentation
Facilitators must reinforce that every procedure performed on a neonate must be documented clearly,
including:
● Date and time of procedure
● Indication and diagnosis
● Consent process and caregiver discussion
● Procedure performed and technique used
● Complications (if any)
● Post-procedure monitoring and plan
Refusal of procedures and associated counseling must also be documented comprehensively
Infection Prevention and Safety
All procedures must follow universal precautions:
● Strict hand hygiene
● Use of sterile or high-level disinfected equipment
● Maintenance of a clean and organized procedural field
● Appropriate disposal of sharps and contaminated materials
These principles are reinforced during clinical practice sessions within the neonatal unit
Pain Prevention and Comfort Measures
Facilitators should ensure that participants consistently apply pain-prevention strategies, including:
Environmental and Behavioral Measures
● Swaddling during procedures
● Non-nutritive sucking
● Minimizing noise and light
● Skin-to-skin contact (Kangaroo Mother Care) following procedures
Physiological Measures
● Oral glucose/sucrose administration according to gestational age
● Gentle tactile stimulation as competitive sensory input
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
206 | Page
TEACHING APPROACH AND SESSION STRUCTURE
Each neonatal procedure session should follow a standardized structure:
1. Brief theoretical overview (indications, risks, anatomy)
2. Facilitator demonstration using manikin
3. Supervised participant practice
4. Group reflection and feedback
5. Safety check and documentation review
Scoring
Score Means
0 Omitted · performed incorrectly and compromises safety · facilitator had to take over ·
contradicts protocol
1 Attempted but incomplete · minor errors not immediately endangering the baby · needed
verbal prompting · correct but poorly sequenced
2 Completed independently · correct, timely and safe · no prompting · aligns with protocol
• Score ONLY what is directly observed. Do not infer from seniority or confidence.
• Verbal explanation does not substitute for correct performance unless the row says so.
• One checklist = one observed procedure. If interrupted for patient safety, score completed steps
and document why.
• ≥ 80% Competent · 60–79% Competent with supervision · < 60% Not competent.
