11.1 Umbilical Vein Catheterization (UVC)
Taught in: Module (1) Advance Resuscitation session (Circulation) ·
Tools: vascular access simulator, UVC kits
Facilitator Script
“Remember this morning’s baby — heart rate still under 60 after 60 seconds of good
compressions. You said adrenaline. Now I am asking you: through what? This is the session that
answers that question.”
Competency
• Emergency vascular access indications
• Catheter length calculation methods
• Safe insertion technique
• Confirmation of catheter position
• Infection surveillance and removal criteria
Indications
• Emergency situation when urgent IV access is required
• To administer adrenaline during resuscitation
• Bolus of fluid for volume expansion
• To correct severe hypoglycaemia
• Single and double volume exchange transfusion
• Measurement of central venous pressure
• TPN, or dextrose concentration above 12.5%
• Vasopressors / inotropes
Contraindications
• Omphalitis · omphalocele · peritonitis · necrotising enterocolitis
Depth — The Number That Changes By Context
Context Insert to
Emergency resuscitation 2 to 4 cm (less in preterm) — until free flow of blood. STOP
there
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
208 | Page
Context Insert to
Elective / long line Usually 5 to 7 cm — until free blood return
Confirmed position Just above the dome of the right diaphragm, T9–T10
“In resuscitation you are not placing a long line. You are opening a road for the adrenaline. Two to
four centimetres, free blood, stop. Every extra centimetre you push is liver.”
Demonstration UVC
1. Arrange equipment; follow aseptic technique of infection prevention. Prepare the solution to
be infused.
2. Restrain the baby on a padded crucifix splint, under a radiant warmer with good light.
3. Cap and mask. Wash hands, sterile gown and sterile gloves.
4. Prepare the umbilicus in a spiral motion inward to outward: spirit → povidone iodine →
spirit, a different swab each time. Allow to dry.
5. Change to a second pair of sterile gloves. Drape with a sterile cloth.
6. Fill the catheter with normal saline via an attached syringe — no air.
7. Tie sterile umbilical tape around the base of the cord, tight enough to prevent bleeding.
8. Cut the stump horizontally with a sterile blade, 1.5–2 cm from the skin.
9. Hold the stump with toothed forceps and identify the vein: two thick-walled arteries; ONE
thin-walled vein with a wider opening, sitting towards the baby's head.
10. Remove any clot and debris from the vein.
11. Insert the saline-filled catheter gently, aiming towards the baby's head and to the baby's
RIGHT (towards the right shoulder). Do not force.
12. Apply gentle suction periodically as you advance. Once blood flows back freely —
STOP. Do not advance further.
13. If resistance is met, especially in the first 2–3 cm: do NOT continue. Remove and try
again. Do not infuse into the liver.
14. Tie the cord tie or suture around the stump to hold the catheter and prevent bleeding
around it or from an artery.
15. Remove the syringe, connect the infusion set — no air bubbles.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
209 | Page
16. Secure with suture material or adhesive tape.
17. Confirm the tip by X-ray or ultrasound — T9–T10. If in the hepatic or portal vein,
remove and retry.
18. The catheter may be pulled back but NEVER advanced once the sterile field is broken.
19. Inspect hourly. Redness or swelling around the umbilicus → stop the infusion, remove the
catheter, establish peripheral IV, treat the umbilical infection.
Common Errors
• Choosing an artery — thick-walled, two of them, constricted lumen.
• Forcing through resistance in the first 2–3 cm.
• Advancing after blood return — ‘just a bit more to be safe’ — straight into the liver.
• Advancing a catheter after the field is broken.
• Skipping the second glove change.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
210 | Page

11.2 Umbilical Artery Catheterization (UAC)
Taught in: Advance Resuscitation session
Tools: vascular access simulator, UAC kits
This checklist previously read ‘Identifies need for UVC’ in its Indication row, and was duplicated
under two headings. Corrected below.
Facilitator Script
“You have just done the vein. Same stump, same field, same gloves — but a different vessel and
a completely different set of dangers. The vein forgives a little. The artery does not: what you put
down here goes to the gut, the kidneys and the legs.”
Competency
• Indications and contraindications
• Equipment selection by birth weight
• High vs. low catheter positioning
• Stepwise insertion technique
• Monitoring and complication recognition
Teach the Difference Before the Technique
Vein Artery
How many ONE TWO
Wall Thin Thick, muscular
Lumen Wide, gaping Small, constricted
Position on the
stump
Towards the baby's head Usually towards the feet
Direction to aim Towards the right shoulder —
cephalad
Caudally first, towards the feet,
then it curves up
Blood on entry Dark, flows freely Pulsatile, brighter
High vs Low Position — Teach Both
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
212 | Page
HIGH LOW
Tip at T6–T9 (above the diaphragm) L3–L4 (below the renal arteries)
Avoids Renal and mesenteric vessel
origins
—
Evidence Fewer ischaemic complications More vascular complications
reported
Never at T10–L2 — the coeliac, mesenteric
and renal origins
Same — never park in T10–L2
Whatever position you choose, the tip must NEVER sit between T10 and L2. That is where the
coeliac, superior mesenteric and renal arteries come off. A catheter parked there is a gut or a kidney.
Demonstration — What Differs From the UVC
1. Preparation, asepsis, draping, cord tie and stump cut: IDENTICAL to the UVC. Do not re-
teach — say ‘everything up to here is what you just did’.
2. Identify the two arteries — thick-walled, small lumen, usually towards the feet.
3. Dilate the arterial lumen gently with fine forceps. Wait. The artery is in spasm; it will open.
4. Insert the saline-filled catheter aiming CAUDALLY — towards the feet.
5. Advance with gentle steady pressure. Resistance at 1–2 cm (abdominal wall) and again at 5–
6 cm (bladder level) is normal — steady gentle pressure, never force.
6. Blood return confirms intraluminal placement.
7. Advance to the pre-calculated length for the chosen position.
8. Secure. Confirm by X-ray — and read the tip against the vertebrae, not the ruler.
9. Then, every hour: look at the legs, the feet and the buttocks.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
213 | Page
Complications to Name Out Loud
• Vasospasm — white or blue leg, foot or buttock. The commonest, and the earliest warning.
• Thrombosis and embolism.
• Ischaemia of the gut (NEC), kidney, or limb.
• Haemorrhage — a disconnected arterial line exsanguinates a newborn in minutes.
• Infection. Hypertension. False tract.
If a leg blanches or goes blue after a UAC: that is the catheter, until proved otherwise. Warm the
OPPOSITE leg reflexly. If it does not resolve quickly — remove the line. A line is replaceable; a
foot is not.
Common Errors
• Forcing at the two normal resistance points instead of waiting with steady pressure.
• Aiming cephalad, like the vein.
• Not dilating the lumen and declaring the artery ‘impossible’.
• Leaving the tip at T10–L2.
• Not looking at the legs afterwards — the whole complication set is visible on the skin.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

11.3 Peripherally Inserted Central Catheter (PICC)
Taught in: Prematurity — the admitted preterm ·
Tools: video demonstration
Facilitator Script
“Your 1.1 kg baby is on day 3. He needs TPN for two weeks. Every peripheral line lasts a day
and a half and costs him a cannulation. This is how you stop hurting him — once, properly, and
then leave him alone.”
Competency (from the guide)
• Indications for prolonged intravenous therapy
• Site selection and measurement techniques
• Aseptic insertion and securing of lines
• Prevention of malposition and infection
Indications
• Prolonged IV therapy — more than 5–7 days
• Total parenteral nutrition
• Dextrose concentration above 12.5%
• Vasopressors / inotropes
• Drugs that are irritant or vesicant
• The VLBW baby who will exhaust peripheral access
Site Selection and Measurement
Site Measure from insertion point to
Basilic (preferred) Right axilla, then to the sternal notch — tip in the SVC
Cephalic As above — but note the sharp angle at the shoulder
Long saphenous Groin, then to the xiphisternum — tip in the IVC above the
diaphragm
Scalp veins Only if others fail
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
216 | Page
The tip must NOT sit inside the heart. A tip in the right atrium causes tamponade and arrhythmia.
Central means the SVC or IVC outside the cardiac silhouette — not ‘as deep as it will go’.
Demonstration — The Steps
1. Select the vein. Measure the distance BEFORE opening the set — measure twice.
2. Full aseptic precautions: cap, mask, sterile gown, sterile gloves, large sterile drape. This is a
central line, not a cannula.
3. Prepare the skin: spirit → dry → iodine → dry → spirit.
4. Achieve venous access with the introducer.
5. Advance the catheter SLOWLY with non-toothed forceps, a few millimetres at a time.
6. If it stops: do not force. Reposition the limb — abduct or adduct the arm, turn the head
towards the insertion side to close off the jugular. Then try again.
7. Advance to the measured length. Not further.
8. Aspirate for blood return; flush gently.
9. Secure the line and apply a sterile transparent dressing so the site stays visible.
10. Request imaging confirmation with contrast if needed. Read the tip position before you
use the line.
11. Then: never use it before the X-ray is seen; inspect the site every shift; minimise access.
Common Errors
• Measuring after opening the set — or once.
• Treating it like a cannula: gloves only, no drape, no gown.
• Forcing when it stops, instead of repositioning the limb or head.
• Using the line before seeing the film.
• Leaving the tip in the heart.
Opening the line repeatedly for sampling — every entry is a sepsis risk.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
217 | Page

11.4 Intraosseous (IO) Access
Taught in: Emergencies — shock session
Tools: chicken thigh, IO kits
Facilitator Script
“Capillary refill five seconds. Heart rate 180. Two nurses have tried four times and there is no
vein. Six minutes have gone. What do you do? — You stop trying for a vein. The bone is a vein
that cannot collapse.”
The chicken thigh is not a joke prop. It has cortex, it has marrow, and it gives the exact ‘give’ the
participant must learn to feel. Let everyone feel the pop before they ever meet a shocked baby.
Competency
• Indications and contraindications
• Correct site of insertion
• Skin preparation
• Stepwise insertion technique
• Monitoring and complication recognition
Indications and Contraindications
Indicated Contraindicated
Emergency — IV access failed or will take too
long
Fracture in that bone
Shock, cardiac arrest, severe dehydration Previous IO attempt in the SAME bone — use
the other leg
Any resuscitation drug, fluid or blood can go
IO
Infection or burn over the site
Osteogenesis imperfecta
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
219 | Page
The Site
• Proximal tibia: 1–2 cm BELOW and 1 cm MEDIAL to the tibial tuberosity, on the flat antero-
medial surface.
• Aim away from the joint — direct the needle slightly caudally, at 90° or angled away from the
growth plate.
Aim AWAY from the growth plate. A needle through the physis is a leg that grows short.
Demonstration — Intraosseous (IO) Access
1. Identify the landmark. Say it out loud: below and medial to the tuberosity.
2. Clean the skin — aseptic technique.
3. Insert with a firm rotating motion, perpendicular or angled slightly away from the joint.
4. Feel for the sudden loss of resistance — the pop. Stop advancing immediately.
5. The needle should STAND FIRMLY WITHOUT SUPPORT. That is your first
confirmation.
6. Remove the stylet. Attempt to aspirate marrow — note that it is NOT necessary to aspirate
marrow to confirm placement.
7. Slowly inject 3 ml of IV fluid to check placement.
8. LOOK for swelling — at the front of the leg, and in the calf muscle at the back. Swelling =
extravasation. Remove the needle and try the OTHER leg.
9. If infusion is difficult but there is NO swelling: the needle may have entered the posterior
cortex. Withdraw about 0.5 cm and cautiously inject again.
10. If no problems: secure the needle. Attach standard IV tubing.
11. Increased pressure — pressure bag or push — may be needed. There is a high risk of
obstruction if continuous high-pressure fluids are not flushed through.
12. Marrow can be sent for glucose, chemistry, blood type and crossmatch, haemoglobin,
blood gas and culture.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
220 | Page
Remove the IO needle as soon as alternative IV access is available, and WITHIN EIGHT HOURS if
possible.
Common Errors
• Pushing past the pop — straight through the far cortex.
• Aiming at the joint.
• Re-attempting in the same bone after a failure — the fluid leaks out of the first hole.
• Not looking at the calf. The swelling is behind the leg where nobody looks.
• Waiting for marrow aspirate to ‘confirm’ — it is not necessary and it wastes seconds.
Leaving it in beyond 8 hours.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
221 | Page

11.5 Needle Thoracotomy
Taught in: Prematurity — complications / RDS
Tools: video demonstration, simulation
Facilitator Script
“A ventilated 28-weeker. Sudden desaturation, bradycardia, the chest is not moving on one side.
You do not have time for an X-ray. This is the one procedure on this course where waiting for a
film is the wrong answer.”
Recognise It First — The Procedure Is the Easy Part
• Sudden deterioration in a ventilated or CPAP baby
• Desaturation and bradycardia
• Reduced or absent air entry on one side
• Asymmetrical chest movement
• Displaced apex beat
• Transillumination positive — useful in the dark, in a small baby
Tension pneumothorax is a CLINICAL diagnosis. In an unstable baby, do not wait for the X-ray.
The X-ray comes after the needle.
The Landmark — and the Rule That Prevents Bleeding
• 2nd intercostal space, mid-clavicular line — or 4th–5th space, mid-axillary line.
• Insert over the SUPERIOR margin of the rib below.
Over the TOP of the lower rib. The neurovascular bundle runs under the rib above. Go under the rib
and you hit the artery, the vein and the nerve.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
223 | Page
Demonstration — Needle Thoractomy
1. Recognise it clinically. Say the findings out loud as you go.
2. Position: supine, affected side up slightly if using the axillary approach.
3. Aseptic skin preparation — fast, but done.
4. Attach a 23–25G butterfly or cannula to a three-way tap and syringe BEFORE you puncture.
5. Insert perpendicular, over the superior margin of the rib below.
6. Advance while aspirating. Air rushes back — that is your confirmation.
7. Aspirate until resistance. Do not over-evacuate.
8. Withdraw the needle if using a butterfly; leave the cannula if you are bridging to a drain.
9. Reassess: air entry, saturation, heart rate, chest movement. It should be immediate.
10. Plan the chest tube. A needle is a bridge, not a treatment.
11. Then get the X-ray.
Common Errors
• Waiting for the film in an unstable baby.
• Going under the rib — into the bundle.
• Puncturing before the three-way tap and syringe are attached.
• Over-evacuating.
Treating the needle as definitive and not planning the drain
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
224 | Page

11.6 Chest Tube Insertion
Taught in: Prematurity — complications / RDS, immediately after needle thoracotomy · Tools:
video demonstration
Facilitator Script
“The needle bought you ten minutes. It is now minute nine. The needle is not a treatment — it is
a bridge, and this is the other side of it.”
Competency
• Recognize indication of chest tube
• Select correct tube size
• Identify correct site for insertion
• Insert tube at correct depth
• Stepwise insertion technique
• Connect drain correctly
• Monitoring and complication recognition
Size and Site
Weight Tube size
< 1000 g 8 Fr
1000–2000 g 8–10 Fr
> 2000 g 10–12 Fr
• Site: 4th–5th intercostal space, anterior to the mid-axillary line — in the ‘safe triangle’.
• Air rises: direct the tube anteriorly and towards the apex.
• Fluid falls: direct it posteriorly and basally.
Stay ANTERIOR to the mid-axillary line and stay AWAY from the nipple. The internal mammary
vessels run just lateral to the sternum; the long thoracic nerve runs posteriorly. In a preterm the
whole chest is the width of your palm.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
226 | Page
Demonstration — chest tube insertion
1. Confirm the indication and select the tube size for the weight.
2. Position: supine, arm abducted above the head, affected side up 30–45°.
3. Full aseptic technique — cap, mask, gown, gloves, sterile drape.
4. Local anaesthetic and analgesia. This baby feels this. Module (2) 7.1 applies here.
5. Small skin incision, one space BELOW the target space.
6. BLUNT dissection with mosquito forceps, over the superior margin of the rib below, until
you feel the pleura give.
7. Insert the tube WITHOUT the trocar, guided by the forceps. Direct it anteriorly for air.
8. Advance to the pre-measured depth — all side holes inside the chest, and no further.
9. Look for misting in the tube and bubbling in the drain.
10. Connect to an underwater seal or a one-way valve drainage bag.
11. Secure the tube with a suture and dressing. Do not kink it.
12. Confirm the position with X-ray.
13. Monitor: bubbling, swinging, output, and the baby.
Chest drains are NEVER clamped — including for transport. Use a one-way valve drainage bag
rather than an underwater bottle if the baby is moving.
Common Errors
• Using the trocar. It goes through the lung.
• Sharp dissection instead of blunt.
• Going under the rib.
• Advancing too far — the tube ends up in the mediastinum or across the midline.
• Not advancing far enough — a side hole outside the chest gives a surgical emphysema and no
drainage.
• Clamping for transport.
Forgetting the analgesia because the baby is sick.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
227 | Page

11.7 Lumbar Puncture
Taught in: Emergencies — convulsions (the S of SHOT)
Tools: video demonstration
Facilitator Script
“Remember the golden hour rule: LP is not routine for early-onset sepsis screening. You do it
when the blood culture is positive, when clinical suspicion is strong, or when the baby presents
with seizures, apnoea or depressed sensorium. Not because the form has a box.”
Competency
• Recognize indication of LP
• Position baby correctly
• Identify correct site for insertion
• Insert needle correctly
• Stepwise insertion technique
• Monitoring and complication recognition
Indications and Contraindications
Do it when Do NOT do it when
Blood culture is positive The baby is haemodynamically unstable —
stabilise first
Strong clinical suspicion of meningitis Signs of raised intracranial pressure
Seizures, apnoea or depressed sensorium Severe respiratory compromise — the position
will apnoea him
Bleeding diathesis / thrombocytopenia
Local skin infection over the site
It is not routine for early-onset sepsis screening. And an unstable baby does not get an LP — the
antibiotics do not wait for the CSF.
Position — This Is Where the Procedure Succeeds or Fails
• Lateral decubitus, or sitting.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
229 | Page
• Flex the hips and knees. Flex the spine.
• Do NOT flex the neck. It obstructs the airway and does not open the interspaces.
• The assistant holds the baby, watches the airway, and watches the monitor — not the needle.
The assistant is a clinical role, not a pair of hands. Neck flexion and over-flexion cause apnoea and
desaturation during LP. Whoever holds the baby has one job: the airway.
“Ninety per cent of failed taps are failed positions. If the back is not perpendicular to the bed and the
spine is not flexed, do not put the needle in — fix the position first. You cannot rescue a bad position
with a good needle.”
The Landmark
• L3–L4 or L4–L5 interspace — on the line joining the top of the iliac crests.
• The neonatal cord ends lower than in an adult. Going higher risks the cord.
Demonstration — Lumber Puncture
1. Confirm the indication and exclude the contraindications out loud.
2. Assign the assistant and brief them: airway and monitor.
3. Position: lateral decubitus, hips and knees flexed, spine flexed, NECK NEUTRAL.
4. Identify the interspace on the intercristal line.
5. Sterile technique: gloves, drape, skin prep spirit → dry → iodine → dry → spirit.
6. Analgesia — topical or local. Module (2) 7.1.
7. Insert the needle in the midline, angled slightly towards the umbilicus, bevel up.
8. Advance slowly. Remove the stylet frequently to check for CSF — the ‘give’ is often not felt
in a newborn.
9. Collect CSF into the tubes: cell count, protein/glucose, culture, and glucose to be paired with
a blood glucose.
10. Replace the stylet before withdrawing.
11. Apply pressure and a dressing.
12. Reassess the baby — saturation, heart rate, breathing.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
230 | Page
Common Errors
• Flexing the neck — the commonest cause of desaturation on the table.
• Starting with a bad position and hoping.
• Not removing the stylet often enough — you have been in the CSF and gone straight through.
• Going too high.
• Doing it in an unstable baby.
Nobody watching the monitor.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
231 | Page

11.8 Urinary Bladder Catheterization
Taught in: Admission — monitoring and orders · Tools: video demonstration
Facilitator Script
“Your admission orders say ‘monitor urine output hourly’. Good. Now tell me how. Urine output
above 1.5 ml/kg/hour is one of your best signs that the shock is resolving — but only if you can
actually measure it.”
Competency (from the guide)
• Recognize indication of urinary bladder catheter
• Select correct catheter size
• Insert catheter correctly
• Stepwise insertion technique
• Monitoring and complication recognition
Indications and Contraindications
Indicated Contraindicated
Accurate hourly urine output in the sick or
shocked baby
Suspected urethral trauma
Urinary retention Blood at the meatus
Sterile urine sample when suprapubic
aspiration is not possible
Known urethral anomaly — hypospadias,
posterior urethral valves
Before certain surgery or imaging Local infection
Never force a catheter in a boy with hypospadias or blood at the meatus. A false passage in a
neonatal urethra is a lifelong stricture.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
233 | Page
Size
Weight Catheter
< 1000 g 3.5 Fr feeding tube
1000–1800 g 5 Fr
> 1800 g 5–6 Fr, or 6 Fr Foley if indwelling
Demonstration — urinary bladder catheterization
1. Confirm the indication. Exclude the contraindications out loud.
2. Position supine; frog-leg for girls.
3. Sterile gloves. Clean the meatus outward in circles — different swab each time.
4. Lubricate the catheter.
5. Boys: hold the penis perpendicular, apply gentle traction to straighten the urethra. Advance
gently. Slight resistance at the external sphincter is normal — pause, steady pressure, do not
force.
6. Girls: separate the labia, identify the meatus ABOVE the vaginal opening. If you enter the
vagina, leave that catheter in place as a marker and use a fresh one for the urethra.
7. Advance until urine flows, then 1–2 cm more — for a Foley, only inflate the balloon once
urine is flowing.
8. Never inflate a balloon against resistance.
9. Secure the catheter to the thigh without tension. Keep the bag below bladder level.
10. Monitor: output, blood, and signs of infection. Remove as soon as it is not needed.
Common Errors
• Forcing at the sphincter.
• Inflating the balloon before urine flows — in the urethra.
• In girls, entering the vagina and not noticing.
• Leaving it in ‘because it is there’ — every day is a CAUTI risk.
Bag above the bladder.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
234 | Page

2. Positions baby correctly

5. Lubricates catheter

7.
Safety
Monitoring
Does not force against resistance
8.
In girls, recognises vaginal placement and uses a fresh
catheter
9. Inflates balloon ONLY after urine flows
10. Secures catheter without tension; bag below bladder level
11. Observes for complications — trauma, blood, infection
12. Documentation Documents procedure
Final Evaluation: ☐ Competent (≥ 80 % ) ☐ Competent with supervision (60-79%) ☐ Not

11.9 Suprapubic Bladder Aspiration
Taught in: Admission — monitoring and orders, alongside catheterization · Tools: video
demonstration
Facilitator Script
“This is the gold standard for a clean urine sample in a newborn — no urethra, no skin flora, no
argument about contamination. It looks alarming. It is safer than the catheter.”
Competency
• Recognize indication of urinary bladder aspiration
• Identify correct site of insertion
• Insert needle correctly
• Stepwise insertion technique
• Monitoring and complication recognition
Indications and Contraindications
Indicated Contraindicated
Sterile urine sample for culture — the gold
standard
Bladder empty — wait, or use ultrasound
Suspected UTI or sepsis workup Bleeding diathesis / thrombocytopenia
Catheterisation failed or contraindicated Abdominal distension, organomegaly, or
bowel loops over the bladder
Local skin infection · known genitourinary
anomaly · recent abdominal surgery
Timing is the whole procedure. At least one hour since the last void. If you have ultrasound, look
first — an empty bladder is a dry tap and a needle in the bowel for nothing. If the baby voids while
you are prepping, catch it and abandon.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
236 | Page
The Site
• Midline, 1–2 cm ABOVE the symphysis pubis.
• Needle perpendicular to the skin, or angled 10–20° towards the head.
• Depth is shallow — the neonatal bladder is an abdominal organ, not a pelvic one.
Midline. Perpendicular. Shallow. Going lateral risks the iliac vessels; going deep risks the bowel.
Demonstration — suprapubic aspiration
1. Confirm the indication; exclude contraindications; confirm at least an hour since the last
void.
2. An assistant holds the legs in the frog position and occludes the urethra gently — boys: hold
the penis; girls: gentle pressure at the meatus.
3. Sterile gloves. Prepare the skin: spirit → dry → iodine → dry → spirit.
4. Analgesia — sucrose, non-nutritive sucking, or local. Module (2) 7.1.
5. Identify the midline, 1–2 cm above the symphysis.
6. Insert a 22–24G needle perpendicular, or angled slightly cephalad, WHILE ASPIRATING.
7. Advance only 2–3 cm. Urine appears. Stop.
8. Aspirate the sample slowly.
9. Withdraw in one movement. Apply pressure and a dressing.
10. Reassess. Look at the next nappy for blood — transient microscopic haematuria is
common and self-limiting.
11. If the tap is dry: do NOT redirect the needle inside the abdomen. Withdraw completely,
wait 30–60 minutes, and try once more.
Common Errors
• Going too deep — through the bladder and into the bowel.
• Going off the midline.
• Not aspirating on the way in, so you pass through the bladder without knowing.
• Fanning the needle around inside to ‘find’ the bladder.
• Doing it on an empty bladder because nobody asked when he last voided.
Skipping analgesia because ‘it is quick’.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
237 | Page

12.1 Radiant Warmer
Taught in: Admission — preparation before receiving the baby
Facilitator Script
“The phone has just rung. A 34-weeker is coming from theatre, ten minutes. What is the first
thing you touch? — Not the trolley. This. Because it takes twenty minutes to prewarm and you
have ten. Which means the honest answer is: you should have turned it on when the labour ward
first called.”
Indications
1. Newborn resuscitation
2. Short-term procedures
3. Severe hypothermia requiring rapid warming
Infants with suspected perinatal asphyxia should NOT be placed under a radiant warmer or in an
incubator — it puts them at risk of hyperthermia. Ask the group why that matters. The answer is the
cooling protocol.
Parts — Name Them on the Machine, Not on a Slide
• Bed / bassinet · overhead unit (radiant heat source) · skin probe · air probe
• Control panel: mode selector · heater output control · heater output display · temperature
selection · temperature display · alarm display
• Storage tray · X-ray tray
Preparation — Two Lists, Both Checked
Machine preparation Baby preparation
Direct power source Alcohol-based hand rub
Temperature sensor (probe) Medical adhesive tape
Clean mattress A thermometer
Linen to cover the mattress Plastic wrap (if below 32 weeks GA)
Cotton swabs with 70% alcohol Items for the specific procedure
• Room temperature must be ≥ 22°C for the warmer to function optimally.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
240 | Page
Standard Operating procedure for Warmer
1. Turn the warmer on 20 minutes BEFORE the baby, to prewarm the linen and mattress.
2. Ensure the mattress and linen are clean.
3. Select the mode.
4. Set the target temperature to 36.5–37.0°C.
5. Apply the probe over the RIGHT HYPOCHONDRIUM in supine position, or the FLANK in
prone position.
6. Connect the skin probe to the abdomen with sticking tape. Cover with a reflective pad if
available.
7. Check the probe is in place every 2–3 hours.
8. Check and record the temperature every 2–3 hours.
9. Change the probe site every 24 hours.
The Two Modes — Teach the Difference or the Rest Is Meaningless
MANUAL SERVO

baby

241 | Page
Common Errors
• Turning it on when the baby arrives — twenty minutes too late.
• Leaving a baby unattended in manual mode.
• Probe on bruised skin, or removed with a fingernail.
• Probe taped to the mattress, not the baby — the heater runs forever.
• Never switching from manual to servo once the baby is warm.
• Forgetting the extra fluid a warmer costs.
• Using spirit or an organic solvent to clean it.

12.2 Radiant Warmer — Disinfection
• External surfaces: cleaned DAILY with light detergent or soap, then chlorine solution or
glutaraldehyde.
• Reusable probe: isopropyl alcohol swab.
• After use: clean, disinfect, and cover with a linen cover to store properly.
• Self-maintenance: inspect the heating element weekly. Calibrate control and power units every
4–6 months. Thorough servicing annually.
NO SPIRIT. NO ORGANIC SOLVENT on the external surfaces. It degrades the housing. The
alcohol swab is for the probe only.

12.3 Portable Warmer
Taught in: Transport — methods of transport
Facilitator Script
“Your baby is 35.8°C. The referral hospital is 45 minutes away. You have no transport incubator,
and the ambulance has no power. Everything you learned about the radiant warmer is now
useless — it needs a wall socket and it is bolted to that room. This device needs neither.”
This is the one device on this list built for exactly the constraints Module (1) names: no organised
transport, no power, families with no resources. Teach it that way.
How It Works — Explain the Wax, or Nobody Trusts It
Part What it does
WarmPak Contains a wax that MELTS at 37°C and releases heat as it
SOLIDIFIES — maintaining the baby's temperature for 4–6 hours
AccuTemp Heater A small electric heater to prepare the WarmPak
BabyWrap The sleeping-bag-like garment that holds the WarmPak and the baby
“The wax cannot go above 37 degrees — that is the physics, not a setting. It cannot burn him. That is
why this device is safe in a vehicle where nobody is watching a dial.”
Standard operating procedure portable warmer
1. Wash hands. Ensure the BabyWrap is clean and disinfected.
2. Plug the AccuTemp Heater into the power source and put the WarmPak inside the heater for 30
MINUTES.
3. Once warm, place the warmed WarmPak inside the pouch in the BabyWrap.
4. Place the infant in the BabyWrap.
5. Use the RAINBOW SIGN to monitor the target temperature.
6. After use, clean the BabyWrap and the AccuTemp Heater.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
244 | Page
Thirty minutes to charge, 4–6 hours of warmth. Ask the group: when do you start heating the
WarmPak? Same answer as the radiant warmer — when the phone rings, not when the baby is at the
door.
The Rainbow Indicator
• It is the only monitor this device has. Teach the participant to read it and to say out loud what it
means.
• It does not replace a thermometer. Check the baby's axillary temperature as well — the rainbow
tells you about the pak, the thermometer tells you about the baby.
Common Errors
• Not allowing the full 30 minutes — a half-charged pak does not last the journey.
• Using a BabyWrap that was not cleaned between babies.
• Reading the rainbow and never touching the baby.
• Assuming it replaces skin-to-skin. For a stable baby, the mother is still better.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
245 | Page
12.4 Portable Warmer — Disinfection
• AccuTemp Heater: disinfect with an appropriate solution (spirit or chlorine-based) BEFORE
EACH USE, by wiping the device surface.
• WarmPak: has no direct contact with the neonate. Clean whenever needed — wipe with a towel
immersed in soap and water, then wipe with an appropriate disinfectant.
• BabyWrap: clean with soap and water BETWEEN PATIENTS.
• Blood exposure: chlorine-based solution 0.05% for 20 minutes only, then wash thoroughly in
distilled water and dry.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

12.5 Infant Incubator
Taught in: Golden Hour — prevention of hypothermia; revisited in Prematurity — thermal
protection
Facilitator Script
“This machine is the difference between a 900-gram baby surviving the week and not. And the
commonest way it fails is not a fault — it is two people opening both sides at once to change a
nappy.”
Who Goes In One
• ALL preterm infants ≤ 30 weeks gestation and/or ≤ 1000 g should be nursed in incubators.
• Incubators are the admission bed of choice for all babies born < 33 weeks or ≤ 1800 g.
• Some babies ≥ 33 weeks may need one if hypothermic on admission.
• Term and near-term infants may be swaddled and nursed in a cot if stable.
• For those requiring respiratory care, an open care system should be used.
Infants with suspected perinatal asphyxia should NOT be placed in an incubator — it puts them at
risk of hyperthermia.
Why the Double Wall, and Why One Side at a Time
• Incubators are fully enclosed cots that add heated air and humidity while reducing stimuli
including light and sound.
• They are DOUBLE WALLED to reduce radiant and conductive heat loss.
• They are efficient when ONE side is opened for cares or procedures — but NOT when both
sides are opened together, which causes high convective and evaporative heat loss.
Demonstrate this. Open one side, then open both. Ask the group what just happened to the air inside.
This single habit costs nothing and is one of the five interventions the manual lists as proven to
reduce hypothermia in very preterm infants.
Standard Operating Procedure — Infant Incubator
1. Ensure the incubator is clean and disinfected before use.
2. Connect to a reliable power source; ensure the power cable is intact.
3. Switch on using the main power button.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
247 | Page
4. Select the mode — servo or manual.
5. Attach the skin temperature probe to the abdomen or back. Change the probe site every 8–12
hours.
6. Ensure the water reservoir is filled with STERILE or DISTILLED water.
7. Adjust the humidity.
8. Then: minimise the time doors on OPPOSITE sides are open during procedures and cares.
The Settings — The Numbers Your Checklist Assumes
Mode Setting
SERVO (skin control) Set the temperature to 36.5–37°C. The manual's Golden Hour section
specifies servo targeting an abdominal skin temperature of 36.5°C
MANUAL (air mode)
— extremely preterm
< 28 wks
34–36°C
MANUAL — very
preterm 28–32 wks
33–34°C
MANUAL — near-
term / term
32–33°C
Servo and manual are set differently and this confuses everyone. In SERVO you set the temperature
you want the BABY to be (36.5–37°C) and the incubator works out its own. In MANUAL you set
the temperature you want the AIR to be (32–36°C by gestation). Setting a servo incubator to 34°C,
or a manual one to 36.5°C, are both dangerous errors.
Humidity
Baby Humidity
Usual start 40–70%, depending on weight and condition
ELBW / VLBW (< 1500 g),
first week
70–90%
After 1 week Gradually reduce to 40–50%
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
248 | Page
• Water reservoir: sterile or distilled water only, changed DAILY.
• Any baby requiring humidity should NOT be dressed or covered by fabrics.
“Humidity is not comfort. In a 900-gram baby the skin is not a barrier — water pours out of it.
Ninety per cent humidity in week one is a fluid prescription.”
Common Errors
• Both doors open at once.
• Confusing servo and manual set-points.
• Tap water in the reservoir.
• Reservoir water not changed daily — it becomes a bacterial nebuliser.
• Probe loose or detached in servo mode.
• Dressing a baby who is in humidity.
• An asphyxiated baby in an incubator.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

12.7 Bubble CPAP
Taught in: Prematurity — RDS session
Facilitator Script
“Twenty-nine weeks, 1.2 kg, grunting, saturation 86. You have no ventilator and no surfactant.
Yesterday you would have called that hopeless. The evidence says early CPAP reduces death,
reduces BPD, reduces intubation — and reduces the NEED for the surfactant you do not have.
This machine is not second best. It is first line.”
The Prerequisite — Say It Before Anything Else
The most important prerequisite for starting CPAP is the PRESENCE OF GOOD
RESPIRATORY EFFORT. CPAP splints an airway; it does not breathe for a baby. A baby with
poor respiratory effort needs PPV, not CPAP.
Indications and Contraindications
Indicated Contraindicated
Spontaneously breathing preterm with
respiratory distress
Poor respiratory effort
Recurrent apnoea not responding to medical
measures
Tracheo-oesophageal fistula
Post-extubation from mechanical ventilation Choanal atresia
Term babies with respiratory distress or low
lung volume
Cleft palate
Very preterm (< 32 weeks) immediately after
birth, with or without distress
Severe cardiovascular instability
pH < 7.25 and PaCO₂ > 60 mmHg —
progressive respiratory failure
Parts
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
251 | Page
• Vayu Blender Pressure Generator · Humidifier · Humidifier Tube · Reusable Filter · Rotator ·
Breathing Tubes and Nasal Prong
Standard Operating Procedure — Portable CPAP
1. Ensure all components are clean and disinfected.
2. Assemble the system according to manufacturer guidance; ensure correct connections.
3. Fit the cap — covering up to the ears, ears lying FLAT under it. A cap that is too small causes
oedema and discomfort.
4. Select the prong size by birth weight (table below). Moisten the prongs with saline before
insertion.
5. Secure the prongs. The bridge of the prongs must NOT abut the columella and must NOT
blanch the skin.
6. Connect the prongs to the circuit. Stabilise the head so the prongs do not dislodge.
7. INSERT AN OROGASTRIC TUBE. CPAP inflates the stomach.
8. Set the pressure, FiO₂ and flow.
9. Ensure CONTINUOUS BUBBLING at all times.
10. Monitor the devices and the patient.
11. Confirm the oxygen cylinder pressure regularly.
Prong Size by Birth Weight
Size Birth weight Size Birth weight
0 < 700 g 3 2000–3000 g
1 700–1000 g 4 3000–4000 g
2 1000–2000 g 5 > 4000 g
The best-fitting prong is the BIGGEST that comfortably fits the nostril without distending it. Too
tight causes pressure necrosis; too loose causes a large air leak and no CPAP. Periodically check the
nasal mucosa for hyperaemia and the columella for blanching.
Device Monitoring — The Housekeeping That Keeps It Working
• Refill the humidifier BEFORE water drops below the ‘REFILL’ line.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
252 | Page
• Refill the pressure generator when water drops below the ‘fill mark’.
• Replace water in jars every 24 hours.
• Replace the filter disk every 24 hours — earlier if discoloured, or after each patient.
No bubbling = no CPAP. If the bubbling stops, the baby is receiving nothing but expensive
humidified air, and the only sign is on the machine, not on the baby — until he tires.
Common Errors
• Starting CPAP on a baby with poor respiratory effort.
• Prongs too small — a leak, and no pressure.
• Prongs too big — pressure necrosis of the columella.
• No orogastric tube — the abdomen distends and splints the diaphragm.
• Not noticing the bubbling has stopped.
• Waiting for an X-ray before increasing the pressure on a grunting baby.
• Water jars and filter not changed at 24 hours.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
253 | Page
12.8 Bubble CPAP (portable) — Disinfection
● Empty the jars, remove any gross soiling, then FULLY disassemble the device.
● Soak in clean water until ready to reprocess.
● Clean with enzymatic detergent or dish soap, using a soft-bristled brush or sponge.
● Brush lumens AND flush lumens THREE TIMES.
● Rinse all components.
● High-level disinfection — flushing each lumen with disinfectant at least THREE TIMES.
● Air dry completely before storage.
Do NOT autoclave or boil components.

12.9 Pulse Oximeter
Taught in: Golden Hour — respiration
Facilitator Script
“This is the smallest device on the list and the only one that tells you the dose of a drug. Oxygen
is a drug. It is the drug that causes ROP. This machine is how you prescribe it — and ‘100%
because he looks blue’ is not a prescription.”
Indications
• All sick newborns — oxygen saturation as the FIFTH vital parameter
• Assessment of response to resuscitation
• Titration of oxygen therapy in newborns
• Apnoea monitor
• Valuable during transport of the newborn
The Targets — The Numbers Your Checklist Assumes
Baby Target SpO₂
Usually 85–95%
Preterm 89–95%
< 29 weeks 85–92%
Module (1) rule Give oxygen below SaO₂ 85%; gradually withdraw above 92%
Never Persistent value > 95%
Avoid persistent values above 95%. In the preterm baby, a saturation of 99% is not a good result —
it is a retina being damaged, and it is your fault, not the disease's.
“Every ROP risk factor in the manual is something you control on this ward: hypoxia, hypothermia,
hypotension, sepsis, poor weight gain. This machine is the one that controls the first one — in both
directions.”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
255 | Page
Standard Operating Procedure — Pulse Oximeter
1. Clean the probe with spirit / alcohol and let it dry.
2. Switch on the pulse oximeter.
3. Apply the probe to a site that is WARM and WELL PERFUSED.
4. Ensure both sides of the probe are DIRECTLY OPPOSITE each other.
5. Secure the probe in place.
6. Set high and low alarm limits for saturation and heart rate — 2% above and below the desired
limit.
7. Observe and CHANGE THE SITE every 6–8 hours if applied continuously.
8. Record the reading every 2 hours.
9. After use, switch it off, clean the probe with spirit, and keep it covered.
Pre-ductal means the RIGHT hand. Teach this alongside the Golden Hour targeted saturation table
— a post-ductal reading in the first minutes of life is a different number and a different decision.
When It Does Not Work — The Row Your Checklist Calls ‘Identifies Limitations’
10. Hypovolaemic states or low perfusion states.
11. Dyshaemoglobinaemias — carboxyhaemoglobin, methaemoglobin.
12. Optical interference from external light sources — phototherapy unit, fluorescent light,
sunlight.
Read that first one again. The shocked baby is exactly the baby whose oximeter lies to you — and
exactly the baby you most want to believe it. In shock, trust the capillary refill and the pulses, not the
number.
Complications
• Pressure necrosis on prolonged contact — which is what the 6–8 hourly site change prevents.
Common Errors
• Probe on a cold, poorly perfused foot, then treating the low number as real.
• Emitter and detector not directly opposite — a plausible but wrong number.
• Probe under a phototherapy light with no cover.
• Alarms not set, or set so wide they never sound.
• Site not changed — pressure necrosis.
• Accepting 99% in a preterm baby as good news.
• Believing it in a shocked baby.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
256 | Page
Pulse Oximeter — Disinfection
• Clean with a spirit swab and let it dry BEFORE and AFTER any use.
• Clean the probe with a spirit swab and let it dry before using on another baby.
• After use: switch it off, clean the probe, keep it covered if not required for any baby.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
257 | Page
13. Patient Safety
Facilitator approach:
● Use interactive questioning before reading goals.
● Ask:
“What types of preventable harm have you seen in newborn units?”
“What makes newborns especially vulnerable?”
Allow 5–10 minutes discussion before formally reviewing the stated goals.
Emphasize throughout that patient safety in neonatal care is proactive, systematic, and team-based.
Definition, Importance, Objectives
Facilitator Script
“Every other session on this course is about a sick baby. This one is about the system around the
baby — the labels, the checks, the handovers. It is the least dramatic session on the course and it
prevents some of the worst harm, because the harm here is harm WE cause, on top of the illness.”
Goals
Open with the two questions from your draft before reading anything: ‘what preventable harm
have you seen in newborn units?’ and ‘what makes newborns especially vulnerable?’ Allow 5–10
minutes. THEN read the goals, unchanged:
1. Improve the accuracy of patient identification.
2. Improve the effectiveness of communication among caregivers.
3. Improve the safety of using medication.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
258 | Page
Definition
Patient safety is defined as freedom from accidental injury. Ensuring patient safety involves the
establishment of operational systems and processes that minimise the likelihood of errors and
maximise the likelihood of intercepting them when they occur.
Then explain WHY neonatal care carries higher risk — the three reasons from your draft:
• Weight-based dosing — every dose is a fresh calculation that changes as the baby grows
• Rapid deterioration — small errors become life-threatening quickly
• Invasive procedures
“Read the definition again — it has two halves. Minimise the likelihood of errors, AND maximise
the likelihood of INTERCEPTING them. That second half is the whole session. We will never stop
all errors. We can build a unit that catches them before they reach the baby.”
Importance
According to the WHO, medical errors affect 1 IN 10 PATIENTS globally, with consequences
including death, lasting or temporary harm, financial setbacks, and psychosocial distress for the
patient and sometimes the caregiver.
• The distinctive, complicated SCNU environment plus the heightened vulnerability of newborns
amplifies the likelihood of error.
Landmarks: the IOM report of 1999; the IOM 2001 report — care should be effective, evidence-
based, patient-centred, timely, efficient and equitable, and patients should not be harmed by the care
they receive.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
259 | Page
Case Scenario
A 1.5 kg preterm baby received 10 TIMES the intended antibiotic dose, due to a decimal error.
Ask: where did the system fail? What could have prevented it?
ANSWER KEY
Answer key — the layers that should have caught it: the prescriber's calculation (three-
quarters of neonatal medication errors occur at the PRESCRIBING stage); the pre-administration
routine; the independent double-check by a second person; a unit formulary or emergency dose
sheet listing the correct dose by weight; adequate lighting and freedom from interruption at the
point of preparation. A tenfold decimal error passes only when ALL of these are missing or
skipped at once.
This scenario is the Swiss-cheese model in miniature, and it returns at Topic 10. Plant it here; harvest
it there. Do not let the group settle on ‘the nurse was careless’ — the manual is explicit that
individual blame is insufficient.
.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
260 | Page
13.1 Patient Identification
Facilitator Script
“Newborns are the easiest patients in the hospital to mix up. Same birth date, similar file
numbers, identical surnames, no way to tell you their name, and often a twin in the next cot.
Everything that lets an adult confirm their own identity is missing. That is why this is Goal
number one.”
Why Newborns Are High-Risk
• Born with similar birth dates and similar medical record numbers
• Multiples with identical surnames, hospitalised at the same time
• Unable to talk to confirm identity
• Lack the distinguishing physical characteristics adults have
Real errors that have resulted from misidentification:
• Two vaccines given to the same infant
• The wrong infant circumcised
• A mother's expressed breast milk fed to the wrong newborn
• A breastfed infant given formula, or a mother breastfeeding the wrong infant
Misidentification causes 11–25% of reported adverse events involving medication in the SCNU. This
is not a rare, theoretical risk — it is up to a quarter of the serious medication events.
The Rules
1. Use at LEAST TWO patient identifiers when providing any care, treatment or service.
2. Distinct naming systems — mother's first and last name plus the newborn's gender: ‘Smith, Judy
Girl’; for multiples ‘Smith, Judy Girl A’ and ‘Smith, Judy Girl B’.
3. Standardized banding — two body-site identification, barcoding, and the medical record number
on BOTH bands.
4. Communication tools — signage alerting staff to newborns with similar names.
Use a wrist or foot band ONLY if it is attached to the patient. DO NOT use the bed number, or a
band attached to the bed or cot. If a parent is present, ask them for the child's first and last name.
Match name, date of birth, and file number on the lab form to the patient before taking blood.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
261 | Page
Skill Station
CASE-BASED DISCUSSION
Scenario: twins in SCBU — Baby A 2.3 kg, Baby B 1.9 kg. The participant must confirm
identity using two identifiers, check the medication chart, and administer to the correct baby.
The facilitator watches for exactly this:
The participant should Fail the station if they
Verify the name on the band Identify the baby by which cot they are in
Check the hospital / file number — a
SECOND identifier
Use only one identifier
Confirm the number matches the chart AND
the band
Read the chart but never check the band
Confirm the dose is for THIS baby's weight Assume A and B are interchangeable
The single error to catch, from your draft: assuming identity based on INCUBATOR LOCATION.
Babies get moved. The band moves with the baby; the cot does not. Anyone who reaches for the
baby without reading the band has failed — whatever else they do correctly.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
262 | Page
13.2 Effective Communication
Facilitator Script
“Communication failures are one of the leading causes of accidental patient harm. Not
knowledge failures — communication failures. The doctor knew, the nurse knew, but the
message between them dropped a word, or was never read back. Getting everyone on the same
page has been shown to reduce adverse events.”
• The plan of care must be actively WRITTEN.
• Care providers must READ BACK verbal medical orders for verification — closed-loop.
• All written orders must use standard acronyms and abbreviations agreed across the
organisation.
Verbal and telephone orders must be read back to the person who gave them. ‘Closed-loop’
means the order is not complete until the giver has heard it repeated and confirmed it. An order
that is not read back is an error waiting for a witness.
Huddles and Handovers
• Huddles enhance communication and safety — they plan and coordinate anticipated deliveries,
discharges, transfers, surgeries, and diagnostic testing.
• A main morning meeting before rounds (nurse manager, charge nurses, discharge coordinator,
nurse practitioners, attending neonatologists, fellow, transport team, lead respiratory therapist,
social worker), and an evening meeting for overnight issues.
• Communication with parents; incident reporting.
• A rounding script for nurses — an outline for patient presentations and a shift-to-shift bedside
handoff tool.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
263 | Page
SBAR — Teach It, Then Drill It
Letter Content
S — Situation Who you are, which baby, and the problem in one sentence
B — Background The relevant history — gestation, day of life, diagnosis, current support
A — Assessment What you think is going on, and how sick the baby is right now
R —
Recommendation
What you want to happen, and how soon
Role Play: Handle handover
Scenario: a night nurse calls the doctor about a baby with RR 80/min, grunting, SpO₂ 86% on
room air.
Model SBAR — What Good Sounds Like
S: ‘This is [name], the nurse in SCBU. I'm calling about Baby Fatima A — she has sudden
respiratory distress and I need you to come.’ B: ‘She's a 34-week baby, day 1, was stable on room
air until 20 minutes ago.’ A: ‘Now her respiratory rate is 80, she's grunting, and her saturation is
86% in room air. I think she's in respiratory distress and needs support now.’ R: ‘I've started
oxygen and put her on the monitor. I need you to come and assess for CPAP within the next few
minutes — can you come now?’
Debrief against four questions:
• Was the situation clearly stated?
• Was background included?
• Was the assessment clear?
• Was a recommendation made — with a timeframe?
The commonest failure: the nurse lists numbers but never says what they WANT. ‘Her sats are 86’ is
a fact; ‘I need you here in five minutes to assess for CPAP’ is a recommendation. Drill the R — it is
the letter people drop under stress.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
264 | Page
13.3 Medication Administration
Facilitator Script
“Medication is where this unit most often hurts a baby by accident. The dose changes every time
the baby gains weight, the margin for error is tiny, and the contents of a needle hub alone can
overdose a premature baby. This is the highest-yield twenty minutes of the whole safety
session.”.
Why Medication Is the Sharp End
• Errors occur MOSTLY in premature infants under 1500 g — they need the most
pharmacological support.
• Enteral nutrition and medication errors account for 13.3% of potentially harmful SCNU errors.
• Three-quarters of neonatal medication errors occur at the PRESCRIBING stage.
• The contents of the DEAD SPACE in a needle hub and syringe may be enough to overdose a
premature neonate.
• The three environmental factors: under-illumination of the preparation area, distractions, and
interruptions while handling medications.
Every prescription needs a specific calculation, and it must CHANGE as the newborn gains weight.
Yesterday's correct dose is today's error. Re-calculate against today's weight, every time
The Preadministration Process
1. A routine preadministration process before giving ANY medication.
2. Double-check — right PATIENT, right DRUG, right DOSE, right TIME, right ROUTE —
before administration.
3. Replace concentrated stock with a single-dose system.
4. Increase the number of personnel who must check for accuracy.
5. Clinical pharmacists monitoring orders; a SCNU-specific formulary; emergency dose sheets
listing calculated doses by weight.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
265 | Page
The manual's paradox, worth teaching out loud: increasing the number of checkers helps —
UNLESS each assumes someone else has already checked. A double-check where the second person
rubber-stamps the first is not a double-check. It must be INDEPENDENT.
Practical — Calculation and the Independent Double-Check
Worked example (from your draft): Baby weight 1.8 kg, antibiotic 50 mg/kg.
The facilitator watches, per your draft, for:
• Decimal placement — 50 × 1.8 = 90 mg, not 900 mg and not 9 mg
• Unit conversion — mg vs mL after dilution
• Dilution calculation — the concentration in the syringe, and the volume to draw
How to Run the Double-Check
1. Participant 1 calculates the dose and prepares it — working alone.
2. Participant 2 calculates INDEPENDENTLY, without seeing the first answer, then compares.
3. They reconcile any difference before ‘administration’ — and the discrepancy, if any, is the
teaching moment.
4. Document after administration — not before.
Make participant 2 turn away and calculate from the order and the weight, THEN compare. If
they just glance at participant 1's number and nod, stop the exercise and explain why that nod is
exactly the failure the manual warns about.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
266 | Page

13.5 Clinical Alarm Safety
Facilitator Script
“Alarms exist to save babies, and they harm them in two opposite ways. Set wrong, they miss the
sick baby. Set to shriek at everything, they train you to ignore them — and one day the alarm you
silence is real. Alarm fatigue is a patient safety problem, not a nuisance problem.”
The Two Rules
1. Alarm settings must be APPROPRIATE — set to the individual baby, not left on a default.
2. NEVER silence an alarm without ASSESSING the baby first.
Silencing before assessing is the cardinal alarm error. The alarm is a question about the baby —
‘is he alright?’ — and the only valid way to answer it is to look at the baby, not to press the
button.
Bedside Simulation (from your draft) — With Answer Key
Scenario: the monitor alarms HR 85/min in a TERM baby.
The participant must:
3. ASSESS the baby — colour, tone, breathing, activity — first.
4. Check the leads and the probe — is this real, or a signal artefact?
5. Interpret clinically.
ANSWER KEY
The reasoning: HR 85 in a TERM baby is below the usual range — but a well, pink, active term
baby with a good trace may simply have a low resting or sleeping heart rate, OR this may be a
genuine bradycardia. You cannot tell from the number. Assess the baby AND confirm the signal
is real (lead contact, probe position) BEFORE acting. The wrong answer is to silence it; the other
wrong answer is to treat a number without looking at the baby.
Discuss alarm fatigue explicitly: on a busy unit where every alarm is a false alarm, staff learn not
to respond — and that learned deafness is what kills the baby whose alarm was real. Appropriate
limits per baby are how you keep alarms meaningful.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
268 | Page
13.6 Reduce Healthcare-Associated Infection
This topic is taught in FULL in the Neonatal Sepsis — Surveillance, Prevention and Stewardship
session (Module 2, Section 10). Do not duplicate it here. Here it is framed as a patient-safety goal;
there it is practised as skill stations.
The Patient-Safety Framing
• Ask (from your draft): where are infection risks highest in your unit? Which procedures carry
the highest risk?
• In small groups, walk through: hand hygiene demonstration; equipment handling; invasive line
insertion preparation.
“Every healthcare-associated infection is, by definition, harm we caused. That is why it sits inside
patient safety and not only inside sepsis. The hand you do not wash is a patient-safety failure before
it is ever a microbiology result.”
For the full technique, scored checklists (hand hygiene, blood culture, stewardship) and the four skill
stations, use the Section 10 guide.

13.7 Medical Errors and the Swiss-Cheese Model
Facilitator Script
“The most important idea in this whole session is this: serious harm is almost never one person's
mistake. It is several small holes, in several defences, lining up on the same day. Which means
the fix is never just ‘be more careful.’ The fix is building more layers, so the holes stop lining
up.”
The Model
• Errors result from MULTIPLE system failures, not a single act.
• Individual blame is insufficient — and it is actively harmful, because it stops you finding the
other holes.
• Each defence is a slice of cheese with holes; harm occurs only when the holes in every slice
line up and a hazard passes straight through.
Draw it: several slices, each with holes in different places. Most days, a hole in one slice is
blocked by the solid part of the next. Harm happens on the rare day the holes align. Every check
in this session — two identifiers, read-back, the independent double-check — is one more slice.
Group Exercise
Case: a preterm infant received the wrong IV fluid concentration.
Participants identify the holes. Your draft names four — here is what each looks like and which
slice it belongs to:
The hole The defence that failed
Communication
breakdown
The plan of care was not clearly written or read back — the order
itself was ambiguous (Topic 6)
Labelling issue The fluid bag or syringe was not labelled clearly, or looked like
another — no barrier to picking the wrong one (Topics 5 & 7)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
270 | Page
The hole The defence that failed
Lack of double-check No independent second person verified the concentration before it
ran (Topic 7)
Staffing issue Too few staff, or too rushed — the interruptions and distractions the
manual names as error factors (Topic 7)
The conclusion to reach: no single one of these caused the harm. Any ONE slice, intact, would
have stopped it — a clear label, or a real double-check, or a written order, or a less rushed nurse. The
harm required ALL of them to fail together. So the improvement plan has four items, not one, and
none of them is ‘the nurse should be more careful.’
Link this back to the tenfold-dose scenario from Topic 3 — same structure, different holes. When the
group sees that both cases are the same shape, the model has landed.
Critical Incident Analysis
• When an event happens, conduct a thorough examination based on DATA and FACT, not
assumption.
• Disseminate the lessons — an incident analysed and not shared has no preventive value.
“The purpose of looking at an error is never to find who to blame. It is to find which slice to thicken,
so the next baby is caught by a layer that was missing this time.”
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
271 | Page

14. Non-Invasive Ventilation
This chapter builds on the CPAP equipment SOP (taught in the Prematurity — RDS session and
detailed in the Equipment volume). Here the device becomes a decision: who needs NIV, which
mode, what settings, how to read the response, and when it has failed.
Learning Objectives
1. Identify respiratory disorders requiring non-invasive ventilation (NIV).
2. Explore non-invasive ventilation options and consider the pros and cons.
3. Explore monitoring and assessment strategies for neonates on NIV.
And, added for competence:
4. Initiate CPAP safely at the correct starting settings.
5. Adjust CPAP and FiO₂ using the traffic-light protocol.
6. Recognise NIV failure early and escalate.
7. Prevent and stage nasal injury.
8. Wean and remove CPAP by protocol.
Materials
• CPAP machine (bubble / Vayu) and HFNC circuit · manikin or doll · caps/bonnets in several
sizes
• Nasal prongs in all sizes · transparent measuring tape · granuflex / barrier dressing · saline
• Pulse oximeter · blender · humidifier · orogastric tubes · blood-gas access if available
• Monitoring charts · the traffic-light protocol poster · nasal-injury staging photos · copies of all
checklists
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
273 | Page
14.1 Recognising the Baby Who Needs NIV
Why NIV, and Why It Replaced the Ventilator
Over the past few decades, invasive mechanical ventilation has been replaced by NIV, because
invasive ventilation is associated with major morbidities: bronchopulmonary dysplasia (BPD),
pulmonary hypertension, increased susceptibility to respiratory infection, neurodevelopmental
impairment, and cerebral palsy.
Definition: NIV refers to any method of providing respiratory support where an endotracheal tube is
NOT used.
“Every day a baby spends on a ventilator is a day their lungs are being injured to keep them alive.
NIV is how we support breathing without paying that price. The whole of this chapter is about
staying on the near side of the tube.”
Scenario 1 — The Baby Who Is Tiring
A preterm baby with respiratory distress. Increasing oxygen has been tried. The baby is now
working hard to breathe.
Facilitator questions:
• What signs suggest respiratory FAILURE, not just distress?
• Is oxygen alone sufficient?
• What is the next appropriate intervention?
Expected Participant Reasoning
• Persistent hypoxia despite oxygen
• High respiratory rate with severe work of breathing
• Prematurity + RDS risk
• Baby tiring → impending failure
Correct Action — Facilitator Emphasises
Start CPAP IMMEDIATELY. Do NOT wait for further deterioration. Do NOT increase oxygen
blindly.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
274 | Page
Key Teaching Points
• RR > 70 + retractions + poor response to oxygen = NIV indicated.
• Early CPAP prevents intubation.
• Delayed action worsens outcomes.
Common Errors to Correct
• Increasing oxygen flow repeatedly
• Waiting for consultant review
• No baseline documentation before NIV
FACILITATOR HINT
the trap in this scenario is that raising the oxygen LOOKS like doing something. Saturation may
even nudge up for a while. Meanwhile the work of breathing is unchanged and the baby is tiring
toward apnoea. Oxygen treats the number; CPAP treats the breathing. Say that out loud.
Indications for NIV
1. Respiratory Distress Syndrome (RDS) 6. Apnoea of prematurity — CPAP is the
standard
2. Transient Tachypnoea of Newborn (TTN) 7. Congenital Diaphragmatic Hernia (CDH)
3. Meconium Aspiration Syndrome (MAS) 8. Post-operative support
4. Persistent Pulmonary Hypertension (PPHN) 9. Neuromuscular disorders affecting
respiratory muscles
5. Bronchiolitis (often RSV) 10. Tailor the choice of method and settings to
the clinical need
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
275 | Page
Contraindications — When NIV Is the Wrong Answer
1. Severe respiratory distress, especially with cardiovascular instability — invasive ventilation may
be required.
2. Haemodynamic instability — positive pressure can affect cardiac function.
3. Severe air leak syndrome — pneumothorax or pneumomediastinum; NIV could worsen it.
4. Facial abnormalities preventing a proper seal or interface placement.
5. Unstable neurological status — may not tolerate treatment.
6. Upper airway obstruction.
7. Gastrointestinal conditions — abdominal wall anomalies or massive distension.
8. Inability to protect the airway.
9. Uncooperative neonate — extreme agitation or non-compliance.
The manual's rule: always consult the neonatal specialist / senior, and consider the individual
patient's clinical status — contraindications can vary. NIV is not for the baby who is already too sick
for it.
FACILITATOR HINT
hold contraindication 3 up next to Scenario 4 (later today). A tension pneumothorax is BOTH a
reason NIV can fail AND a reason it should never have been pushed harder. The same finding
appears in both places — that is not a coincidence, it is the lesson.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
276 | Page
14.2 NIV Modalities
Facilitator Script
“There are six names on this list and they frighten people. But they are only three ideas: flow
with no reliable pressure (HFNC), one pressure (CPAP), and two pressures (everything else).
Learn the three ideas and the six names sort themselves out.”
The Modalities — One Table
Mode Pressure levels What it is
HFNC Variable,
unreliable
Heated humidified high flow, 2–8 L/min via loose
nasal prongs. Washes out dead space, reduces work of
breathing. Pressure NOT reliably measurable
CPAP ONE (PEEP) Constant distending pressure throughout inspiration
and expiration. The workhorse of neonatal NIV
NIPPV TWO (PIP +
PEEP)
CPAP with superimposed inflations to a set PIP, via a
ventilator. May be synchronized. Used when CPAP is
failing
BiPAP TWO (lower) High and low pressures differing by no more than 3–4
cmH₂O; longer Ti (0.5–1 s); rates 10–30/min. Often
considered the same as NIPPV
nHFOV Oscillatory Small tidal volumes at supraphysiological rates for
better CO₂ elimination. Infrequently used; needs
further evaluation
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
277 | Page
The Evidence — What to Teach, and What Not to Overstate
Comparison What the evidence says (as written in the manual)
Early CPAP vs
mechanical
ventilation
Prophylactic or very early CPAP reduced BPD, the combined outcome
of death or BPD, and mechanical ventilation
NIPPV vs CPAP Early NIPPV reduced respiratory failure, the need for intubation,
treatment failure and mechanical ventilation — without increasing
complications (Cochrane)
HFNC as
PRIMARY support
No significant difference vs CPAP in most outcomes EXCEPT a
HIGHER treatment failure rate. Decreased nasal injury. No robust
evidence in extreme low gestational age
HFNC post-
extubation
No significant difference vs CPAP in extubation failure. Decreased
nasal injury
The one line to make participants remember: HFNC is gentler on the nose but fails more often as
PRIMARY support, and the pressure it delivers is UNKNOWN. It is a comfort trade-off, not a free
upgrade.
Advantages of NIV — Ask Before You Tell
• Preserves airway integrity; avoids invasive ventilation and its complications
• Reduces ventilator-associated pneumonia and lung injury
• Enhances parental involvement — the baby stays accessible for kangaroo care
• Improves respiratory muscle function by minimising work of breathing
• Enhances gas exchange — CPAP creates and maintains PEEP, recruits alveoli
• Decreases risk of chronic lung disease; better tolerated in preterms
• Decreases need for intubation; flexible; allows earlier weaning
Disadvantages — The Reasons This Chapter Has Six More Sessions
• Interface challenges — leak or skin breakdown
• Gastric distension → regurgitation and aspiration (why an OG tube is mandatory)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
278 | Page
• Nasal injury — trauma, irritation, septal necrosis, especially with prolonged use
• Discomfort and agitation
• Difficulty with synchrony — no flow sensor as in invasive ventilation
• Limited ventilatory support — NIV failure can occur
• Risk of air leak — the pressure reaching the lungs cannot be measured; auto-PEEP
• Needs SKILLED monitoring — bedside nurses must be trained in NIV care
FACILITATOR HINT
point at the last disadvantage. ‘Needs skilled monitoring.’ That is you. That is the entire reason
this is a full-day session and not a device demonstration. The machine is easy. The watching is
the skill.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

14.3 Safe CPAP / HFNC Initiation
Facilitator Script
“The prerequisite for CPAP is good respiratory effort. CPAP splints an airway open — it does
not breathe for the baby. A baby with poor respiratory effort does not need CPAP; they need
PPV. Get that one thing wrong and everything after it is dangerous.”
PREREQUISITE: the most important prerequisite for starting CPAP is the presence of GOOD
RESPIRATORY EFFORT.
Demonstration — CPAP Step by Step
1. Confirm the indication.
2. Explain the procedure to the mother.
3. Check the equipment — assemble, connect air and oxygen, humidifier with distilled water,
ensure the heating wire is in the inspiratory limb.
4. Choose the correct nasal interface — the biggest prong that fits the nostril without distending
it (size table below).
5. Fit the cap covering up to the ears, ears lying FLAT under it. A cap too small causes
oedema.
6. Apply granuflex / barrier dressing under the nose and over the bridge to prevent necrosis.
Moisten the prongs with saline before insertion.
7. Secure the interface — the bridge of the prongs must NOT abut the columella and must NOT
blanch the skin.
8. INSERT AN OROGASTRIC TUBE — CPAP inflates the stomach.
9. Start CPAP at the correct settings (below).
10. Ensure continuous bubbling in both phases of respiration.
11. Document the time and the settings.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
280 | Page
Nasal Prong Size by Birth Weight
Size Birth weight Size Birth weight
0 < 700 g 3 2000–3000 g
1 700–1000 g 4 3000–4000 g
2 1000–2000 g 5 > 4000 g
Too TIGHT causes pressure necrosis. Too LOOSE causes a large air leak and no CPAP. Periodically
check the nasal mucosa for hyperaemia and the columella for blanching.
Starting Settings — By Indication (Module 3 initiation protocol)
Preterm with RD Recurrent apnoea Post-extubation
CPAP 5 cmH₂O 4–5 cmH₂O 5 cmH₂O
FiO₂ 30% < 25% (room air if
SpO₂ target met)
As on the ventilator
(titrate to target
SpO₂)
Flow 6–8 L/min 6–8 L/min 6–8 L/min
nasal CPAP is usually started at 5 cmH₂O, flow 5 L/min, FiO₂ 30%; then titrated — pressure to
reduce work of breathing, flow to obtain adequate continuous bubbling, and FiO₂ to the saturation
target (90–95% for preterm neonates).
FACILITATOR HINT
there is a documented discrepancy across the manuals on the SpO₂ target — 89–92% (Level II),
90–94% (MSSN practical), 90–95% (Module 3). Do not paper over it. Tell participants to state
and follow THEIR unit's written protocol, and to write which one on the chart.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
281 | Page
Skills Station Tasks (CPAP assembling)
• Assemble CPAP and do the wall connections.
• Apply the interface correctly.
• State the initial settings.
• Identify the danger signs after initiation.
• Safety emphasis: a poorly fitted CPAP causes nasal injury, air leaks, and treatment failure.
The fit IS the therapy.

14.4 Monitoring the Baby on NIV
Facilitator Script
“Never judge NIV by oxygen alone — look at the baby. Write that on the board and leave it
there. A saturation of 92 tells you almost nothing on its own. The work of breathing tells you
everything.”
Scenario 3 — Response to CPAP
30 minutes after CPAP initiation: RR 62/min · HR 148/min · SpO₂ 92% on FiO₂ 0.30 · chest
indrawing mild · baby more active.
Facilitator questions:
• Is this improvement or failure?
• What parameters tell you the baby is responding?
• What should be documented now?
• What is the plan for the next 2 hours?
ANSWER KEY
IMPROVEMENT. Compare to a distressed baby: the rate has settled, the indrawing is now only
mild, the baby is more active, and the saturation is on target at a LOW FiO₂ of 0.30. Every trend
is the right way.
The parameters that prove it: reduced work of breathing (mild indrawing), a settling
respiratory rate, improved activity — not the saturation alone.
Document: the settings, the full set of vitals, the work of breathing, and the time.
Plan: continue CPAP, maintain monitoring, avoid unnecessary changes.
Teaching point: this is the GREEN column of the traffic light. The instinct of an anxious team is
to keep adjusting a baby who is getting better. Do not. ‘Avoid unnecessary changes’ is an active
decision, not passivity.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
284 | Page
The CPAP Adjustment Traffic Light (Module 3)
INCREASE CPAP INCREASE FiO₂ NO CHANGE
CRITERIA:
• Retractions ++ (severe)
• SpO₂ < 90%
• Chest X-ray < 6 spaces (30
min post-CPAP)
ACTION:
• Increase CPAP by 1 cmH₂O
steps
• Reassess after each
adjustment
• Monitor for improvement
CRITERIA:
• Retractions + (mild) or
absent
• SpO₂ < 90%
• Adequate CPAP pressure
ACTION:
• Increase FiO₂ by 5% steps
• For every 10% FiO₂ rise:
reassess need for 1 cmH₂O
CPAP increase
• Monitor SpO₂ response
CRITERIA:
• Baby comfortable
• Minimal / no retractions
• Normal CRT & BP
• SpO₂ 90–99%
• Good bubbling
• Audible breath sounds
ACTION:
• Continue current settings
• Maintain monitoring
• Document stable status
The golden rule of adjustment: FiO₂ and pressure go hand in hand. If there is a mismatch, something
is wrong. Bring down the FiO₂ FIRST, then the pressure.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
285 | Page
Monitoring — What You Check, and How Often
Frequency Check
EVERY HOUR (visual) The nose is not squashed or pushed upwards · the eyes are clearly
visible · tapes not too tight, no indentation, pitting or ocular oedema
At least 4-HOURLY
(physical)
Hat fit — not too tight/loose, not rubbing · remove prongs/mask to

Remember (manual): remove prongs/mask by LOOSENING the tapes, not by pulling them off the

Document whenever the nose is checked.
• Also monitor: capillary refill, blood pressure, urine output; tone, activity and response; a
respiratory distress score (e.g. Downe's); chest X-ray at start and if deterioration occurs.
Feeding on NIV
• Babies are initially kept nil per mouth until stabilised.
• Thereafter, commence feeds by orogastric tube.
The NCPAP System — Four Things Right, Every Round
• Tubes in the nose
• Nasal airway clear
• Mouth closed
• Neck slightly extended
Ward Round (your draft) — Run It Live
• Conduct a ward round of 3–4 patients on NCPAP (or simulated stations).
• For each baby, demonstrate: pressure · FiO₂ and oxygen saturation · clinical signs · any
complications · whether adjustments are needed.
• Each pair of participants answers for each patient, then places the baby in a traffic-light column
and justifies it.
