14.5 Nasal Injury: Prevent, Inspect, Stage
Facilitator Script
“Nasal injury is the price of CPAP, and it is almost entirely preventable. A baby who survives
RDS and goes home with a hole in the septum was failed — not by the disease, but by the
watching. This is the session that prevents it.”
Why It Happens
• Nasal prongs cause trauma, irritation or septal necrosis, particularly with PROLONGED use.
• The commonest mechanism is a prong bridge pressing on the columella — the strip of skin
between the nostrils — which blanches, then breaks down.
Prevention — Built Into Every Step Already
1. Correct prong size — biggest that fits without distending.
2. Barrier dressing (granuflex) under the nose and over the bridge.
3. The prong bridge must NOT abut the columella; must not blanch.
4. Remove prongs/mask 4-hourly to rest the nose — more often if the baby's condition dictates.
5. Loosen tapes to remove; never pull off the face.
6. Inspect and DOCUMENT at every check.
Inspect — What You Are Looking For
Site Signs of injury
Columella / septum Redness, blanching, indentation, altered shape, breakdown, bleeding
Nares Redness, skin breakdown, bruising
Around the eyes Ocular oedema, pitting, indentation from tapes
Ears Creasing, folding, redness, bruising, swelling, discharge, bleeding
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
287 | Page
Stage It — A Simple Working Scale
Stage Appearance Action
I Persistent redness / hyperaemia,
blanching that recovers
Reassess fit NOW; barrier dressing;
increase rest periods; document
II Superficial skin breakdown,
bruising, altered shape
Change interface type (alternate
prongs/mask); dressing; senior review;
document with time
III Necrosis — septal erosion, deep
breakdown, bleeding
Stop that interface; escalate to senior
immediately; consider alternative NIV
mode; wound care; incident report
Blanching is stage I and it is your window. Once the columella is white under the prong bridge,
injury has started — act on the blanch, not on the ulcer. By the time it is stage III you are managing a
complication that should never have happened.
FACILITATOR HINT
rotate the interface. If your unit has both prongs and a nasal mask, alternating them gives each
pressure point a rest — the same logic as turning a bed-bound patient. Show the group how to
swap without losing the pressure.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
288 | Page
14.6 Troubleshooting, Failure and Weaning
Scenario 4 — Sudden Deterioration, With Answer Key
Baby on CPAP for 6 hours: RR 84/min · HR 182/min · SpO₂ 85% despite FiO₂ 0.60 ·
asymmetric chest movement · reduced breath sounds on the right · baby pale and lethargic.
Facilitator prompts:
• Is this CPAP failure or a complication?
• What is your FIRST concern?
• What immediate actions are required?
ANSWER KEY
This is a COMPLICATION, not simple failure. Asymmetric chest movement + reduced breath
sounds on one side + sudden deterioration = PNEUMOTHORAX until proved otherwise — a
known complication of CPAP (auto-PEEP, air leak).
First concern: tension pneumothorax. Do not reach for the FiO₂ dial.
Immediate actions: call for help · assess for tension · transilluminate · if unstable, needle
decompression BEFORE the X-ray (cross-reference: needle thoracotomy, taught in Prematurity
— RDS) · prepare for chest drain · escalate to the paediatrician · this baby may now need
intubation.
The trap: this baby's saturation is 85% on 60% oxygen, so the reflex is to turn the oxygen to
100% and increase the pressure. In a tension pneumothorax, increasing the pressure makes it
WORSE. Recognise the asymmetry first. Every group has someone who reaches for the dial —
let the debrief teach it.
When Has NCPAP Failed? (Module 3 / MSSN)
Any ONE of these is CPAP failure — all require immediate consultation with a paediatrician for
further management and retrieval if possible:
1. FiO₂ rising to above 0.6
2. Respiratory acidosis — pH < 7.28 with rising PaCO₂ > 60 mmHg
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
289 | Page
3. Development of recurrent apnoea requiring stimulation
4. Worsening indrawing / grunting / tachypnoea
5. Agitation not relieved by simple measures
FACILITATOR HINT
note that four of these five are about the BABY, and only one is a number on the blender. A baby
who needs more and more oxygen, or who keeps having apnoeas, has failed CPAP even if a
single gas looks acceptable. Failure is a trajectory, not a threshold.
Complications to Look Out For
• Pneumothorax · pulmonary interstitial emphysema — from excessive pressure and over-
distension
• Decreased venous return, increased pulmonary vascular resistance, reduced cardiac output
• Nasal trauma / septal necrosis
• Gastric distension → regurgitation and aspiration
• Agitation · continued deterioration
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
290 | Page
14.7 Weaning CPAP
Criteria for Weaning
• Absence of significant episodes of apnoea
• Minimal work of breathing
• Decreasing oxygen requirements to less than 30%
The Four Steps
Step Phase Action
1 Initial FiO₂ reduction When SpO₂ > 95%: reduce FiO₂ by 5%
steps. Continue until ready for CPAP
reduction
2 CPAP reduction phase When FiO₂ reduced to ~50% AND
retractions mild or absent: reduce CPAP by
1 cmH₂O steps. Goal: CPAP to 5 cm, FiO₂
to 50%
3 Further FiO₂ reduction At CPAP 5 cm, FiO₂ 50%: reduce FiO₂ by
5% steps. Goal: FiO₂ < 30%
4 Final weaning & removal CPAP 5 → 4 cm when FiO₂ < 30%.
REMOVE when FiO₂ < 25% AND CPAP at
4 cm
The rule underneath all four steps: decrease FiO₂ BEFORE decreasing pressure. Wean the oxygen
down first; the pressure is what is holding the lung open, so it comes off last.
• Trial off NCPAP once the baby is stable for 6–12 hours on CPAP 5 cmH₂O, FiO₂ < 0.3, RR <
70.
• Reduce CPAP by 1 cmH₂O every 6 hours until at 5 cmH₂O once RR < 70, FiO₂ < 0.3, and less
effort.
• If CPAP was given for apnoea, it can be removed within 24–48 hours of an apnoea-free period.
FACILITATOR HINT
warn the group — it is not uncommon to see a mild rise in respiratory rate and inspired oxygen in
the FIRST HOUR after coming off CPAP. That is expected, not failure. Do not put a baby
straight back on CPAP for a wobble at 20 minutes; give the hour.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026

15. Invasive Ventilation
Learning Objectives
1. Know the basic physiology of respiration.
2. Be familiar with common ventilation terminology.
3. Understand the concept and common principles of mechanical ventilation.
4. Be aware of common types and modes of mechanical ventilation.
5. Know the common indications of mechanical ventilation.
6. Develop the skills to use the ventilator safely from initiation to weaning and extubation.
7. Know how to improve the care of ventilated babies and to monitor them.
8. Be familiar with common complications and how to prevent them.
“The responsibility of the intensivist is to optimise ventilation and prevent the harmful effects of
OVER- or UNDER-ventilation. Read that twice. The ventilator is the only treatment on this course
that harms in both directions — too much and too little are both injuries.”
Materials
• Neonatal ventilator(s) · test lung · manikin · ventilator circuits · humidifier
• Self-inflating and/or T-piece resuscitator for the disconnect-and-bag drill · manometer
• Blood-gas examples (printed) · chest X-ray examples · the disease-settings table poster
• Caffeine, surfactant (for cross-reference) · CPAP setup for post-extubation · copies of all
checklists
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
292 | Page
15.1 Who Needs Invasive Ventilation
Facilitator Script
“This morning you learned everything you could do to keep a baby OFF a ventilator — CPAP,
the whole NIV chapter. This session begins where that one ends: the baby for whom non-invasive
support was not enough. Putting a baby on a ventilator is not a failure of the NIV chapter. It is the
correct next step, made in time.”
The Decision Point — When NIV Is No Longer Enough
The manual's indications for intubation and invasive ventilation, drawn from across the disease
sections:
• Increased FiO₂ and CPAP inadequate to maintain gas exchange (RDS)
• Infants who TIRE from the increased work of breathing
• Worsening clinical distress; increased oxygen requirements
• Worsening hypoxaemia / acidosis
• Clinical instability and intolerance of CPAP
• Apnoea severe enough to warrant ventilator support, even without lung disease
Cross-reference the NIV failure criteria: FiO₂ > 0.6, pH < 7.28 with PaCO₂ > 60, recurrent apnoea
needing stimulation, worsening work of breathing, agitation not relieved. The baby who meets NIV
failure is the baby who arrives in THIS session.
Case — The Baby CPAP Could Not Hold
A 28-week baby, day 1, on CPAP 6 cmH₂O and FiO₂ 0.55 for RDS. Over the last hour: rising
oxygen requirement, now 0.65; a blood gas shows pH 7.24, PaCO₂ 64; two apnoeas needing
stimulation; visibly tiring.
Ask:
• Has CPAP failed? Which criteria are met?
• What is the next step — and what must happen before you set a single ventilator dial?
• What surfactant decision runs alongside this?
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
293 | Page
ANSWER KEY
CPAP has failed. Three criteria met: FiO₂ now > 0.6; pH < 7.28 with PaCO₂ > 60; recurrent
apnoea requiring stimulation. This is not a borderline call.
Next step: intubation and invasive ventilation. Before touching a dial — the intubation itself
(taught in the Advance Airway session), confirmed tube position, and a plan. HFNC during
intubation improves first-attempt success and stability (cross-reference NIV).
Surfactant: this RDS baby who is now intubated should receive surfactant — early rescue. The
ventilator and the surfactant are one decision, not two (cross-reference Prematurity — RDS).
FACILITATOR HINT
the teaching point is that ‘put him on the ventilator’ is the START of the work, not the end. The
dangerous team relaxes once the tube is in. The next four sessions are everything that happens
after that tube is secured.

15.2 How a Ventilator Breath Works
Interactive teaching
Facilitator Script
“The ventilator frightens people because of the acronyms. But there are only three questions any
ventilator breath answers: who starts it, what limits it, and what stops it. Trigger, limit, cycle.
Learn those three and every mode on the machine becomes readable.”
The Three Breath Sequences (3.3)
Sequence What it means
CMV — Continuous
Mandatory Ventilation
ALL breaths are mandatory, AND spontaneous breaths do NOT occur
between mandatory breaths
IMV — Intermittent
Mandatory Ventilation
Mandatory breaths, with spontaneous breaths allowed between them
CSV — Continuous
Spontaneous
Ventilation
All breaths are spontaneous
The Three Phase Variables — The Spine of the Whole Session
Variable What it governs
TRIGGER Regulates the START of each breath — who begins it, the baby or the
machine
LIMIT SUSTAINS inspiration — what caps it (a pressure, or a volume)
CYCLE Controls the TERMINATION of inspiration — what ends it
“Trigger, limit, cycle. Start, sustain, stop. Write it on the board and point at it every time a new mode
comes up for the rest of the day.”
The Four Control Variables (3.4)
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
295 | Page
Only ONE variable can be controlled and limited to a peak during inspiration — it becomes the
independent variable, and the others vary with the baby's compliance and resistance. The controlled
variable's waveform is fixed breath to breath.
Control What is controlled Where used
Pressure Control (PC) The pressure waveform — a square
wave — and the pressure limit. Volume
and flow vary
COMMONLY USED IN
NEONATES
Volume Control (VC) The volume waveform and the volume
limit. Pressure and flow vary
Commonly used in PICU
Flow Control The flow waveform; volume and
pressure vary
Paediatric patients —
NOT neonates
Time Control Flow, volume and pressure during the
set Ti depend on respiratory mechanics
HFOV is the example
In neonates, PRESSURE CONTROL is the common mode. The square pressure waveform is the one
to recognise. When you set PIP and PEEP, you are setting a pressure and letting the delivered
volume vary — which is why you watch the chest and the tidal volume, not just the dial.
FACILITATOR HINT
draw the square wave. PIP is the top of the square, PEEP is the bottom. In pressure control you
choose the height of the square; the baby's lung decides how much air fits inside it. On a stiff
RDS lung, the same square delivers less volume — which is the whole reason the next session
exists.
The Modes Used in Neonates
The Paediatric Mechanical Ventilation Consensus Conference could recommend NO specific mode
over another. Mode choice is guided by clinician experience, patient pathophysiology, institutional
protocol, and ventilator availability.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
296 | Page
Mode In one line
Assist Control (AC) Every breath — triggered by the baby or the machine — is fully
supported to the set pressure/volume
SIMV Synchronized Intermittent Mandatory Ventilation — a set number
of supported breaths, synchronized; the baby breathes spontaneously
between them
Pressure Support (PS) The baby triggers; the ventilator supports each spontaneous breath
with a set pressure
Dual / Targeted modes Volume-targeted — the ventilator adjusts pressure breath-to-breath
to hit a set tidal volume
Do not let the group get lost choosing a favourite mode. The manual is explicit that none is proven
superior. What matters is that they can read whatever mode their own unit's ventilator offers, using
trigger-limit-cycle.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
297 | Page
15.3 High-Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation (HFOV)
Teaching
Facilitator Script
“Conventional ventilation gives big-ish breaths at a normal-ish rate. HFOV does the opposite —
tiny breaths, smaller than the dead space, hundreds of times a minute, on top of one constant
pressure that holds the lung open. It exists for one reason: to keep the lung open without the
damaging pressures that conventional ventilation would need.”
What It Is
• Rapid ventilation rates with small tidal volumes (often LESS than anatomical dead space).
• Active inspiratory AND expiratory phases.
• A constant distending airway pressure (MAP) is applied to the alveoli, maximising functional
residual capacity and V/Q matching, over which small tidal volumes are superimposed at high
rate.
Indications
1. Alternative to conventional ventilation, as a PRIMARY mode.
2. When adequate oxygenation and/or ventilation cannot be achieved on conventional ventilation
— as a RESCUE mode.
3. To minimise lung injury by avoiding high inspiratory pressures or FiO₂ on conventional
ventilation.
Disease settings where HFOV may suit: RDS · chronic lung disease of prematurity · MAS · CDH ·
air-leak syndrome with pulmonary interstitial emphysema · pneumonia · pulmonary haemorrhage ·
hypoplastic lungs.
The One Danger to Teach Hard
The disadvantage of high MAP is OVER-INFLATION of the lungs — which decreases cardiac
output, reduces BP, raises pulmonary vascular resistance, decreases venous return, and causes
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
298 | Page
pulmonary leaks. It must be identified in time by clinical assessment, BP and chest X-ray, and
corrected by reducing the MAP.
“HFOV holds the lung open with one steady pressure. Too little and the lung collapses; too much
and you strangle the circulation. The chest X-ray and the blood pressure are how you tell the
difference — not the saturation alone.”
Weaning HFOV
• Primarily by reducing the MAP as compliance improves. Reduce FiO₂ as tolerated; once below
~30%, wean MAP in 1–2 cmH₂O increments (except when over-inflation is evident).
• In air-leak syndromes, reducing MAP may take priority over weaning FiO₂.
• Reduce ΔP (amplitude) in increments of 2–4 according to the PaCO₂; observe the chest wall for
vibration.
• Extubate from HFOV to CPAP, or transition to conventional ventilation first (set PEEP, then a
PIP giving a MAP 1–2 cmH₂O below the HFOV setting).
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
299 | Page
15.4 Settings: General, Then by Disease
Before You Set Anything — Define the Goal (3.6)
The manual insists the goal is chosen before the dials. Assess the pathophysiology (RDS vs MAS,
post-surfactant, lung hypoplasia), the clinical findings, the blood gas (oxygenation vs ventilation),
and the imaging. Then define ONE goal:
● Improve V/Q mismatch — but avoid oxygen toxicity, OR
● Recruit the lung — but avoid lung trauma, OR
● Support respiration — and plan for extubation as soon as indicated.
“You cannot set a ventilator without a goal, any more than you can set off on a journey without a
destination. ‘Improve the gas’ is not a goal. ‘Recruit this atelectatic lung while keeping the plateau
under 30’ is a goal.”
General Starting Point — Pressure Control (3.6)
Parameter Usual starting value
PEEP 4–6 cmH₂O, usually 5
PIP 14–25 cmH₂O, usually 16–20
Rate 30–60/min
Ti 0.30–0.40 s
I:E ratio At least 1:2
These are the GENERAL starting numbers. The next table — the disease-specific one — is where
they get adjusted to the actual lung in front of you. Teach the general set first so participants have an
anchor, then show how each disease pulls the numbers in a particular direction.

This is the manual's disease comparison table. It is the single most useful page in the chapter. Walk

(ball-valve air-trapping, pneumothorax risk), they will reconstruct the numbers. If they only
memorised '30', they will apply it to an RDS baby and do harm.

15.5 Caring for the Ventilated Baby
Demonstration + ward round
Facilitator Script
“The ventilator does not care for the baby. You do. The machine delivers a breath; everything
that keeps that breath safe — the oxygen you wean, the pressure you watch, the tube you secure
— is nursing and medical work at the bedside. This session is that work.”
Oxygen — A Drug With a Deadline
Inspired oxygen harms the lung parenchyma after prolonged exposure through toxic oxygen radicals.
Reduce the FiO₂ to 0.5 (50%) or BELOW as soon as possible — ideally within the FIRST 24
HOURS. The primary determinants of oxygenation are FiO₂ and mean airway pressure.
“The first 24 hours has a target: FiO₂ at or below 0.5. If you are still on 0.8 at hour 20, that is not the
disease being stubborn — that is a baby who needs a plan, whether that is surfactant, recruitment, or
a mode change.”
The Pressures You Watch
• PIP reflects the pressure needed to overcome elastic recoil — the best estimate of peak alveolar
pressure, an important indicator of alveolar distension.
• Accurate peak pressure requires NO patient effort during inspiration or exhalation.
• High inspiratory pressure risks barotrauma, volutrauma, and reduced cardiac output.
Inspiratory PLATEAU pressure should ideally be < 30 cmH₂O. Elevated Pplat may be reduced by
decreasing PEEP (which may reduce oxygenation) or decreasing VT (which may reduce minute
ventilation). Permissive hypercapnia and a controlled fall in pH may be accepted when a high Pplat
must be reduced.
I:E, Auto-PEEP and the Trap of Stacking Breaths
• Total cycle time = 60 ÷ respiratory rate. Inspiration and expiration share it; normal spontaneous
I:E is ~1:2.
• In obstruction (asthma, some chronic lung disease) expiration is prolonged — I:E becomes 1:2.5
or 1:3.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
303 | Page
Reduce auto-PEEP by (in order of effectiveness):
● Decreasing the respiratory rate — the MOST effective way.
● Shortening the inspiratory time — extends expiration for fuller exhalation.
Decreasing VT.
FACILITATOR HINT
auto-PEEP is breath-stacking — the next breath arrives before the last one has fully left. The
instinct is to breathe FASTER to fix a rising CO₂; here that makes it worse. Slowing down is the
fix. This is counter-intuitive and worth a full minute.
Also Monitor
• Gas exchange, lung mechanics, work of breathing, neuromuscular capacity, metabolic status —
assessed FREQUENTLY to guide changes.
• Humidification of inspired gas.
• Remember: adjusting one parameter is likely to affect the others — take care with every change.
15.6 Ward Round — Run It Live
• 3–4 ventilated babies (or simulated stations). For each: state the mode, the settings, the last gas,
the FiO₂ trend, the Pplat, and the goal.
• Each pair decides: is this baby over-ventilated, under-ventilated, or on track — and what ONE
change (if any) they would make.
• Enforce the rule: change one thing, then reassess. Never turn three dials at once.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
304 | Page
15.7 Weaning and Extubation
Facilitator Script
“Weaning and extubation are two different decisions. Weaning is turning the support down.
Extubation is taking the tube out. A baby can be weaned to low settings and still not be ready to
have the tube out — and confusing the two is how babies get re-intubated.”
The manual is explicit: decisions about WEANING are separate and apart from decisions about
EXTUBATION. Some babies wean easily to low settings but are not yet ready to breathe without
the tube.
Weaning — The Factors That Say ‘Ready to Turn It Down’
• The circumstances that required ventilation must be RESOLVING.
• Level of alertness; need for sedation.
• Respiratory drive and adequate oxygenation — SpO₂ > 95% with FiO₂ < 40% and/or low PEEP
requirement (< 7 cmH₂O).
• Adequate ventilation — PaCO₂ < 45 (no chronic respiratory failure), or > 45 with acceptable
pH.
• Time since the most recent respiratory or haemodynamic decompensation.
Permissive hypercapnia helps weaning and reduces lung injury: target PaCO₂ 50–60 while keeping
pH over 7.20 (limited by pH 7.20–7.25). Preterms on permissive hypercapnia for 10+ days showed
improved respiratory drive, fewer days on the ventilator, and a trend to less BPD or death.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
305 | Page
15.8 Extubation steps
1. Have a written mechanical ventilation protocol — including weaning, extubation and re-
intubation criteria — agreed by neonatologists, nurses and respiratory therapists.
2. Use assist control with volume-targeted ventilation if a proximal flow sensor is available.
3. Allow permissive hypercapnia (PaCO₂ 50–60) and set SpO₂ targets (91–95%).
4. Give a LOADING DOSE OF CAFFEINE before extubation — ideally within 24 hours prior.
5. Place the infant on CPAP or NIPPV IMMEDIATELY after extubation (PEEP ≥ 5) using short
bi-nasal prongs or a nasal mask.
6. Stop enteral feeds for 4 hours before extubation.
7. Confirm acceptable weaning parameters: MAP ≤ 8 cmH₂O, FiO₂ < 30–40%, rate < 30/min with
an acceptable gas.
Step 5 is not optional. A preterm has insufficient drive and muscle strength to hold his lung open
after the tube comes out. The distending pressure goes on IMMEDIATELY — not after a wait-and-
see. Have the CPAP set up and running before you pull the tube.
Why Caffeine
• A large multi-centre RCT showed caffeine before extubation gave less BPD, shorter oxygen
duration, and intubation for almost a WEEK less.
• Early caffeine (before day 3) may further reduce time on the ventilator.
• Give within at least 24 hours of the projected extubation. (Cross-reference: caffeine prescribing
and stopping, Module 2.)
“Caffeine buys you a week off the ventilator. There are not many single drugs on this course you can
say that about. Load it before you extubate — every time.”
Post-Extubation, and When Weaning Fails
• After extubation, a distending pressure (CPAP or NIPPV) is necessary immediately to prevent
post-extubation atelectasis.
Common reasons for FAILED weaning:
• Cardiac dysfunction · cognitive dysfunction · respiratory pump failure · respiratory muscle and
diaphragmatic dysfunction · metabolic disorders
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
306 | Page
FACILITATOR HINT
if a baby keeps failing extubation, the reasons above are the differential. It is rarely just ‘the
lungs’ — check the heart, the metabolic state, the diaphragm, the sedation. A baby who fails
twice needs the list, not just a higher PEEP.

15.9 Complications and Troubleshooting
Facilitator Script
“A ventilated baby desaturates. The whole room wants to run to the machine and turn dials. The
manual says the opposite: take the baby OFF the machine and bag by hand. Because your hands
will tell you in three breaths what the ventilator cannot — whether the problem is the baby or the
box.”
The Core Algorithm — Sudden Desaturation With High CO₂ (3.11)
DISCONNECT the patient from the ventilator and BAG by hand. This single action is the fork in
the whole algorithm.
What happens when you bag What it means
The baby IMPROVES with
hand bagging
The problem is the VENTILATOR or the tube. Check: O₂
source, increase FiO₂, mechanical or electrical failure. Check
the tube for displacement, obstruction or leak
The baby does NOT improve The problem is the BABY. Assess for pneumothorax,
hypoglycaemia, hypotension, intraventricular haemorrhage,
and sepsis
This is the neonatal DOPE, in the manual's own words. If bagging helps, the fault is in the circuit or
tube (Displacement, Obstruction, Equipment). If it does not, the fault is in the baby (Pneumothorax
and the rest). Teach the fork first; everything else hangs off it.
The Other Three Troubleshooting Patterns
Pattern Likely cause Action
Gradual desaturation with
high CO₂
Inappropriate ventilator
settings
Increase PIP / PEEP / I:E ratio;
repeat blood gas
Gradual rise in PCO₂ (PO₂
unchanged)
Insufficient tidal volume or
rate; increased dead space
Increase PIP by 2–5; and/or
decrease PEEP by 1; and/or
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
308 | Page
Pattern Likely cause Action
(long ETT, non-ventilated
alveoli)
increase rate by 5–10; and/or
shorten a long ETT
Fall in PCO₂ (over-
ventilation)
Too much minute ventilation Decrease the rate; decrease PIP
and/or increase PEEP
“Notice the pattern in the timing. SUDDEN means something broke — disconnect and bag.
GRADUAL means the settings drifted from the lung — adjust and re-gas. The speed of the change
tells you where to look before you look anywhere.”
Complications of Mechanical Ventilation (3.10)
• Air leaks — pneumothorax, pulmonary interstitial emphysema (cross-reference: needle
thoracotomy and chest tube, Prematurity — RDS).
• Barotrauma and volutrauma from high pressure/volume.
• Reduced cardiac output, reduced venous return, raised pulmonary vascular resistance from high
mean airway pressure.
• Ventilator-associated pneumonia (cross-reference: sepsis prevention bundles, Module 2 Section
10).
• Bronchopulmonary dysplasia — the chronic price the whole chapter is trying to avoid.
• Oxygen toxicity — ROP and lung injury.
Every complication above is a reason the goal was ‘support respiration and plan for extubation as
soon as indicated.’ The safest ventilator is the one the baby comes off soonest. This session and the
weaning session are the same argument from two ends.
Scenario — Run the Algorithm Live
A ventilated 27-week baby, day 2, suddenly desaturates to 78% with a rising CO₂ on the monitor.
Give the group 60 seconds.
Expected first action: DISCONNECT and BAG by hand — not reach for the ventilator.
If bagging is easy and the baby pinks up: ventilator or tube. Check O₂ source, FiO₂, circuit, and
the tube for displacement/obstruction/leak.
FACILITATOR GUIDE Sudan Training Course on Management of Sick and Small Newborns — 2026
309 | Page
If bagging is HARD and the baby stays blue: the baby. Think pneumothorax first —
transilluminate, prepare to decompress — then hypoglycaemia, hypotension, IVH, sepsis.
The trap: someone will start increasing the ventilator FiO₂ and PIP while the baby is still
connected. Let the debrief land it — you cannot diagnose a machine problem while still trusting
the machine. Hands first.

Manikin
Full-term newborn life-size manikin with anatomical features.
Facilitates training of following skills
● Veins allow for injections, blood withdrawal and fluid infusion.
● Umbilical catheterization and infusion site.
● Airway management including suctioning and oral and nasal intubation.

vascular access and
